  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum, approximately £1,700 net per month. (2) Part-time employment earning approximately £890 net per month, but has ceased as of August 2025. (3) Yvette's employment income of £28,000 gross per annum. (4) Current regular expenditure approximately £2,493 per month (essential costs). (5) Discretionary spending approximately £800 per month on entertainment and leisure. (6) Around £5,000 annually on holidays. (7) In the Cashflow Analysis section (page 20), current expenditure is approximately £2,206 per month. (8) Total monthly expenditure derived from stated components: £2,493 + £800 + £417 (annualized holidays) = £3,710.17.
    File:     The Fact Find (P1) states: Total Net Monthly Income of £2,590.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £2,206.67. The Fact Find income table (P11) shows Anthony Barnes receives £890.00 net monthly (Basic Employment) ending 06/08/2025, and £1,700.00 net monthly (Private Pension Aon). A separate calculation (P9) shows Yvette's reduced income at 3 days per week would be £1,400.00 gross monthly or £1,301.30 net monthly. The Cashflow analysis (P2) shows 2025 money in of £38,800 annually for both clients combined. The report (P21) states current expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month plus discretionary spending £800 per month on entertainment and leisure, plus around £5,000 annually on holidays. The Fact Find (P1) Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure is £2,206.67.
    Discrepancies:
      - Expenditure inconsistency: report states £2,493 per month essential expenditure in narrative section but Cashflow Analysis section states £2,206 per month. Difference of £287 per month (13%) is material and unexplained. The Fact Find corroborates £2,206.67 monthly expenditure.
      - Yvette's current employment income: report states she earns £28,000 gross per annum but does not provide her net monthly equivalent in the income section, making it impossible to verify her contribution to household cash flow against the stated total monthly income of £2,590 from the Fact Find.
      - Total stated expenditure components: narrative identifies £2,493 (essential) + £800 (discretionary) + £417 (annualized holidays) = £3,710.17 per month when all components are included, but the Fact Find and Cashflow use £2,206.67 as the operative expenditure figure. The report does not explain which figure applies to the affordability analysis.
    The primary discrepancy is the dual treatment of expenditure. The report's narrative on page 4 states current regular expenditure is £2,493 per month plus discretionary elements (£800 + £417 annualized = £1,217), totalling £3,710.17. However, the Fact Find (P1) and the Cashflow Analysis (P2, page 20) both use £2,206.67 as the operative monthly expenditure figure. This £287 monthly difference (or £1,504 annually) is material to affordability analysis and cashflow modelling. The extraction notes in the canonical model acknowledge this inconsistency: 'Cashflow model expenditure input: £2,206/month' versus 'Current regular expenditure: £2,493/month,' noting the £287 difference is unexplained. The Fact Find shows Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure of £2,206.67 (P1), which reconciles to the Cashflow figure, suggesting the lower figure was used for detailed financial modelling. However, the report does not transparently explain why the consolidated narrative figure of £2,493 + discretionary spending differs from the £2,206.67 used in modelling. The income figures are internally consistent: £22,000 gross Aon pension matches [P21] and [P2]; £890 net employment (ceased) matches [P19] and [P11]; Yvette's £28,000 gross matches [P21]. Yvette's net monthly income is not stated in the report's income section but can be derived from [P9] as £1,301.30 net monthly at reduced hours. The report states total monthly household income will be £2,200 net per month in retirement (P18 and P22), which aligns with £1,700 (Anthony) + £1,301 (Yvette reduced) = £3,001, but the narrative does not clearly reconcile current household income to the Fact Find total of £2,590 net monthly. The Fact Find income table shows Anthony's total as £2,999.67 gross annually (P11) but the narrative does not separately state Yvette's net contribution. The guard on consolidated household expenditure applies: the report provides one consolidated household expenditure figure but uses different values in different sections. The comparison must be made on the totals that drive the affordability outcome, which are the Cashflow model figures (£2,206.67) corroborated by the Fact Find, not the narrative components.

  [G1.5] Assets and liabilities are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: Main residence at 27 Reigate Road, Urmston, Manchester valued at £400,000 (mortgage-free); Inheritance from late father's estate £100,000 (due this month, December 2025); Chartered savings account £40,000; Cash ISA (Anthony) £20,000; Cash ISA with Moneybox (Yvette) £20,000; Emergency fund £10,000; No outstanding liabilities (£0). Total assets £190,000 cash and savings plus £400,000 residence equals £590,000 in personal assets (excluding pensions). Assets & Liabilities table shows total £669,092 including Personal Pension (Aviva Platform) £79,092.
    File:     Fact Find [P1] records: Main residence House Mortgage-free valued at £400,000 (valuation date 06/08/2025); Incoming funds from sale of Dad's property £100,000 (valuation date 06/08/2025); Chartered Savings Account £40,000 (valuation date 06/08/2025); MoneyBox Cash ISA 4.5% £20,000 (valuation date 06/08/2025); Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000 (valuation date 06/08/2025); Cash Emergency Fund £10,000 (valuation date 06/08/2025); No liabilities stated. Cashflow model [P6] shows opening values: Surplus Account £0, Chartered Savings Account £40,000, Aviva ISA £20,000, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000, Aviva GIA £52,326, Cash Emergency Fund £10,000, Total savings/investments £142,326; Aviva Platform Personal Pension Plan £82,692; Total Liabilities £0. Cashflow [P9] as at 2025 shows Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions opening value £225,018.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states inheritance £100,000 with phrasing 'due this month' (December 2025) at valuation date 2025-12-16, but Fact Find [P18] values it as at 06/08/2025 (earlier date, approximately 4 months prior). Ambiguity exists whether funds have been received or are expected.
      - Report Assets & Liabilities table totals £669,092 but this appears to sum £400,000 (residence) + £190,000 (cash/savings) + £79,092 (pension) = £669,092. However, Fact Find [P18] lists only personal assets totalling £590,000 (residence £400,000 plus cash items £190,000), and does not include pension in the asset listing shown.
      - Cashflow opening value [P6] shows total savings/investments of £142,326 but this excludes the £100,000 inheritance and includes an Aviva GIA of £52,326 not separately listed in the Suitability Report's cash/savings breakdown.
      - Report narrative states 'Cash & Savings £190,000' in Assets & Liabilities summary [P12] but Cashflow [P6] opening model shows only £142,326 in savings/investments with pensions listed separately at £82,692. The £190,000 figure appears to include the £100,000 inheritance (£40,000 + £20,000 + £20,000 + £10,000 = £90,000 stated items plus £100,000 inheritance = £190,000).
      - Aviva Platform pension value reported as £79,092 in Assets & Liabilities table [P12] but Fact Find [P18] does not list pension values in its assets section, and Cashflow [P6] shows £82,692 for Aviva Platform - Personal Pension Plan (a difference of £3,600).
      - Cashflow [P9] 2025 opening figure of £225,018 for 'Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions' does not reconcile directly with the reported asset figures. This appears to be a combined total but the breakdown differs from the stated component values.
    The report's assertion side and the evidence can be compared. All material asset values in the report are corroborated by the Fact Find [P1, P18]. The valuation date of the inheritance (06/08/2025 in Fact Find vs. 2025-12-16 in report) represents a 4-month gap consistent with the ambiguity noted in the extraction: the Fact Find records the asset as valued on the date of the fact find interview (6 August 2025), while the report updates the valuation to the report date (16 December 2025), which is appropriate given the extraction's own note that it is 'due this month' (December 2025). This is a timing reconciliation, not a material discrepancy. The residence, inheritance, savings accounts, ISAs and emergency fund are all individually matched between [P18] and the report's narrative sections. However, significant internal inconsistencies exist within the report itself: (1) The Assets & Liabilities summary table [P12] states 'Cash & Savings £190,000' which is the sum of the six cash/savings items (£100,000 + £40,000 + £20,000 + £20,000 + £10,000 = £190,000), but the Cashflow model [P6] shows only £142,326 in savings/investments at opening, excluding the £100,000 inheritance from the current-year liquid assets. This suggests the inheritance was not yet received at the model build date. (2) The Aviva Platform pension shows £79,092 in the report's Assets & Liabilities table [P12] but the Cashflow [P6] shows £82,692 for the same arrangement—a £3,600 discrepancy within the report's own numbers. (3) The Cashflow opening position of £225,018 for 'Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions' combines both elements but does not equal the report's stated totals when pensions and cash are summed. The report's own extracted passages note these inconsistencies: the charge comparison calculation produces implausible fund values (£79,074 from one method, £82,691 from another), and the expenditure figure differs between the narrative (£2,493/month) and cashflow input (£2,206/month). These are internal contradictions within the report, not contradictions with the evidence. The external evidence (Fact Find, Cashflow tables) is consistent with one another and corroborates the individual asset values stated in the report. Liabilities are confirmed as nil by [P11, P18].

      - Policy start date in fact find [P1] is 09/03/2026, which is after the report date of 23/11/2025. This is a logical inconsistency — the policy cannot start in future if it holds current investments as at 23/11/2025.
    The core arrangement is consistent: provider, policy number, plan type, fund names, and allocations all match between the report and [P12] client report. The current value of £79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025 is directly corroborated by [P12] which shows identical holdings valued at that date totalling £79,092.32. The policy start date error in the fact find is an error in that document, not the report. All material facts about the existing arrangement—provider, policy number, plan type, current holdings, current value as at the report date, transfer value, and contribution status—are corroborated by the provider client report [P12] and supporting Aviva documentation [P3], [P10].   [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The extraction report identifies three internal inconsistencies within the suitability report: (1) Platform charge reduction stated inconsistently — existing platform charge 0.32%, recommended platform charge 0.21%, existing fund charge 0.22%, recommended fund charge 0.41%, with net charge change stated as +0.08%, which contradicts the platform charge reduction; (2) Risk profile mismatch — existing arrangements show mixed Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (60% at 35.02% and 80% at 64.95%) but risk assessment states 'risk level of 6' without noting mixed composition; (3) Expenditure figures stated differently — 'Your current situation' section states £2,493/month plus discretionary spending, but Cashflow Analysis section states £2,206/month, a difference of £287/month which is material and unexplained.
    File:     The case file contains only the suitability report itself. There are no independent supporting documents to contradict or corroborate these internal inconsistencies, as this check compares the report against itself. The extraction report has identified these contradictions within the document's own content.
    Discrepancies:
      - Charge arithmetic: Platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21% (a reduction of 0.11%), yet total charge is stated to increase by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), implying fund charges increase by 0.19% (from 0.22% to 0.41%), which is internally documented but the net effect contradicts the stated platform savings narrative.
      - Expenditure figure discrepancy: £2,493/month stated in 'Your current situation' section versus £2,206/month stated in 'Cashflow Analysis' section — a difference of £287/month (11.5% variance) with no explanation provided for the reduction.
      - Risk profile representation: Existing portfolio comprised of two different Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (60% and 80% equity) with different implicit risk levels, but described as single 'risk level of 6' without clarification of the blended or mixed nature.
    All three inconsistencies are internal to the suitability report and can be verified within the document itself. (1) Charge inconsistency: The extracted figures show platform charges decline from 0.32% to 0.21%, but fund charges rise from 0.22% to 0.41%. The arithmetic of these components is internally consistent (0.32% + 0.22% = 0.54%; 0.21% + 0.41% = 0.62%), but this contradicts any narrative suggesting the recommendation reduces charges overall. The 0.08% increase is correctly computed but represents a cost rise, not savings. (2) Risk profile: The report holds two funds with materially different risk profiles (LifeStrategy 60% and 80%), and treating them as a single 'risk level 6' lacks transparency about the blended composition. This is a presentation inconsistency rather than an arithmetic error, but it obscures the client's current position. (3) Expenditure: The £2,493/month figure appears to be stated as 'regular expenditure' in the narrative, while £2,206/month is used in the cashflow model. A difference of £287/month (approximately 11.5%) is material and unexplained — no notation indicates whether discretionary spending has been excluded, timing assumptions differ, or an error has occurred. The guard concerning 'as at' dates does not apply here; both figures refer to current situation with no date qualifier distinguishing them. The extraction report's confidence level of 0.87 indicates some ambiguity in the source document itself, but the numerical contradictions are evident.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts four income streams: (1) Defined benefit pension £22,000 gross annually, stated as approximately £1,700 net per month; (2) Part-time employment £890 net per month, now ceased (£0 current monthly equivalent); (3) Yvette's employment £28,000 gross per annum; (4) State Pension £11,973 gross per annum from age 67.     Discrepancies:
      - Report states defined benefit pension as £22,000 gross per annum; fact find records this as £1,833 gross per month, which equals £21,996 annually (reconciles within tolerance).
      - Report states Aon pension provides approximately £1,700 net per month; fact find confirms £1,700 net monthly from this source (reconciles).
      - Report states part-time employment earnings £890 net per month; fact find confirms £890 net monthly [P4] and shows total gross annual earnings £14,000, which at 20% tax rate approximates £890 net monthly (reconciles).
      - Report uses total monthly net income £1,700 currently; fact find [P1] shows total net monthly income £2,590.00 and total monthly disposable income £383.33. The £1,700 represents only the pension component, not the combined household income during employment period.
      - Report states regular expenditure £2,493 monthly in narrative section; fact find [P1] itemises essential expenditure but calculated total household expenditure is £2,206.67 monthly [P2]. Difference of £287 unexplained: report narrative uses £2,493 but cashflow uses £2,206.67.
      - Report states £800 discretionary spending monthly; fact find [P10] confirms Entertainment £800 monthly (reconciles).
      - Report states £5,000 annually for holidays; fact find [P10] confirms Holidays £5,000 annually (reconciles).
      - Report derives £416.67 monthly from annual figure; fact find shows £5,000 annually ÷ 12 = £416.67 monthly (reconciles as derived).
      - Cashflow [P13] shows annual income 'In' of £38,800 for years 2025-2029; this does not equal stated gross incomes when summed: pension £22,000 + employment £14,000 (gross) + Yvette £28,000 = £64,000 gross, not £38,800. Appears to be net or adjusted figure but basis not explicit in report narrative.

Report does not reconcile the separate versus household income figures.
      - State Pension figure in report stated as £11,973 per annum; fact find contains no matching BR19 projection or State Pension detail [P6]. Yvette's income reduction calculation [P12] shows £16,800 gross annually for reduced scenario, not £11,973 per person.
    The requirement asks whether values in different frequencies or formats reconcile across the report and supporting evidence. Most individual figures reconcile within tolerance when converted to common frequency and basis. However, three material discrepancies emerge: (1) The report's headline figure for current regular expenditure (£2,493 monthly) contradicts the fact find's itemised and calculated total (£2,206.67 monthly [P2]). This is a £287 or 13% variance, material to cashflow modelling. The report uses £2,493 in narrative but the cashflow analysis [P13] appears to use £2,206.67. This represents an internal inconsistency within the report's own data, not merely a frequency conversion issue. (2) The report's statement of total monthly net income as £1,700 is incomplete during the employment period. Fact find [P1] clearly shows Anthony's total £2,999.67 net monthly and household total £2,590.00 after tax and NI, yet the report presents only the pension component (£1,700) as if it were the current total. This omits current employment income (£890 net) and Yvette's employment income in the household analysis, creating ambiguity about the income basis for affordability. (3) The State Pension projection of £11,973 per annum has no supporting source in the fact find or other evidence provided [P6] shows State Pension fields are blank, and no BR19 projection is documented. The figure is asserted but unsupported in the evidence pack. The cashflow 'In' figures (£38,800 annually 2025-2029, £22,000 from 2030) do not derive clearly from stated gross incomes when summed, suggesting adjustments or netting that are not transparent in the report narrative. On frequency and basis conversions that are explicit: the gross-to-net pension calculations, the monthly-to-annual holiday spending, the annualisation of weekly amounts—these all reconcile within the 1% or £1/month tolerance. The guards for gross/net and frequency conversion do explain legitimate variation. However, they do not address the £287 expenditure discrepancy or the incomplete income statement for the household. The extraction metadata notes the £287 expenditure variance as an internal inconsistency already, and the employment income status as ambiguous.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states cashflow was modelled (wasCashflowModelled: true) with the following assumptions: General expenditure inflation 2%, Savings inflation 1.25%, State pension inflation 2.5%, Property value growth 4.7%, Lending growth 4%, Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5%, Mid-growth rate for critical yield analysis 2.94%, Surplus income treatment 0% saved (all spent), Liquid assets drawn before pension/ISA/GIA access, and Current expenditure modelled at £2,206 per month.     File:     The cashflow files [P6], [P8], [P10] confirm inflation assumptions: GENERAL 2%, PROPERTY 4.7%, STATE PENSION 2.5%, LENDING 4%, and Surplus Income Percentage Saved 0%. The model confirmation in [P10] states 'Unless specified otherwise, expenditure will reduce to 80% on the death of Anthony and 80% on the death of Yvette'. The savings inflation rate of 1.25% is not found in any passage. The mid-growth rate of 2.94% is corroborated in [P18].     Discrepancies:
      - Report narrative states current regular expenditure 'approximately £2,493 per month' but cashflow model uses £2,206 per month (£287 monthly difference, material for projection purposes)
      - Savings inflation stated as 1.25% in assumptions but not found in any cashflow file; nearest evidence is 1.5% context in [P12] but no corroboration of 1.25%
      - Report states Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5% but this specific figure does not appear in retrieved cashflow passages
      - The extraction metadata notes this £287 discrepancy as an internal inconsistency between '/financialPosition/0/monthlyExpenditure' and '/needsAnalysis/cashflowInputs'
    The report asserts cashflow was modelled and provides detailed assumptions. Most assumptions are corroborated by the cashflow files [P6], [P8], [P10]: general expenditure 2%, state pension 2.5%, property 4.7%, lending 4%, and surplus income 0% are all confirmed in the cascade of scenario documents. The mid-growth rate of 2.94% is confirmed in [P18]. However, two material discrepancies exist: (1) The savings inflation rate of 1.25% stated in the report's assumptions array cannot be matched to any passage in the evidence pack. The instruction 'do not estimate or assume it' applies here—this is an unsupported assertion. (2) More significantly, the report's own narrative section states 'your current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month' but the cashflow model documented in [P10] and used for projections inputs £2,206 per month. This is a £287 monthly difference (11.5% variance), which compounds materially over a multi-decade projection. This is not a presentation or rounding difference; it is a substantive factual mismatch that directly affects the cashflow projections supporting the recommendation. The Aviva Platform investments growth rate of 4.5% is asserted in the assumptions but not retrieved from the cashflow documents, though this may reflect it being a specific assumption not displayed in the standard cashflow headers. The discrepancy between £2,493 and £2,206 is the primary concern: the model used a lower expenditure figure than the narrative describes, which would make the projected cashflow appear more favourable than it would be if the £2,493 figure had been used. This is a material inconsistency between what the report says about the client's situation and what the model actually incorporated.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7). Report states objective 5 (pension contribution of £3,600 gross / £2,880 net in 2025/26 tax year) with no supporting evidence in the Fact Find, notes, or risk profile discussion recording this as a stated client objective. Report marks objective 6 (do not wish to move Chartered savings account) as client-stated with no evidence this was discussed or recorded in any document. Report asserts risk level 4 as 'agreed' objective 1, but [P18] shows no additional notes were recorded on agreed risk level, and [P10] discusses how risk level was agreed through discussion but provides no record of that discussion or the client's stated preference for risk level 4 specifically. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts six client objectives in priority order: (1) Ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level (4) to align with tolerance for volatility; (2) Invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA; (3) Provide flexibility and growth to support desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month; (4) Simplify administration by keeping pension, ISA and GIA on one Aviva Platform with ongoing professional oversight; (5) Take advantage of pension annual allowance with a contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in the 2025/26 tax year; (6) Do not wish to move Chartered savings account at this time. All six are marked isClientStated=true.
    File:     The Fact Find [P3] records: 'Review his Aviva pension and look to improve upon it. Client will complete ATRQ in his own time. Wants to be fully retired by 60. Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month. Also looking at investment advice on £60k of his Dad's property money coming in this month, and the £40k cash he currently has so as to avoid paying tax on interest. He has already used his full ISA allowance this year, so has his wife. Potentially interested in transferring his cash ISA to S&S ISA but would have to be really worth his while - not dead set on doing this.' Notes [P15] state: 'About to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom. Has another £40k in high interest savings, but only look at investing the £60k. Possibly move ISAs – not likely though. Only ATR 4. Expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISA's. Both used ISA allowance.' Notes [P16] state: 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?' The Cashflow analysis [P6] shows projections to age 70/74 and beyond. Risk profile discussion [P18] records no additional notes on agreed risk level or investment target. The Fact Find [P2] states: 'I/We the undersigned confirm my/our financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document.' However, the objectives marked as prioritised in the Fact Find appear to be the unranked items from [P3], not a numbered priority order.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states objective 5 (pension contribution of £3,600 gross / £2,880 net in 2025/26 tax year) with no supporting evidence in the Fact Find, notes, or risk profile discussion recording this as a stated client objective.
      - Report marks objective 6 (do not wish to move Chartered savings account) as client-stated with no evidence this was discussed or recorded in any document.
      - Report asserts risk level 4 as 'agreed' objective 1, but [P18] shows no additional notes were recorded on agreed risk level, and [P10] discusses how risk level was agreed through discussion but provides no record of that discussion or the client's stated preference for risk level 4 specifically.
      - Report lists six objectives with explicit priority ranks 1-6, but the Fact Find [P3] presents objectives as a narrative list without priority numbering. No document records explicit priority ordering by the client.
      - Report objective 3 states 'approximately £2,200 net per month' retirement income requirement. [P3] records 'Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month' — the language differs (desired vs minimum) and may indicate different priority or framing.
      - Report objective 2 regarding GIA and ISA transfer is supported as a general area of interest, but [P3] and [P15] record this as tentative ('potentially interested', 'would have to be really worth his while', 'not likely though'), not a firm objective.
    The Fact Find [P2] confirms the client affirmed that 'my/our financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document,' and [P3] is that document. However, examination reveals material gaps between what the report asserts and what the file records.
    
    Objective 1 (risk level 4) is supported by the report's own narrative that discusses aligning with Cautious Balanced profile, and the risk research report [P10] confirms an 'agreed risk level' was reached, but the actual notes from that discussion [P18] are blank ('No additional notes were recorded'). The substance is supported (risk level 4 is discussed and recommended), but the explicit client statement is not recorded.
    
    Objective 2 (GIA and ISA transfer) is evidenced in [P3] and [P15], but the recording emphasises tentativeness. [P3] states 'Potentially interested in transferring his cash ISA to S&S ISA but would have to be really worth his while - not dead set on doing this.' This is not the same as a firm objective; it is a conditional interest. The report lists it as priority 2 without qualification of this uncertainty.
    
    Objective 3 (£2,200 net monthly retirement income) is clearly evidenced in [P3] as 'Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month.' The terminology shift from 'minimum' to 'desired' is minor; the substance is supported.
    
    Objective 4 (simplify administration on one platform) is supported by general business practice and the recommendation narrative, but no explicit client statement of this objective appears in the file. The Fact Find records the adviser's analysis, not a stated client preference for platform consolidation.
    
    Objective 5 (pension contribution £3,600 gross / £2,880 net in 2025/26) is entirely absent from the file. [P15] contains notes 'Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working – none. Aviva appears to be only pension and no contributions made.' and [P16] questions 'Defo opting out?' These notes suggest uncertainty about contributions, not a client-stated objective to contribute £3,600. The figure appears in the recommendations section but not as a pre-existing client objective.
    
    Objective 6 (do not wish to move Chartered savings account) has no supporting evidence. No document mentions a 'Chartered savings account' or records the client declining to move it.
    
    The report marks all six as isClientStated=true, but the evidence supports only objectives 1, 2 and 3 as genuinely client-stated (though 2 is conditional). Objectives 4, 5 and 6 are either adviser-derived recommendations or completely unsupported.
    
    The extraction report notes an ambiguity regarding the client's employment income status (currently working or ceased?), which affects the validity of objective 5 (pension contribution). If the client is not currently employed or earning, a stated objective to make a pension contribution requires explicit confirmation; the file shows only uncertainty.
    
    No explicit priority ordering (rank 1-6) is recorded in any file document. The Fact Find presents goals narratively. The priority ranking in the report is the extraction's ordering, not a documented client-stated ranking.
    
    Guard consideration: Advisers paraphrase client objectives — minor wording differences are expected. The shift from 'minimum income' to 'desired retirement income' and the paraphrasing of inheritance/tax-efficiency language are acceptable. However, objectives entirely absent from the file (5 and 6) and objectives presented as firm when the file records them as tentative or undiscussed (4 and 5) represent a material gap, not a paraphrasing variation.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts six objectives with time horizons: four marked 'LongTerm' and one marked 'ShortTerm'. Life expectancy basis is stated as ONS life expectancy for 55-year-old male approximately 84 years and 59-year-old female 87 years, with funds projected to last well beyond these ages. The report does not state explicit retirement target dates or ages in the canonical objectives path. The pension switch analysis compares projections at age 60 (multiple comparisons across low, mid, and high growth scenarios, all showing 'Projected value at age 60'). The comparison summary states 'The retirement age used is 60.' Cashflow scenarios show Anthony with 'Retire' event age 67 in 2037 and Yvette with 'Retire' event age 67 in 2033.
    File:     Fact Find [P23] records Target Age as 60. Cashflow projections [P8, P10, P12, P14] consistently show Anthony (born 06/01/1970, currently 55) with retirement event 'age 67 in 2037' and state pension age 67. Yvette scenarios show retirement 'age 67 in 2033' for most scenarios [P18, P14] but one scenario [P12, P13] shows Yvette 'Retire' at age 67 in 2033 while another [P17] shows events across ages 55/59 through 104/108 with specific retirement events for both. The pension switch report [P6] states 'The retirement age used is 60' for the comparison. Client is currently 55 years old [P8, P10]. Life expectancy figures in cashflow [P8]: 'ONS life expectancy for 55-year-old male is approximately 84 years old, and for 59-year-old female is 87' matches report assertion but the female age reference is 59 not the current stated age.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts life expectancy for 59-year-old female as 87 years; Fact Find and client birth dates show Yvette as currently 59 (born 1966), but the life expectancy statement refers to 'a 59-year-old female' which matches current age. However, the report's canonical model states this as 'isClientSpecific: true' linked to OBJ3 with no explicit target date for that objective.
      - Pension switch analysis uses retirement age 60 [P6] for projections, but cashflow analysis shows retirement age 67 for both clients. This is a 7-year discrepancy in the term used to justify the investment recommendation.
      - Fact Find [P23] shows Target Age 60 with Time Horizon marked 'Medium Term', suggesting 60 is the planning horizon for a specific objective (likely pension-focused). Cashflow shows retirement at 67, suggesting two different time horizons are in play: medium-term planning to age 60 and longer-term planning to age 67.
      - The report's six objectives have time horizons recorded (four LongTerm, one ShortTerm, one not specified in extracted data), but none state explicit target dates or ages in the canonical objectives array. The term used to justify the recommendation (age 60 for pension switch) differs from the primary retirement date stated in cashflow (age 67).
    The comparison can be performed because retirement ages and time horizons are stated in multiple places in the case file. However, they are inconsistent, and the inconsistency is material. The pension switch recommendation is justified using age 60 as the retirement age [P6: 'The retirement age used is 60'], which appears in the Fact Find [P23] as 'Target Age 60'. This is a medium-term horizon. However, the primary retirement events in the cashflow analysis [P8, P10, P12, P14] show both clients retiring at age 67, which is 7 years later. This creates a material ambiguity: is the recommendation designed to deliver funds at age 60 or age 67? The report's assertion that funds are 'projected to last well beyond' life expectancy ages of 84 and 87 assumes the cashflow to age 104 (the stated 'Death' event age in scenarios). The life expectancy figure for the female (87) is stated correctly as at age 59 [P8], which matches her current age. The male age 84 is corroborated [P17 shows ages through 100+]. The guard notes that pension investment horizons may differ from income drawdown horizons—the pension may be locked until 67 (minimum pension age) while income objectives are met to age 60 from other sources. This explains the co-existence of the two ages but does not reconcile them: the report uses 60 as 'the retirement age' for the switching decision without distinguishing that this is a liquidity target, not the pension accessibility date. The canonical model shows four objectives as 'LongTerm' and one as 'ShortTerm', but does not link objectives to dates or scenarios. No objective in the canonical extraction is explicitly linked to age 60 or age 67. The expenditure discrepancy [P23 vs. cashflow £2,493 vs. £2,206 monthly, difference £287] suggests the planning model may be working from different assumptions than the stated position, though this does not directly affect the time horizon consistency required here. The report does not state which objective is 'ShortTerm' or what its term is. The absence of target dates in the canonical objectives means the recommendation cannot be linked to specific objectives by date—only by boilerplate language ('funds projected to last well beyond life expectancy'). This is a classic Potential Concern: the stated term (60 for pension switch) contradicts the stated goal horizon (67 for retirement), and the report asserts a recommendation justified by the 60-year term without clarifying that this is a sub-goal of the broader 67-year retirement plan.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts four access requirement categories: (1) Emergency access: client confirms sufficient liquid capital held immediately or on short notice for unexpected emergencies; (2) Planned access: circa £40,000 from inheritance for property renovations in near-term; (3) Regular access: pension fund access via Flexi-Access Drawdown from age 55; (4) Ad-hoc access: pension and ISA/GIA funds accessible within 10-15 working days, with client comfortable given significant liquid cash available. Emergency fund of £10,000 is stated and confirmed as sufficient.
    File:     Evidence from cashflow models (P1-P16) projects forward year-on-year asset, income and expenditure flows but does not explicitly state or confirm the emergency fund amount or the client's access needs in those terms. The Fact Find (P18) records total monthly household expenditure of £2,206.67 and emergency fund amount of £10,000 with no shortfall identified. The suitability report (P19) lists assets totalling £669,092 including £190,000 cash and savings. Cashflow analysis inputs (P21) state current expenditure of approximately £2,206 per month. No evidence passage explicitly addresses the emergency access requirement, the £40,000 renovation spend timing, the 10-15 working day settlement standard, or client comfort with ad-hoc access delays.

      - Report states inheritance of circa £40,000 intended for renovations in 'near-term'; no evidence passage dates the inheritance receipt or explicitly links the £40,000 figure to a specific timeframe or planned renovation schedule
      - Report states client is comfortable with 10-15 working day access for ad-hoc pension/ISA/GIA withdrawals because of significant liquid cash available; no evidence passage quantifies what 'significant' means or confirms the client's stated comfort level independently
      - Report describes four distinct access needs; evidence confirms only the emergency fund amount and generic expenditure levels—no independent corroboration of the planned renovation spend, the stated preference for Flexi-Access Drawdown, or the acceptability of settlement timings
    The comparison can be performed because the key figures and categories are stated in both the report and the supporting evidence. The Fact Find (P18) corroborates the £10,000 emergency fund figure exactly and confirms no shortfall. The Assets table in the report (P19) shows £190,000 in cash and savings, which supports the statement that liquid capital is held, though no passage independently verifies it is 'sufficient' for emergencies—the sufficiency is an assertion the report makes, not something the evidence corroborates. The inheritance and renovation spend (£40,000) are stated only in the report itself (P19 extract mentions 'consider transferring your Cash ISA' but the renovation figure appears in the canonical model from page 4 of the report). The cashflow models (P1-P16) do not itemise the £40,000 renovation as a separate event or withdrawal, so the link between stated need and modelled outcome cannot be verified from the evidence. The access settlement timings (10-15 working days) and the client's stated comfort with them are asserted in the report but not independently evidenced. The guard permits 'No access needs identified' as a valid evidenced answer; here access needs ARE identified by the report, but independence of that identification from the supporting case file is weak—the Fact Find and cashflow models establish only expenditure baseline and asset position, not explicit access requirement conversations or client preferences. No actual passage from category B or F independently states the client prefers Flexi-Access Drawdown, intends to spend £40,000 on renovations by a specific date, or is comfortable with 10-15 day settlement. These are asserted only in the report (category I), which cannot corroborate itself.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts five foreseeable life changes: (1) Client recently retired after 35 years in factory role; part-time work ceased, with loss of £890 net monthly income from 2025-08-01; (2) Inheritance of £100,000 from father's estate expected 2025-12-16; (3) Yvette plans to reduce work hours to 3 days per week from 2026, then fully retire when client turns 60 (2030-12-16); (4) State Pension eligibility for both clients projected at £11,973 per annum each from age 67 (client age 67 in 2035, Yvette age 67 in 2033); (5) Potential IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 including unused DC pensions in taxable estate, though clients' combined estate is below £1m threshold.
    File:     The case file evidence supports most stated life changes: Retirement confirmed in notes and cashflow scenarios showing 2025 as commencement year. Inheritance of £100,000 confirmed in notes as 'circa £100k' with £40k earmarked for kitchen/bathroom renovation. Yvette's employment reduction and retirement plan corroborated in cashflow files showing retirement event 'age 67 in 2033' and multiple scenario timeline references to Yvette retiring at age 67. State Pension amounts stated in cashflow files as £11,973 per annum from age 67. However, a material discrepancy exists: the suitability report states Yvette's State Pension eligibility at age 67 (which would be 2033), but the report also states the client will turn 60 in 2030 when Yvette 'plans to fully retire', implying Yvette's full retirement at age 64 (2030), not age 67 (2033). The cashflow files consistently show Yvette's retirement event as 'age 67 in 2033' across all scenarios, contradicting the report's assertion that she plans to 'fully retire when you turn 60' (2030).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Yvette 'plans to fully retire when you turn 60' (2030) but evidence shows retirement event scheduled for 'age 67 in 2033', a 3-year discrepancy
      - Report states combined State Pension of £23,946 (£11,973 each) but does not clearly reconcile Yvette's stated full retirement at client age 60 (2030) with State Pension commencement stated at age 67 (2033)
      - Notes indicate £40k of inheritance earmarked for kitchen/bathroom spending, reducing investable inheritance to £60k, but report does not explicitly quantify the net investable amount or spending intention
    The report's statement that Yvette 'plans to fully retire when you turn 60' (client age 60 = 2030-12-16) directly contradicts the cashflow evidence, which consistently schedules her retirement event as 'age 67 in 2033' across baseline and all stress test scenarios. This is not merely a timing ambiguity: it represents a 3-year gap in planned retirement. The evidence from multiple cashflow files (Yvette Predeceases 2040, Tony Predeceases 2040, Market Crash, Current Situation, Higher Inflation) all corroborate the 2033 retirement date. The notes mention only that Yvette will 'reduce her hours to 3 days per week from next year' (2026), but do not confirm full retirement at age 60. The State Pension figure of £11,973 per annum is correctly stated and corroborated by the cashflow files. The inheritance amount of £100,000 is confirmed but the notes reveal £40k is committed to home improvements, though the report does not explicitly address this constraint on investable capital—only noting that the client has retained sufficient liquid capital. The retirement of the client himself is evidenced and the £890 monthly loss from ceased part-time employment is supported by the notes. The IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 is correctly stated and is a legislative change affecting the general population, not a client-specific foreseeable change; per the guard, this is not treated as a client-specific life change requiring corroboration. The material inconsistency is Yvette's retirement timing: the report's statement is contradicted by the cashflow modelling evidence embedded in the same advisory package.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts six prioritised objectives: (1) ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level; (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA; (3) be fully retired by age 60; (4) pension contribution of £3,600 gross in 2025/26; (5) ensure adequate liquidity for emergencies; (6) estate planning and IHT efficiency. Target amounts stated: £60,000 for inheritance investment and £3,600 for pension contribution. The report identifies time horizons through cashflow analysis to age 100, with Yvette's retirement from full-time work anticipated, and foreseeable life changes including Anthony's retirement at age 67 in 2037 and both clients' eventual death. No explicit target dates are stated for objectives 1, 2, 5, or 6.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Policy research document [P18] lists four client objectives including review of existing Aviva Platform pension, investment of £100k inheritance tax-efficiently, full retirement by age 60, and completion of attitude to risk questionnaire.       - Target date for objective 1 (risk alignment) is absent from report but this is a standing objective requiring no date.

The apparent discrepancy between stated age 60 retirement and planned age 67 retirement is a material finding that suggests the objectives or plan have not been aligned or reconciled in the report.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three recommendations with linked objectives: (1) Aviva Pension Portfolio (SIPP) linked to OBJ1, OBJ4, OBJ5; (2) Aviva Investment Portfolio (GIA) linked to OBJ2, OBJ3, OBJ4; (3) Aviva ISA Portfolio linked to OBJ2, OBJ4. Stated objectives include: matching investments to Cautious Balanced risk level (OBJ1); investing inheritance tax-efficiently via GIA (OBJ2); transferring Cash ISA (OBJ3); building retirement fund via pension (OBJ4); planning for Yvette's retirement and potential shortfall period (OBJ5). Time horizons are stated as LongTerm for OBJ1-4 and OBJ6, and ShortTerm for OBJ5. Access needs include flexibility from age 55 via Flexi Access Drawdown, an emergency fund of £10,000, and liquid assets to support lifestyle. Foreseeable changes identified: Yvette retiring at age 67; temporary shortfall period between Yvette's retirement and State Pension onset; potential long-term care costs.
    File:     Evidence supports: client aged 55 (retired after 35 years), married to Yvette aged 59 (working, intending to reduce to 3 days/week); current expenditure £2,206/month in cashflow modeller (though £2,493 stated in narrative); defined benefit pension £22,000 gross p.a.; inheritance of £100,000 due December 2025; £190,000 in liquid assets; main residence valued £400,000 mortgage-free; emergency fund £10,000; Yvette to retire at age 67 (2033); State Pension age 67 for both; cashflow projections show accessible pension and liquid assets through retirement; minimum pension age 55; death benefits transferable to spouse; platform/fund charges 0.63% (per cashflow) or 0.54% (per existing arrangement tables). Pension projections to age 60 at £74,000-£93,400 (real terms). No evidence explicitly names OBJ1-OBJ6 or confirms which specific objectives each recommendation serves beyond the report's own assertion.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report links GIA recommendation to OBJ2 and OBJ3 (transferring Cash ISA and investing inheritance) but the evidence shows a £57,120 GIA value after initial fee, not a direct statement of inheritance deployment mechanism.
      - Report states access need is Flexi Access Drawdown from age 55, consistent with evidence (minimum pension age 55), but does not explicitly reconcile this against the stated Short-term time horizon for OBJ5 (Yvette's retirement planning), which runs to age 67—a 12-year gap between minimum access and the identified planning horizon.
      - Expenditure figure inconsistency: report narrative states 'current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month' but cashflow modeller uses £2,206 per month, a material £287 difference that affects needs assessment underlying objective appropriateness.
      - Report asserts capacity for loss is 'Medium-High' but also identifies a temporary shortfall period 'when Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins,' creating internal tension over whether capacity is stable or time-dependent, and whether OBJ5 (planning for this shortfall) is adequately addressed by the current asset allocation strategy.
      - Report links recommendation to OBJ5 (planning for Yvette's retirement and shortfall period) but the recommended Cautious Balanced (Risk Level 4) approach is justified primarily on current risk profile alignment (OBJ1), not on the cashflow resilience required to survive the identified 2033–2040 period when Yvette has retired but State Pension has not commenced.
    The requirement tests whether recommendations are appropriate for stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes. The report links each recommendation to specific objectives (OBJ1–OBJ5), but the link mechanism relies partly on boilerplate reasoning ('suitable for your circumstances') rather than demonstrating that each recommendation directly serves the named objective under the identified constraints.
    
    On access needs: The pension allows Flexi Access Drawdown from age 55, matching the client's preference for flexibility. The GIA and ISA provide immediate access to liquid assets. No access need is unaddressed in principle.
    
    On time horizon alignment: OBJ1–OBJ4 are stated as Long-Term, and OBJ5 (Yvette's retirement planning) is Short-Term (to age 67, approximately 8 years from the advice date of late 2025). The pension contribution and Cautious Balanced fund selection are justified as long-term retirement building, which aligns with OBJ4 but does not explicitly address the Short-Term horizon of OBJ5. The cashflow analysis does model the 2033 event and years beyond, but the recommendation does not articulate how the Risk Level 4 strategy specifically mitigates the 'shortfall for a small period of time' identified in the capacity assessment.
    
    On foreseeable changes: The report identifies Yvette's retirement at 67 and the temporary shortfall period until State Pension. The cashflow projects these events, but the investment recommendation (Cautious Balanced, Risk Level 4 across all three vehicles) is not articulated as a response to these specific changes. The report does not explain why this risk profile is appropriate should the temporary shortfall prove more severe than projected, or whether the strategy should adjust at any point during the 2033–2040 window.
    
    On expenditure basis: The £287/month discrepancy between stated (£2,493) and modelled (£2,206) expenditure means the underlying cashflow analysis and therefore the suitability of the recommended assets to meet OBJ5 may rest on an understated expense baseline. This undermines the evidence base for sufficiency during the shortfall period.
    
    On objective-recommendation link specificity: The linking of recommendations to objectives (via linkedObjectiveIds) is present in the extraction, but the supporting narrative does not always name the specific objective when justifying a recommendation. For example, the GIA recommendation is introduced as 'tax-efficient' and refers to 'access requirements' and 'financial goals' without naming OBJ2 and OBJ3 explicitly in the adjacent text. The pension recommendation text does not mention OBJ5 (Yvette's retirement shortfall) at all, only OBJ4 (general retirement building).
    
    The report does identify foreseeable changes and does model them in cashflow scenarios. The recommendations do address the main objectives in broad terms (long-term growth via pension, tax-efficient income via GIA and ISA, flexibility via drawdown). However, the specific appropriateness of the Cautious Balanced risk profile for surviving the identified Short-Term shortfall period, and the impact of the expenditure data discrepancy on needs validation, are not reconciled in the evidence presented.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Six objectives stated as client-stated: (1) Ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level (4) to align with tolerance for volatility; (2) Invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA; (3) Provide flexibility and growth to support desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month; (4) Simplify administration by keeping pension, ISA and GIA on one Aviva Platform with ongoing professional oversight; (5) Take advantage of pension annual allowance with a contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in the 2025/26 tax year; (6) Do not wish to move Chartered savings account at this time. Ten planning assumptions disclosed: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings inflation 1.25%, state pension inflation 2.5%, property value growth 4.7%, lending growth 4%, Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5%, mid-growth rate for critical yield analysis 2.94%, surplus income treatment 0% saved, liquid assets drawn before pension/ISA/GIA access, current expenditure modelled at £2,206 per month.
    File:     Fact Find dated 06/08/2025 records: retirement age target of 60 (P9); desired retirement income £2,200 net per month (P3, P9); looking at investment advice on £60k inheritance coming 'this month' and £40k cash to avoid paying tax on interest (P3, P7); considering transferring cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA but 'not dead set on doing this' (P3, P7); both client and spouse used full ISA allowance (P3, P7); emergency fund £10,000 (P5); current expenditure £2,206.67 per month (P5); no expected expenditure changes in foreseeable future (P5); current monthly disposable income £383.33 (P5); non-essential expenditure includes £5,000 annually for holidays and £800 monthly for entertainment (P10). Defo 60?' regarding part-time work cessation, 'Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working' with no P60/P45 provided. Pension illustrations provided for ages 67 (P16) and 75 (P17) but not age 60.
    Discrepancies:

      - Report objective (5) states pension contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in 2025/26 tax year; fact find notes (P7) indicate no P60/P45 provided and uncertainty about employment status and whether contributions are feasible; no pension contribution summary or payroll documentation in file
      - Report states retirement income objective of £2,200 net per month desired; fact find (P5) shows current expenditure is £2,206.67 monthly with no expected expenditure changes, creating internal inconsistency within the file (expenditure £2,206.67 but income target only £2,200)
      - Report objective (4) states simplification by keeping pension, ISA and GIA on one Aviva Platform; fact find (P3, P7) notes client is 'Possibly' moving ISAs and 'not dead set on doing this', indicating less commitment than report's assertion of this as a stated objective

      - Report mid-growth rate assumption of 2.94% stated as used 'up to age 60' (page 12); pension illustrations provided (P16, P17) show projections to ages 67 and 75, not age 60; no projection illustration for retirement age 60 in evidence despite this being the stated target age
    Objective 1 (risk level alignment): Stated in report as client objective; fact find records target risk level 4 and notes confirm this was discussed, but risk profile report (P11) records 'no additional notes were recorded' on how this was agreed. This is internal consistency within the report-file relationship but lacks richness of evidence of client authorship. Guard applies: the adviser-inferred and client-confirmed test is met by the fact find reference and meeting date 06/08/2025. Objective 2 (tax-efficient inheritance investment via GIA): Report states objective; fact find confirms client aware of inheritance (£60k to be invested) and interested in tax efficiency (avoiding tax on interest, P3, P7). However, notes show ambivalence ('Possibly move ISAs – not likely though') and no GIA documentation or tax advice in file. Objective 3 (desired retirement income £2,200 net per month): Stated consistently in fact find (P3, P9) and assumed in cashflow modelling (P5). The £2,206 figure in cashflow (P5) matches the assumption in the report, so this appears to be the working assumption, but the source of the £2,493 figure stated elsewhere is not explained in file. Objective 4 (simplification on one platform): Report presents this as a stated objective; fact find notes 'Possibly move ISAs – not likely though' (P7), indicating less commitment than the report's language suggests. Objective 5 (pension contribution £3,600 gross 2025/26): Report includes this as objective; fact find notes (P7) record uncertainty: 'Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working – none. Other assumptions are stated in report but not sourced to documents in evidence pack — they may be adviser's standard assumptions or drawn from suitability report itself (category I), which cannot corroborate. Critical yield 2.94% is stated in report as calculated 'up to age 60' (page 12 reference); pension illustrations provided are for ages 67 (P16) and 75 (P17), not the stated target age 60. This creates a temporal misalignment: report uses age 60 for critical yield calculation but provides illustrations only for later ages. No issue found on Objective 6. Risk profile record (P11) shows no recorded notes on how risk level or investment target were agreed, suggesting minimal documented agreement beyond the fact find itself. Extraction notes record ambiguities on employment status and inheritance timing that bear on whether the client is capable of the stated contributions and whether the inheritance receipt is confirmed. The vulnerability overlay: extraction notes do not flag any vulnerability drivers, support needs or reasonable adjustments, so overlay does not materially affect this assessment. However, the absence of recorded agreement notes on risk tolerance (P11) combined with uncertainty on employment status (P7, P8) suggests the advisory process may not have been as thorough as stated objectives imply.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.9, G3.11). The report asserts capacity for loss is 'High' but the suitability report itself [P11] states 'your capacity for loss is Medium-High', creating an internal inconsistency within the report's own assertions. The capacity for loss rating 'High' does not appear in the risk profile document [P13] or questionnaire responses [P1-P2], where it is sourced only from the suitability report itself. The assessment basis references 'a good level of guaranteed income' and 'a small period of time' shortfall when Yvette retires, but the cashflow analysis [P14] shows annual deficits of approximately £7,000 in 2030-2032 (3 years), which may exceed 'a small period'. 12 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an attitude to risk (ATR) assessed code of 4, agreed code of 4, with label 'Cautious Balanced' on a 1-10 scale. The report states: 'Your risk profile was agreed as 4 on a scale of 1 - 10' and describes the client as 'quite a cautious person' inclined to view risk negatively, with limited investment experience. The report characterises the profile as suitable for someone who may take time making decisions and be somewhat anxious about them, looking for safer investments but willing to consider some risk for increased returns.
    File:     The evidence corroborates the assessed and agreed ATR code of 4 on a 1-10 scale. Passage [P1] from the Risk profile report dated 11 August 2025 confirms 'Natural risk level: 4 Cautious Balanced'. Passages [P4], [P8], [P10], and [P11] all confirm the agreed risk level is 4 on a 1-10 scale with the label 'Cautious Balanced'. The Risk profile report [P1] and [P15] provide the full questionnaire responses and assessment documentation. The label and scale are consistently applied across all supporting documents [P1], [P4], [P5], [P8], [P9], [P10], [P11], [P21]. The description of the Cautious Balanced profile appears identically across multiple passages, confirming consistency of characterisation.
    The report's assertion that the assessed ATR code is 4 is directly corroborated by [P1], which documents the questionnaire completed on 09/08/2025 resulting in a natural risk level of 4 Cautious Balanced. The agreed code of 4 matches the assessed code with no override, and this is confirmed across multiple research reports and the suitability report itself [P4], [P8], [P10], [P11]. The scale (1-10) is explicitly stated and consistent throughout. The qualitative description of the Cautious Balanced profile—cautious person, views risk negatively, limited experience, takes time with decisions, somewhat anxious, seeks safer investments but willing to consider some risk for increased returns—appears verbatim in the supporting evidence [P1], [P4], [P9], [P15], [P21], demonstrating full consistency. No questionnaire override or amendment has occurred; the natural risk level and agreed risk level are identical. All supporting documentation uses consistent terminology, scale and label. The risk assessment documentation [P1] confirms the process whereby the natural risk level from the psychometric questionnaire was discussed with the client and agreed without modification. No discrepancy exists between what the report asserts and what the evidence documents.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts capacity for loss is 'High'. The assessment basis states: 'You have a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions, and this means that you will only have a shortfall for a small period of time, when Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins. You also have significant funds to cover this, providing you with greater resilience to investment volatility.'
    File:     The evidence pack contains: (1) Risk profile questionnaire [P2] with no additional notes on capacity for loss [P1]; (2) Cashflow analysis [P14] showing liquid assets of £225,018 in 2025, declining through 2030-2032 to £227,191 in 2033 with deficits of £7,101-£7,052 in years 2030-2032 when Yvette retires; (3) Stress test under market crash scenario [P15] maintaining positive liquid assets; (4) The risk profile report [P13] stating agreed risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced), with natural risk level also documented as 4; (5) Suitability report [P11] internally stating 'capacity for loss is Medium-High' on the same page as the assessment basis cited by the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts capacity for loss is 'High' but the suitability report itself [P11] states 'your capacity for loss is Medium-High', creating an internal inconsistency within the report's own assertions.
      - The capacity for loss rating 'High' does not appear in the risk profile document [P13] or questionnaire responses [P1-P2], where it is sourced only from the suitability report itself.
      - The assessment basis references 'a good level of guaranteed income' and 'a small period of time' shortfall when Yvette retires, but the cashflow analysis [P14] shows annual deficits of approximately £7,000 in 2030-2032 (3 years), which may exceed 'a small period'.
      - The assessment states 'significant funds to cover this' referring to resilience to volatility, but does not quantify what 'significant' means or reconcile it against the identified shortfall period and asset level.
    The canonical model extraction records the capacity for loss rating as 'High', citing the assessment basis from [P11]. However, examination of the actual suitability report [P11] reveals the capacity for loss is stated as 'Medium-High', not 'High'. This is a material discrepancy within the report's own text on the same page. The assessment basis is accurately quoted and does rely on two components: (1) guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions, and (2) significant liquid funds. The cashflow evidence [P14] corroborates that guaranteed income exists and that liquid assets are substantial (£225,018 in 2025). The identified shortfall period when Yvette retires occurs in 2030-2032 (years when she would be age 64-66, before her State Pension at 67), lasting approximately 3 years with annual shortfalls of £7,000-£7,100 to be met from liquid assets. This is consistent with 'a small period of time' and the substantial liquid asset base provides resilience. However, the extracted rating of 'High' contradicts the report's own statement of 'Medium-High'. The risk profile questionnaire [P2] and additional notes [P1] provide no independent assessment of capacity for loss—no notes were recorded. The assessment basis provided is qualitative and supported by financial position evidence but lacks quantitative thresholds. The rating itself—whether correctly stated as 'Medium-High' or extracted as 'High'—has not been independently evidenced from documents outside the suitability report; it is asserted by the report and not corroborated by separate assessment instruments. The agreed risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced) [P13], and the capacity for loss assessment feeds into but is distinct from this risk level determination.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report extracts six objectives with time horizons: five marked 'LongTerm' and one marked 'ShortTerm'. The report asserts in multiple places that risk levels were discussed and agreed 'considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal' with reference to 'desired time horizon' as a factor in the discussion. However, the extracted model contains no specific investment term in years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears is absent). The report does not quantify the time horizon in years for any of the six objectives, nor does it state explicitly how each term supports the agreed risk level of 4.
    File:     The evidence includes cashflow projections spanning from 2025 to 2073 (approximately 48 years from 2025), showing assets and cash flows through ages 93/97 to 103/107. The report references 'desired time horizon' as part of the discussion with the client but provides no specific term length statement. Passage P1 states the risk level discussion involved showing 'a projection, based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon' but does not name that horizon. Passage P4 shows a cashflow projection to age 103/107 (year 2073), implying a very long planning horizon. Passage P7 shows liquid assets and pension values through 2074 (ages 104/108). No document quantifies the investment term for any individual objective or explains which objectives map to which time horizons.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model lists six objectives with time horizons (five 'LongTerm', one 'ShortTerm') but the case file contains no evidence identifying which specific objectives these refer to, their amounts, or the rationale linking each horizon to the agreed risk level 4.
      - The report asserts time horizon was a factor in the risk discussion ('desired time horizon') but provides no quantified term—neither in years nor as a specific date—for any objective.
      - The extraction notes that /riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears is absent; the report is silent on investment term in years for any of the objectives.
      - Five objectives are marked 'LongTerm' in the canonical model, but the supporting recommendations (Investment ISA, GIA, Pension) each show a single agreed risk level of 4 without stating which objective each serves or how the time horizon supports the risk rating.
      - The cashflow analysis projects to age 103/107 (48+ year horizon) but no document links this long horizon to the short-term objective or to any specific investment recommendation.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Quantified investment term in years for each objective, Mapping of the six extracted objectives to the three recommendations (ISA, GIA, Pension), Explicit statement of which objective carries the 'ShortTerm' horizon and why, Connection between each horizon and the agreed risk level 4 in the discussion notes, Identification of the planning horizon end-date or retirement date that anchors the term
    The requirement asks whether the time horizon used to support the risk profile is consistent with and supported by evidence. The canonical model extracts six objectives with mixed time horizons, but the case file does not evidence the specific term length, target amount, or purpose of any of these six objectives. The report states in multiple passages (P1, P2, P9, P10, P11) that 'desired time horizon' was considered in the discussion and that projections were shown based on 'investment amount, target and desired time horizon', but these passages do not name the actual horizon (e.g., 10 years, 20 years, to retirement, to age 85). Passage P13 provides a critical discrepancy: it states 'The 10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' This shows a 10-year test but does not clarify whether this is the agreed term for any of the six objectives. The cashflow projections in P4 and P7 run to ages 103/107 (circa 2073), suggesting a very long horizon, but no document explicitly reconciles this with the objectives extracted or states why a long-term horizon justifies a cautious (risk level 4) rather than a higher or lower profile. The extraction report itself notes ambiguity: the asset timing 'due this month' (December 2025) is not clearly marked as received versus pending, and the employment status is ambiguous (though not directly relevant to time horizon). Critically, the comparison cannot be performed: the report asserts time horizons (five long-term, one short-term) but the evidence does not specify which objectives these are, what term each represents, or how each term supports the agreed risk profile. The absence of investment term in years for any objective, combined with the absence of mapping between objectives and recommendations, means the comparison of consistency between time horizon and risk profile cannot be made. This is a missing input problem, not an internal inconsistency that can be adjudicated from the pack alone.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends a target risk rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced). The recommended investment strategy is a Bespoke Portfolio consisting of 100% allocation to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II. The fund carries a risk rating of 4 and an ongoing charge figure of 0.41% pa. The report states that the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II 'provides more consistent performance aligned with your Risk Level 4 profile' and demonstrates 'lower volatility than your current mix of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6.' The report asserts that the current holdings 'carry higher risk than your agreed profile, which is why we recommend switching to the Aviva fund that matches your Cautious Balanced approach.'
    File:     The evidence corroborates that the agreed risk level is 4 across multiple research reports. [P14] states 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P8] confirms the same mapping. The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is identified as a risk-rated multi-asset investment of level 4, described in [P8] as 'A risk rated multi asset investment of level 4 targets a moderate level of capital appreciation by investing in a multi-asset portfolio.' The fund factsheet [P2] establishes the fund's risk rating scale ranges from 1 (Lowest Volatility) to 7 (Highest Volatility), but does not state the fund's specific risk rating in the pages provided. [P17] provides detailed asset allocation showing Equities 41.7%, Bonds 33.8%, Alternative Assets 5.4%, Cash 1.3%, Gold 2.0%, and Other 9.1%, totalling approximately 100%. The ongoing charge figure of 0.41% is corroborated in [P7] which shows 'AMC 0.41%' for the recommended product, and in [P8] 'Weighted AMC 0.41%'. The current holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity at 35.02% and 80% Equity at 64.95%) are detailed in [P10]. [P15] confirms that 'The 10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.'

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an agreed risk level of 4 on a 1-10 scale for Mr Barnes. The report describes his Cautious Balanced profile and states his capacity for loss is Medium-High. The recommended investment strategy targets risk level 4. The report presents the existing pension holdings as having Risk Level 6, comprising Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%) and 80% Equity (64.95%) funds.
    File:     Evidence from multiple sources confirms the agreed risk level of 4 on a 1-10 scale and describes the Cautious Balanced profile consistently across P1, P2, P6, P12, P15. The Pension Switching research report [P15] states: 'The client's agreed risk level is 4.' The Personal Pension Plan review report [P2] states: 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The 10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' The recommended strategy in the Personal Pension Plan research report [P3] confirms: 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The capacity for loss in the Suitability Report [P11] is stated as Medium-High, justified by 'a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' and 'significant funds to cover' a temporary shortfall period. The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C [P13] is rated 4 on the Risk and Reward Indicator.
    Discrepancies:
      - The existing pension arrangement's risk level (6) materially exceeds the agreed risk level (4). Report [P2] explicitly flags this: 'This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' This is a retained arrangement that does not align with the agreed profile.
      - The report states existing funds are at risk level 6 but does not explicitly explain or justify why a client with agreed risk level 4 continues to hold investments at risk level 6 prior to the recommendation being implemented.
    The comparison can be performed on a single consistent 1-10 scale provided in the evidence. The agreed risk level of 4 is corroborated across multiple independent passages (P1, P2, P6, P12, P15), all using the same 1-10 scale and Cautious Balanced descriptor. The capacity for loss is assessed as Medium-High and is supported by cashflow analysis and pension income projections detailed in P7, P11, and P19. The recommended strategy achieves alignment: P3 confirms the new portfolio risk mapping is 4, matching the agreed level. However, a material concern exists with the existing arrangements. P2 explicitly documents that the current holdings' volatility of 12.34 'maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' The existing Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (60% Equity at 35.02% and 80% Equity at 64.95%) are documented in the report at P23 as Risk Level 6. The report does not explain why these retained holdings—which predate the recommendation and remain part of the client's portfolio during transition—are permitted to exceed the agreed risk level of 4. The guard states: 'A retained plan invested outside the agreed profile is a concern wherever the report claims alignment for it—silence about a retained plan's risk level is a gap; a claim of alignment that the plan's own rating contradicts is a finding.' Here the evidence shows both the existing arrangement's actual risk level (6) and the report's silence on reconciling this to the agreed level (4). The new recommended strategy (Aviva Platform with CT Universal MAP Cautious at risk level 4) does meet the agreed profile. The capacity for loss assessment (Medium-High) is supported by the client's DB pension income (£22,000 gross p.a. from Aon), spouse's employment income (£28,000 gross p.a.), projected state pensions from age 67, and the cashflow model showing no projected shortfall throughout lifetime (P7, P11, P19). The Suitability Report [P11] documents that Medium-High capacity is appropriate because the client 'will only have a shortfall for a small period of time, when Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins. You also have significant funds to cover this, providing you with greater resilience to investment volatility.' The recommended strategy at risk level 4 does not exceed either attitude to risk (4) or capacity for loss (Medium-High). The concern is structural: the report does not address why the existing holdings at risk level 6 are being retained or when they will be brought into alignment, creating a potential gap in the transition management and ongoing monitoring of the existing portfolio.
