CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G4.2, G4.3, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report asserts 'fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs' but provides no corroborating evidence in the file for this characterisation of understanding level or degree. Report states client has 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes' but also asserts client 'has invested in...stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself' — this is internally contradictory: the report claims simultaneous absence of understanding and evidence of self-directed investment in the same product class. Risk Profile Questionnaire [P1] records client's answer to 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?' as 'No understanding / knowledge', which contradicts the report's narrative characterisation of stocks and shares experience. 3 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states that the client 'has a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs'; has 'invested in current accounts, savings, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself'; 'has also invested in pension planning through a financial adviser and check the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis'; but has 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes'.
    File:     The Risk Profile Questionnaire [P1] records the client's responses as follows: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work...?' — 'No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work...?' — 'No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works...?' — 'Very little understanding / knowledge'; 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' — 'Yes, by myself'; 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' — 'Yes, by myself'; 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' — 'Yes, through a financial adviser'; 'Do you check the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis?' — 'Yes, by myself'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs' but provides no corroborating evidence in the file for this characterisation of understanding level or degree.
      - Report states client has 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes' but also asserts client 'has invested in...stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself' — this is internally contradictory: the report claims simultaneous absence of understanding and evidence of self-directed investment in the same product class.
      - Risk Profile Questionnaire [P1] records client's answer to 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?' as 'No understanding / knowledge', which contradicts the report's narrative characterisation of stocks and shares experience.
      - Risk Profile Questionnaire [P1] records client's understanding of 'pension planning and income in retirement' as 'Very little understanding / knowledge', whereas the report's statement that client 'has also invested in pension planning through a financial adviser' does not capture the limited understanding recorded in the questionnaire.
      - Report states understanding of Cash ISAs without citation to questionnaire responses, whereas the questionnaire does not explicitly ask about Cash ISAs separately; the questionnaire asks about 'Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments' as a grouped category, which the client answered 'Yes, by myself'.
    The suitability report contains material contradictions between its own narrative statements and the documented evidence in the Risk Profile Questionnaire. The most significant discrepancy is that the report claims the client has 'no understanding of...stocks and shares' while simultaneously asserting he 'has invested in...stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself'. This is logically irreconcilable: either the client has invested in stocks and shares (evidencing some practical experience or capability), or he has no understanding of them. The questionnaire evidence [P1] confirms the client self-reported 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work, yet the client's own responses indicate he has invested in shares within the last 5 years, by himself. This suggests the report's distinction between 'understanding' and 'experience' is being applied inconsistently. The report's characterisation of a 'fair degree of understanding' of cash products is not supported by citation to the questionnaire or fact find; the questionnaire groups these products and records only a 'Yes, by myself' response without grading the understanding level. The report's treatment of pension experience similarly conflates passive holding via an adviser with understanding: the questionnaire explicitly records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning, a material qualifier the report does not adequately surface. The report does correctly distinguish between holdings and understanding in some contexts (e.g., Enterprise Investment Schemes held = false, understood = false), but this consistency breaks down for stocks and shares. The evidence in the file (questionnaire completed 09/08/2025 [P1], Fact Find completed 06/08/2025 [P14]) does not indicate that the client answered differently or that the report was correcting misunderstandings; the report appears to have recharacterised the questionnaire data inconsistently. No vulnerability factors recorded in [P4] (accessible format requirement: No Requirement) materially affect this assessment.
      · Risk profile report 11 08 2025 08 58.md [D] (P1)

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that three products are recommended: (1) Aviva Pension Portfolio (SIPP) - Personal Pension Plan, classified as NonComplex with no complexity drivers; (2) Aviva Investment Portfolio (GIA) - General Investment Account, classified as NonComplex; (3) Aviva Platform ISA Portfolio - Stocks & Shares ISA, classified as NonComplex.     File:     The evidence shows: (1) The Pension Switching research report [P16], Personal Pension Plan research report [P13], and GIA research report [P12] all confirm risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) is the agreed risk level for the client. These features suggest complexity considerations that are not addressed in the report's assertion of non-complexity.

    The requirement cannot be fully assessed because critical evidence is absent from the pack.   [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states the client 'describe[s] yourself as having very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and 'have a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs, but no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes.' The risk assessment records: 'you tend to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity. You have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand, but you are suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing.' The report recommends a Custodian Investment Portfolio (CIP) comprising multiple funds across different asset classes, regions and sectors, with active oversight by an investment committee.
    File:     The fact find dated 06/08/2025 records the client was sent an ATR questionnaire by email to complete himself on Defaqto, as 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today.' Meeting notes indicate ATR 4 was agreed. Notes state 'Only ATR 4. Expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISA's.' There is no record of client questions, comprehension testing, or discussions about the complexity of the proposed multi-fund CIP strategy. The capacity for loss questionnaire records 'No additional notes were recorded' and the agreed risk level section also records 'No additional notes were recorded.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts very limited investment knowledge and no understanding of stocks and shares, unit trusts or enterprise investment schemes; no evidence of client questions or comprehension about a multi-asset CIP comprising diverse funds
      - Report recommends complex multi-fund strategy with active committee oversight; no recorded client discussion about this complexity or how it differs from the single Vanguard LifeStrategy funds currently held
      - Report states 'fair degree of understanding' of cash/savings/ISAs but 'no understanding' of unit trusts and stocks; the CIP contains precisely these products (unit trusts as underlying holdings), yet no evidence of explanation or comprehension check
      - Client ATR questionnaire not completed in person but sent by email to self-complete; no subsequent record confirming completion, understanding or questions raised
      - Risk assessment notes state 'No additional notes were recorded' for both capacity for loss and agreed risk level sections, indicating minimal documented discussion
    The requirement tests whether a client with limited knowledge can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension. The guard specifies that absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand—it is an evidence gap weighted by product complexity. Here, the product complexity is significant: the CIP is explicitly described as 'a carefully curated selection of funds across different asset classes, regions, and sectors' with 'active oversight and governance' by an investment committee. The client is recorded as having 'very little understanding' of investing, 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares,' and does not find financial matters 'particularly easy to understand.' The recommended product comprises unit trusts across multiple asset classes. No questions or comprehension evidence appear in the file. The ATR was sent for self-completion by email with no subsequent record of client review, questions, or understanding. The capacity for loss and risk level agreement sections contain no recorded notes. The report does not evidence that the client was given detailed explanation of the CIP structure, the distinction between this and their existing single multi-asset funds, or confirmation of their ability to evaluate it. The discrepancy is stark: a client recorded as having no understanding of the product types now being recommended, advised toward a complex multi-fund strategy, with no contemporaneous record of comprehension questions, explanation or agreement on the fit. The charge section acknowledges the complexity requires 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' yet the knowledge and experience section does not evidence that the client understood what they were getting or agreed it was appropriate for them. This is not internal inconsistency in the report itself—the report internally states the client's limited knowledge—but a gap between what the report claims (suitability) and what the file evidences (no comprehension check for a complex product type the client explicitly does not understand).

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client has held: (1) Cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs with 'a fair degree of understanding' [P9, page 9]; (2) Stocks and shares, with investments made by the client himself within the last 5 years [P9, page 9]; (3) Pensions, invested through a financial adviser with annual portfolio review [P9, page 9]; (4) No understanding of unit trusts, enterprise investment schemes [P9, page 9]. Current holdings in the Aviva Personal Pension Plan (AV2075926-001) are: Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%), Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (64.95%), and Cash (0.03%) [extracted from page 5 of suitability report].
    File:     The Fact Find [P2, P4] confirms the client has a Personal Pension Plan with Aviva Platform (AV2075926-001) valued at £88,590.88 as of 23/07/2026. The Client Report [P3] shows current portfolio value of £79,092.32 as of 23/11/2025, with cumulative performance of 33.91% over 3 years and 41.83% over 5 years. Transaction history [P9, P10, P11] documents holdings of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity with transactions dating back to at least 2018. No personal pension arrangements other than the Aviva plan are recorded. No evidence in the file documents holdings of cash accounts, savings accounts, Cash ISAs, or stocks and shares outside the pension arrangement. The Fact Find contains no explicit statement of the client's knowledge or understanding of any product type.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client has invested in 'stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself' but the only documented holdings of equities are within the Aviva pension plan (Vanguard LifeStrategy funds), not separate self-directed equity holdings
      - The report asserts prior pension experience 'through a financial adviser,' but transaction history shows the Aviva plan existed since at least 2018 and was managed by an adviser; the file does not establish whether the client had pension arrangements prior to this or actively participated in investment decisions
      - The report claims the client 'check[s] the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis' but no evidence in the file supports this claim of active portfolio review behaviour
      - No corroborating evidence in the file documents the client's stated 'fair degree of understanding' of cash accounts, savings accounts, or Cash ISAs; no holdings of these products appear in any arrangement listing
      - The Fact Find does not record any existing holdings of unit trusts, stocks and shares ISAs, or other investment products outside the pension arrangement, yet the report asserts experience with 'stocks and shares investments'
    The comparison can be performed. The core pension holding is corroborated: the report correctly identifies an Aviva Personal Pension Plan with holdings in Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity funds, which matches transaction history and the Client Report. However, material discrepancies arise when examined against the stated experience narrative. First, the report asserts the client has made personal stocks and shares investments within the last 5 years, but the only equity holdings documented in the file are within the tax-wrapped pension arrangement, which are not self-directed investments in the ordinary sense — they are subject to pension rules and were selected within a structured advisory framework. Under the guard that 'holdings corroborate experience; they are not proof of knowledge,' the Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (which are diversified multi-asset funds managed by the fund house) do not directly evidence that the client has made independent investment decisions in individual stocks and shares. Second, the report states the client 'check[s] the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis,' implying active engagement, but no record in the file (no emails, no interaction logs, no adviser notes) supports this behavioural claim. Third, the claimed 'fair degree of understanding' of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs is not tested or evidenced anywhere in the file — no fact-find question asks the client to rate knowledge of these products, and no holding of such products is recorded. The Fact Find records only the pension arrangement and makes no mention of other savings or investment holdings. The transaction history shows pension fund management only. The file therefore does not corroborate the breadth of product knowledge the report asserts. The client's actual documented experience is narrower: ownership of a pension wrapper with multi-asset funds selected by an adviser, with a documented 3-year performance track record. The report's framing of this as evidence of independent investment decision-making in stocks and shares, and as a basis for claiming understanding of multiple product types, is not supported by the supporting evidence.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts three recommended products with complexity classification of 'NonComplex': (1) a Pension Portfolio via Aviva Platform, (2) an ISA Portfolio via Aviva Platform, and (3) a General Investment Account (GIA) via Aviva Platform. The report provides multiple rationales across pages 8 and 12, including: platform quality and comprehensive investment choices; risk alignment (moving from risk level 6 to level 4); cost reduction (platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%); professional oversight; tax efficiency; and access to Capital Gains Tax allowances through GIA structure and Bed & ISA facility. The client's knowledge level is stated but no detailed knowledge assessment, evidence of comprehension, or complexity drivers justifying the non-complex classification are extracted.
    File:     The evidence pack contains: (1) Fact Find completed 06/08/2025 showing client is retired after 35 years, briefly worked part-time but stopped, and is described as 'only ATR 4' with 'expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISA's' (P10). (2) Risk profile report noting no additional notes were recorded on agreed risk level or investment target discussion (P14, P18). (3) Product research showing Aviva ISA Portfolio is administered through Aviva Platform, requires financial adviser for changes, five-year investment horizon expected, and includes Bed & ISA facility (P5). (4) Pension switching research showing the new solution has 0.7% charges vs existing 0.6%, projecting £86,700 vs £86,800 (P9). (5) Investment ISA research showing 1 product from 168 total matched filters, with Aviva as largest UK insurance group (P15). (6) Meeting notes show client did not complete ATRQ at meeting, sent email version to complete himself (P16). (7) No meeting record, comprehension notes, or detailed knowledge discussion documented in the case file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% but Pension Switching research report shows new solution at 0.7% total charges vs existing 0.6%, an increase not a reduction (P9)
      - Report describes products as 'NonComplex' but provides no extraction of complexity drivers or formal complexity assessment to support this classification
      - Report provides rationales but case file contains no documented evidence of client comprehension, questions asked, or discussion of product features during the meeting; ATRQ was not completed at meeting but sent by email for client to complete alone (P16)
      - Risk profile report states 'No additional notes were recorded' on agreed risk level (P14), yet report asserts risk alignment as primary rationale
      - Knowledge statement references 'ATR 4' in adviser notes (P10) but report does not extract or detail client's actual knowledge level, investment experience, or understanding assessment
    The requirement asks whether the report's assertion of non-complex products with supporting rationales is adequate and supported by evidence of the client's knowledge, experience, and understanding. Three issues emerge: (1) Complexity classification: The report classifies all three recommended products as 'NonComplex', but the extraction notes this property was not captured. Non-complex products do not require rationale under the guard 'Absence of a rationale where the recommended product is simple is not a finding.' However, the report then provides extensive rationales (risk alignment, cost reduction, tax efficiency, professional oversight), which creates internal tension—either the products warrant the rationale (suggesting complexity), or the products are truly simple and the rationale is surplus. The GIA with Bed & ISA facility and CGT allowance planning mechanics could be characterized as having unusual features requiring comprehension. (2) Knowledge and experience evidence: The case file shows the client completed a telephone-recorded fact find on 06/08/2025 (P20), but did not complete the ATRQ at that meeting; it was sent by email for self-completion (P16). The adviser notes describe ATR 4, current ISA holdings, and expectation of 4.5% growth (P10-P11), but the risk profile report explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded' on how the risk level was agreed (P14) and 'No additional notes were recorded' on capacity for loss discussion (P18). This means the suitability report's assertion of rationales—particularly those premised on risk alignment, tax planning comprehension, and professional oversight—lack corroboration from meeting discussion records. (3) Rationale content vs. charge reality: The report asserts 'By moving the pension under TFAS agency, you will benefit from a reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' but Pension Switching research report (P9) shows the new solution total charge at 0.7% vs existing 0.6%, an increase of 0.1%. This discrepancy undermines a key rationale. The report's rationales are largely generic (platform quality, professional oversight) or aspirational (tax planning through careful use of CGT allowance) rather than client-specific evidence of suitability. The case file does not evidence that complex mechanics of Bed & ISA or GIA tax features were explained to and understood by a client recorded as ATR 4 with no formal knowledge assessment. The absence of comprehension notes, client questions, or explained features in the meeting record, combined with the email ATRQ that was not completed in real time, means the rationale is asserted but the underlying client knowledge is not demonstrated in the file.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report states ongoing commitment £1,558 annually; figure derived from 0.62% charge on stated platform value £155,812 does not reconcile to any asset value shown in evidence. Nearest corroborated figure is £82,691.94 implied by Aviva pension illustration charge table [P23], which would imply ongoing charge of ~£512.69, not £1,558. Report states initial commitment based on 'total investment values' of £79,092 + £57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £159,812; however charges documented separately apply to different platforms (Aviva Pension SIPP, Aviva GIA, ISA, Pension contribution) with distinct fee structures, suggesting the £4,794 is adviser initial fee, not an investment commitment to be afforded from income. Expenditure inconsistency: report asserts 'current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month' in one section but Cashflow Analysis uses £2,206/month — £287/month difference, material to surplus calculation. Extraction notes flag this as internal inconsistency. 17 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial commitment: £4,794 based on total investment values (£79,092 pension transfer + £57,120 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 pension contribution) as at 2025-12-16. Ongoing commitment: £1,558 annually, based on total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction. Affordability asserted as 'an amount you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds without affecting your current lifestyle.' Broader affordability grounded in 'good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' and 'significant funds to cover' a temporary shortfall 'when Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins.' Current surplus income is stated to provide 'a solid foundation for your retirement planning.'
    File:     Cashflow model (Current Situation, [P1]) shows total money in 2025 at £38,800 (DB Pension £22,000 + Yvette Employment £16,800); total money out £29,915; in-year surplus/deficit £0. Liquid assets beginning 2025: £225,018. From 2030 onward (when Yvette retires), model shows income drops to £22,000 and in-year deficits appear: 2030 deficit (£7,101), 2031 (£7,052), 2032 (£7,002), then recovery to £0 from 2033 when 'State Pension begins' (ages 63/67). Fact Find values: DB Pension £22,000 p/a, employment income stated as £890/month net but then 'stopped working altogether' (ambiguity on current status per extraction note). Regular expenditure stated as £2,493/month in 'Your current situation' section but £2,206/month in Cashflow Analysis — a £287/month discrepancy. Inheritance 'due this month' (December 2025) funding the initial £4,794 commitment. Advisor charges: 1% ongoing on investment portfolio, estimated at £47.60/month [P19] on first-year portfolio value. Additional product charges (platform 0.21% on Pension, fund 0.41%) and GIA platform 0.5% + 1% adviser charge, noted separately.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states ongoing commitment £1,558 annually; figure derived from 0.62% charge on stated platform value £155,812 does not reconcile to any asset value shown in evidence. Nearest corroborated figure is £82,691.94 implied by Aviva pension illustration charge table [P23], which would imply ongoing charge of ~£512.69, not £1,558.
      - Report states initial commitment based on 'total investment values' of £79,092 + £57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £159,812; however charges documented separately apply to different platforms (Aviva Pension SIPP, Aviva GIA, ISA, Pension contribution) with distinct fee structures, suggesting the £4,794 is adviser initial fee, not an investment commitment to be afforded from income.
      - Expenditure inconsistency: report asserts 'current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month' in one section but Cashflow Analysis uses £2,206/month — £287/month difference, material to surplus calculation. Extraction notes flag this as internal inconsistency.
      - Report asserts affordability rests partly on 'good level of guaranteed income'; evidence shows temporary deficits 2030–2032 (£7,000–£7,100 annually) when 'Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins,' contradicting implicit claim of uninterrupted surplus income.
      - Report states ongoing commitment 'based on current values and expected contributions, this would be equal to approximately £1,558 for the upcoming year'; evidence shows first-year illustration figures on pension £82,600 total payments [P23] and GIA £57,100 [P21], implying combined platform values far below £155,812 stated. Charge reconciliation does not hold.
      - Inheritance receipt timing ambiguous in extraction: stated as 'due this month' (December 2025) but whether received or merely expected is unclear. This affects whether the initial £4,794 is actually affordable at the stated date.
    The initial commitment of £4,794 is an adviser fee, not an investment capital amount, funded from inheritance 'due this month' (December 2025). The report states this is 'an amount you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds without affecting your current lifestyle' [P2, P12]. Inheritance receipt date is ambiguous per extraction (expected vs. received), but if funds arrive as stated, the initial fee is covered. However, the affordability claim rests on inheritance funding, not surplus income, which is a material distinction not highlighted in the report.
    
    The ongoing commitment of £1,558 annually cannot be reconciled to evidence. The report derives this from a 'total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction' and states it is 'based on current values and expected contributions.' However, the evidenced asset values—Pension £79,092, GIA £57,100, ISAs £20,000 each—do not sum to £155,812. The pension illustration [P23] shows total payments £82,600 and values around £91,500, and GIA illustration [P21] shows £57,100 payments. A combined platform value after fees would be lower than stated. The charge figure of £1,558 (implying 0.62% on £251,613 or 1% on £155,800) does not match either evidenced value. This is a data quality issue: the ongoing commitment assertion lacks evidential support.
    
    Affordability of ongoing commitment: Cashflow model [P1] shows income £38,800 (2025–2029) and expenditure £29,915–£30,488, yielding consistent nil surplus. Report cites 'good level of guaranteed income' and 'solid foundation' from 'current surplus income.' However, the model shows £0 surplus annually for years 2025–2029; surpluses do not appear until 2030+ when income falls to £22,000 and deficits emerge (−£7,000+). The model explicitly flags a 'shortfall for a small period of time, when Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins' [extraction ambiguity note]. Reported affordability of ongoing charges from surplus income is not supported by the cashflow evidence, which shows no surplus to draw from.
    
    Expenditures are inconsistently stated: £2,493/month vs. £2,206/month (£287/month gap). If the lower figure was used in cashflow analysis, the model may understate required withdrawals; if the higher, surplus estimates are further eroded. The report does not clarify which was used in the affordability assessment.
    
    Guard: Fees taken from platform funds reduce investment, not income. The adviser charges are deducted from contributions and investment value, not drawn directly from monthly income. The Guard applies: ongoing platform charges (0.21% pension, 0.5% GIA) and adviser charge (1%) reduce the fund value but do not directly consume the client's monthly income surplus. However, if the client must fund these via drawdown during the 2030–2032 deficit period, they consume the available liquid assets. The cashflow model [P1] shows liquid assets declining from £227,770 (2026) to £231,288 (2032) despite annual deficits, implying either investment growth or drawdown from reserves. The model does not show whether platform charges have been deducted from this projection.
    
    Guard: One-off transfer of existing pension is not a new affordability commitment. The £79,092 pension transfer and £57,120 GIA and £20,000 ISA are reallocation of existing assets, not new contributions from income. The £3,600 gross pension contribution (£2,880 net) is a new annual commitment. The report justifies this as 'you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds' but also states 'we are unsure of your earnings this tax year' and notes client 'stopped working altogether' [P12]. A £3,600 gross annual contribution requires either income (absent or ambiguous) or drawdown from liquid reserves (which face deficits 2030–2032). This is not assessed against monthly surplus in the report.
    
    Conclusion: The initial £4,794 fee is supported by inheritance funding (with timing ambiguity). The ongoing commitment of £1,558 annually is unsupported by evidence—the figure does not reconcile to documented asset values or charge percentages. Affordability of ongoing commitments from surplus income is contradicted by the cashflow model, which shows nil to negative surplus from 2030 onward. The expenditure discrepancy (£287/month) introduces data quality doubt. The £3,600 annual pension contribution is unjustified given ambiguous employment income and future deficits. Emergency funds and liquid assets are documented but their depletion during deficit years (2030–2032) is not explicitly addressed in the affordability narrative.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts total net monthly income of £1,700 (from defined benefit pension; part-time employment ceased), total monthly expenditure of £3,710.17 (sum of £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £417 annualized holidays), and a monthly surplus that is not explicitly stated. The report states total assets of £669,092 and zero liabilities, yielding net worth of £669,092. The cashflow analysis shows no shortfall periods across baseline and stress scenarios (high inflation, combined market crash and inflation, survivor scenarios). Liquid assets are modelled at £225,018 initially. The model assumes £2,206 per month current expenditure in the cashflow analysis. Scenarios show funds projected to last beyond age 100 and beyond ONS life expectancy (84 for male age 55, 87 for female age 59). An emergency fund of £10,000 is stated as required and available.
    File:     Fact Find [P1] records total net monthly income of £2,590.00 (Anthony's DB pension £22,000 annual, Yvette's employment £16,800 annual), total monthly expenditure of £2,206.67, and monthly disposable income of £383.33. Emergency fund amount stated as £10,000 required and £10,000 available (shortfall £0.00). Cashflow Current Situation [P7, P16, P20] shows opening liquid assets and accessible pensions of £225,018 in 2025, with projections extending to 2074 (age 104/108) showing no red bars (no shortfalls requiring depletion of all assets). Cashflow models across all scenarios [P7, P6, P12, P14, P17] show consistent pattern of no in-year surpluses/deficits after the 2030-2032 period when Yvette retires. Higher Inflation scenario [P21] shows lower asset values but still positive through lifetime. Income statements in cashflows [P12, P14, P20] consistently show Anthony DB Pension £22,000 and Yvette Employment £16,800, totalling £38,800 annual (£3,233 monthly), not £1,700.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total monthly net income of £1,700; Fact Find [P1] and all cashflow scenarios evidence combined net income of £2,590 (Anthony) or £3,233 annualized when Yvette employed
      - Report asserts total monthly expenditure of £3,710.17 (sum of £2,493 + £800 + £417); this figure does not appear in any evidence; Fact Find [P1] shows £2,206.67 and report's own cashflow assumption states £2,206 per month
      - Report derives monthly expenditure as £3,710.17 but cashflow models consistently use £2,206 per month - a discrepancy of £1,504.17 monthly or 68.2%
      - Report states total assets of £669,092; no evidence passage corroborates this figure; Fact Find and cashflow opening position [P1, P7] show liquid assets of £225,018 plus other assets not summed to £669,092 in evidence
      - Report's own extraction notes acknowledge "exact Yvette income in net monthly terms is not provided" yet derives total income of £1,700; Fact Find [P1] clearly shows combined income £2,590 and breakdown of Anthony £22,000 annual DB pension and Yvette £16,800 annual employment
      - Extraction metadata notes 'discrepancy' between expenditure stated as £2,493 in 'Your current situation' section versus £2,206 in Cashflow Analysis - a difference of £287/month unexplained in the report itself
      - Report's cashflow conclusion of 'no shortfall' rests on £2,206 modelled expenditure but is presented as supporting total expenditure of £3,710.17 - the model inputs do not support the stated expenditure position
    The core affordability assertion cannot be reconciled between what the report states and what the evidence shows. The report asserts monthly net income of £1,700 but the Fact Find [P1] and all cashflow scenarios unambiguously evidence combined monthly income of £2,590 (£38,800 ÷ 12 = £3,233 or more precisely as modelled £2,590 per [P1]). The extraction metadata itself flagged ambiguity about Yvette's employment income status (whether ongoing or ceased), but the Fact Find [P1] records it as current earning of £16,800 annual and all cashflow documents [P7, P12, P14, P20] model this consistently. The report's assertion of £1,700 appears to represent only Anthony's net income and excludes Yvette's £16,800 annual employment entirely. This is material: it understates household income by £890/month (52.3%). On expenditure, the report derives a total of £3,710.17 by adding stated components (£2,493 + £800 + £417) but no evidence passage records a single-month expenditure of £3,710.17. The extraction itself notes this inconsistency: the report states regular expenditure of £2,493 in one section but the Cashflow Analysis uses £2,206. The Fact Find [P1] records calculated total household expenditure of £2,206.67. All cashflow models, including the high inflation stress [P6, P21], use £2,206 monthly. The discrepancy between stated total of £3,710.17 and modelled £2,206 is unexplained. This is not a rounding or inflation assumption difference: it is a 68% mismatch in the same year. The 'Your current expenditure is approximately £2,206 per month' quote in the report's own needs analysis [P1 Cashflow section] directly contradicts the stated sum of £3,710.17. The report's conclusions about affordability and 'no shortfall' rest on the £2,206 model, making them internally consistent but not with the stated £3,710.17 total. On assets, the report claims total assets of £669,092 but provides no supporting evidence passage. The initial liquid assets shown in cashflow models are £225,018 [P7]. The assets and liabilities section of the report that reportedly shows 'TOTAL £669,092' is not reproduced in the passages provided, so the basis for this figure cannot be verified against the file. On monthly surplus, the report does not state an explicit monthly surplus or deficit figure. The Fact Find [P1] records monthly disposable income of £383.33 (income £2,590 minus expenditure £2,206.67). The cashflow models show the surplus is spent in full (0% savings rate per the modelled assumption) so no accumulation occurs. The emergency fund position is supported: Fact Find [P1] shows £10,000 required and £10,000 available, with no shortfall. However, this is quantified evidence that contradicts the affordability narrative resting on incorrect income and expenditure figures. The vulnerability overlay is not materially engaged by this requirement on the facts available: no specific support needs or communication requirements are documented that change the nature of the cashflow mismatch itself, though the ambiguity in the extraction about Yvette's employment status (whether temporary or permanent cessation) is noteworthy and relates to a key income assumption.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts an emergency fund of £10,000 held as cash. It states: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital, either immediately or on short notice, to meet any unexpected emergencies. This provides a reassuring level of financial security as you move forward with your planning.' The report identifies four liquidity needs: (1) Emergency access required immediately, funded from emergency fund and liquid assets; (2) Property renovations of £40,000 in near-term, funded from inheritance; (3) Pension access via Flexi-Access Drawdown from age 55; (4) Ad hoc pension/ISA/GIA access within 10-15 working days. The report confirms the client is comfortable with the 10-15 day access timeline as they have 'significant liquid cash available should you require instant access to funds.' The emergency fund sufficiency is confirmed as adequate without quantification of required amount.
    File:     Fact Find [P11] corroborates £10,000 emergency fund with required amount also stated as £10,000, showing no shortfall. Cashflow scenarios [P3], [P6] list emergency fund as £10,000 with 1.25% return, liquidated 8th in priority order. Fact Find [P11] shows total net monthly income of £2,590.00, total monthly expenditure of £2,206.67, and disposable income of £383.33. Cashflow Current Situation [P3] details liquid assets: Surplus Account £0, Chartered Savings Account £40,000, Aviva ISA £20,000, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000, Aviva GIA £52,326, and Emergency Fund £10,000. Total liquid assets before pension approximately £142,326. Multiple cashflow scenarios [P1], [P4], [P5], [P7] project liquid assets ranging from £129,625 to £376,005 over the planning horizon. Access timelines for ISA and GIA withdrawals noted in Key Features documents [P8], [P18] indicate funds are held on Aviva platform with standard withdrawal procedures. Pension access via drawdown is documented in [P9], [P10], [P24] with standard 10-15 working day settlement noted in report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states emergency fund sufficiency is 'confirmed' by client but does not quantify the required amount needed, only that held amount is £10,000
      - Report references access to funds within '10-15 working days' for pension and non-pension investments; Aviva Key Features [P8], [P10] indicate this is standard platform settlement but do not explicitly confirm the client's stated comfort with this timeline for their specific circumstances
      - Report states capital for 'ad hoc' access is 'significant' but does not quantify what amount constitutes 'significant' in the context of foreseeable needs
      - Report does not explicitly reconcile the property renovation £40,000 need against the available Chartered Savings Account £40,000, though the amounts match precisely
    The emergency fund of £10,000 is directly corroborated by the Fact Find [P11], which shows the same amount held and required with zero shortfall. This represents consistency on the quantified element. Liquid assets available total approximately £142,326 before pensions according to [P3], substantially exceeding the emergency fund, which supports the report's characterisation of 'significant liquid capital.' The £40,000 property renovation need is precisely matched by the Chartered Savings Account balance in [P3], suggesting this funding is earmarked and identified. Monthly surplus income of £383.33 [P11] provides ongoing capacity to rebuild the emergency fund if drawn. The access timeline of 10-15 working days for non-pension holdings is confirmed as standard platform practice in [P8] and [P10], and the report states the client is 'comfortable' with this. However, the report's assertion of emergency fund sufficiency lacks a quantified basis — it does not state whether £10,000 represents 3 months, 6 months or any other metric of expenditure, despite expenditure being documented at £2,206.67 monthly [P11]. At current spend, £10,000 covers approximately 4.5 months of expenditure, which is reasonable, but the report does not make this calculation explicit. The extraction's ambiguity note regarding inheritance receipt timing ('due this month' vs received) affects confidence in the near-term funding of the £40,000 renovation, though the funds are identified. The report's statements are internally consistent with the evidence where quantified, but the sufficiency assessment lacks transparent quantification of the required amount and the basis on which sufficiency was determined. This is a guard condition: an asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be fully verified.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts affordability of the recommendation based on: (1) an initial commitment of £4,794 stated as 'an amount you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds without affecting your current lifestyle'; (2) ongoing commitment of £1,558 annually; and (3) affordability grounded in 'good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' with 'significant funds to cover' a temporary shortfall when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins. The report states current surplus income is £383.33 monthly (derived from £2,590 net income less £2,206.67 expenditure per the cashflow model). The report also states 'your current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month' in the 'Your current situation' section.
    File:     The Fact Find evidences: (1) Total Net Monthly Income of £2,590.00 comprising employed income £890/month and private pension £1,700/month; (2) Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure of £2,206.67; (3) Total Monthly Disposable Income of £383.33; (4) Emergency Fund of £10,000 with no shortfall; (5) Initial investment into GIA of £57,100 and Pension of £82,600 (illustrated), with £20,000 ISA and £3,600 pension contribution stated in report; (6) Inheritance of £60,000 'coming in this month' (December 2025); (7) Current cash holdings of £40,000. The cashflow illustrations (Market Crash, Tony Predeceases, Yvette Predeceases scenarios) show liquid assets remaining positive throughout the projection period, with accessible pension funds available from age 60. No scenario shows capital depletion or inability to meet commitments.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 'current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month' in narrative but cashflow model uses £2,206.67, a difference of £286.33/month unexplained
      - Report asserts ongoing annual charge of £1,558 based on 'total platform value of £155,812' but £1,558 at 0.62% implies £251,290, not £155,812, and does not match stated platforms
      - Ongoing commitment calculation lacks clarity: £1,558 as ongoing annual advisor charge (1.00% × platform value) is stated but no ongoing platform or fund charges are separately quantified for affordability assessment
      - Initial commitment of £3,600 for pension contribution is stated but Fact Find shows no regular monthly pension contribution commitment; single pension payment of £82,600 is illustrated
      - Report silent on whether the employed income of £890/month will continue; Fact Find records employment ended ('stopped working altogether') but report does not explicitly confirm permanent cessation or update the surplus calculation
      - Emergency fund treatment in affordability assessment is not addressed: while £10,000 is evidenced, no analysis of whether this remains intact post-recommendation or would be deployed
    The core affordability test is whether committed outflows can be sustained from evidenced inflows. The Fact Find establishes monthly surplus of £383.33 (£2,590 income minus £2,206.67 expenditure). The report claims affordability based on (a) inheritance funds for initial outlay, and (b) 'good guaranteed income' for ongoing commitments. Three points require scrutiny: First, the discrepancy between stated expenditure (£2,493) and modelled expenditure (£2,206.67). The extraction notes flag this as an internal inconsistency. The report narrative states £2,493 but the cashflow analysis that must support affordability uses £2,206.67. This is material to the surplus calculation. However, the Fact Find explicitly records 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure' of £2,206.67 and 'Total Monthly Disposable Income' of £383.33, establishing the cashflow model as the authoritative figure for ongoing planning. The discrepancy is noted but the lower figure (£2,206.67) is the one the Fact Find confirms and that the cashflow illustrations use. Second, ongoing commitment clarity. The report quotes £1,558 as the 'upcoming year' charge on 'total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction'. At 0.62% (the stated ongoing rate), £155,812 × 0.62% = £965.03 annually, not £1,558. At 1.00% (advisor charge alone), £155,812 × 1.00% = £1,558.12. The £1,558 figure appears to represent advisor charges only, not total ongoing charges. The monthly impact stated as '£47.60 taken each month' from the GIA (per P9) plus an unstated ongoing adviser charge on the pension and ISA are not separately quantified in the affordability statement. This is a weak justification: ongoing costs are asserted as 'approximately £1,558' but the composition and total platform ongoing charges (platform + advisor + fund charges across all three wrappers) are not fully itemised against the £383.33 monthly surplus. Third, the initial commitment is supported by inheritance: £60,000 'due this month' per Fact Find, enabling the £4,794 initial adviser charge to be paid without affecting discretionary income. This is corroborated. Fourth, the guardpoint on employment income cessation. The Fact Find notes employment at £890/month 'ended' (stopped altogether), but the £2,590 income used in the cashflow model appears to include this figure. The extraction notes flag ambiguity: 'Currently earning £0 (unemployed)' vs 'Recently ceased earning but may resume'. If this employment income has permanently ceased and is no longer being earned, the true current surplus is £2,590 - £890 = £1,700 (pension only) minus £2,206.67 = a £506.67 monthly deficit, not surplus. However, the Fact Find itself states 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future? No', suggesting current income is stable. The report does not explicitly confirm whether the £890 is current income or historical. This ambiguity prevents a full determination of whether ongoing commitments are actually affordable from current income alone. Fifth, the cashflow scenarios model the long-term position and show Liquid Assets remaining positive through 2057 even under stress scenarios (Market Crash, death scenarios). These support resilience but do not directly test whether the £1,558 annual ongoing charge is comfortably affordable from monthly surplus of £383.33. The ratio is £1,558 ÷ 12 = £129.83/month, well within the £383.33 surplus if the surplus is genuine. If the £890 employment income has ceased and is not recoverable, the position is unsustainable. Sixth, the vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes flag no recorded vulnerabilities affecting this assessment. The report does not flag any support needs, communication requirements or reasonable adjustments that would materially affect affordability. No vulnerability concern identified for this check. Seventh, the emergency fund: the Fact Find records an Emergency Fund of £10,000 with no shortfall. The report does not explicitly state whether the recommendation will consume or preserve this buffer. The cashflow illustrations show this is preserved (Liquid Assets include the emergency reserve), supporting resilience. Overall: The initial commitment of £4,794 is supported by inheritance funds and does not consume ongoing surplus. The ongoing commitment is stated as approximately £1,558 annually (£129.83/month), which fits within the evidenced surplus of £383.33/month IF (a) the £383.33 surplus is current (not dependent on ceased employment income), and (b) ongoing charges are limited to the £1,558 stated. The material risk is the unresolved employment income ambiguity. The report asserts affordability but does not explicitly confirm that the £890 employment income has permanently ceased and should be excluded, nor does it recalculate surplus on a pension-only basis (£1,700/month). The Fact Find's statement 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future? No' appears to treat current income as stable, but this does not clarify whether the £890 is still being earned. A weak justification results: affordability is asserted with reasonable grounds (inheritance for initial outlay, surplus for ongoing, long-term resilience evidenced) but is undermined by (1) the unresolved expenditure discrepancy in the narrative (£2,493 vs £2,206.67), (2) incomplete itemisation of ongoing charges, and (3) ambiguity over whether the employed income component of the surplus is current or historical.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that affordability can be funded from 'Emergency fund and liquid assets', 'Inheritance funds', 'Pension funds', and 'Pension and ISA/GIA funds'. The canonical model lists these as fundedFrom sources but does not specify whether an emergency fund shortfall exists, whether it is depleted, or whether it remains available when required.
    File:     The case file evidences a £10,000 Cash Emergency Fund across multiple cashflow scenarios (P1, P2, P3). The liquidation order in all scenarios places this emergency fund as 'LIQUIDATED 8TH', after Surplus Account (1st), Chartered Savings Account (2nd), Aviva ISA (5th), Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA (6th), and Aviva GIA (7th). In the Current Situation scenario (P2), the emergency fund is not liquidated in any year shown. In the Market Crash scenario (P3), year-by-year cashflow tables (P12) show 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' consistently at £0 from 2071-2074, indicating no shortfall requiring emergency fund drawdown. In the Higher Inflation scenario (P11), cashflow tables show consistent £0 in-year surpluses/deficits across 2034-2044, with no liquidation of the emergency fund. The Yvette Predeceases and Tony Predeceases scenarios similarly show the emergency fund ranked 8th in liquidation order but not actually drawn. Across all scenarios examined, the emergency fund of £10,000 remains intact and untouched.
    The report asserts that affordability relies upon emergency fund and liquid assets as funding sources. The evidence shows a £10,000 emergency fund is identified and maintained across all modelled scenarios. The critical guard applies here: 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it, whatever the report says about retaining liquid capital. Read the liquidation order in the scenario before accepting an assertion of sufficiency.' The liquidation order places the emergency fund 8th, behind other liquid assets (Surplus Account, Chartered Savings Account, Aviva ISA, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA, Aviva GIA). Across all scenarios reviewed—Current Situation (P2), Market Crash (P12), Higher Inflation (P11), Tony Predeceases (P15, P6), and Yvette Predeceases (P1, P7, P13)—the cashflow tables show in-year surplus/deficit consistently at £0, indicating that no shortfalls occur that would require drawing the emergency fund. The emergency fund is therefore available and not depleted. The report does not explicitly quantify or discuss the emergency fund adequacy, but the evidence demonstrates it is maintained as a reserve and not consumed by the recommendation. No material contradiction exists between the report's assertion that liquid assets and emergency funds support affordability and the evidence showing the emergency fund remains intact and untouched across all scenarios.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts liquidity and access needs of: Immediate, Near-term, Age 55, and As needed. The recommended wrappers are SIPP, GIA, and ISA. The report does not explicitly state a retirement age, planned withdrawal schedule, or matching of specific access needs to specific wrappers.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Client is age 55 as of 2025 [P14]. (2) Pension minimum access age is 55, already reached. (3) SIPPs permit flexible drawdown from age 55 onwards [P1], including Self-Select phased income drawdown and flexi-access drawdown options. (4) GIA permits withdrawals at any time [P20]. (5) ISA permits withdrawals at any time [P10]. (6) Cashflow projections show deficits in 2030-2032 (ages 60-62) requiring withdrawals [P19], and the report notes 'you will only have a shortfall for a small period of time, when Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins' [extraction ambiguity notes]. (7) Planned retirement is age 67 in 2037 [P14]. (8) State Pension age is 67 [P14]. (9) The report mentions 'Age 55' as an access need but does not clarify whether this is a current need or a future benchmark.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report lists 'Age 55' as a liquidity/access need but the client is already 55 years old at the time of recommendation; it is unclear whether this represents a current immediate need or a reference point that has already been reached.
      - The report does not explicitly specify which access needs map to which wrappers, leaving the appropriateness of each wrapper's liquidity profile to inference.
      - The report does not state a planned withdrawal date or schedule for each wrapper, making it impossible to verify that access restrictions (if any) align with stated needs.
      - The report contains an ambiguity about employment income cessation that affects cashflow certainty, yet proceeds to affirm financial resilience without clarifying the permanence of nil employment income going forward.
    The client is currently age 55 with a minimum pension age of 55 already reached. The three recommended wrappers — SIPP, GIA, and ISA — all permit immediate or flexible withdrawals. The evidence supports that a SIPP permits drawdown from age 55 [P1], and both GIA and ISA permit withdrawals at any time [P20, P10]. Cashflow projections evidence a shortfall period in 2030–2032 (ages 60–62) requiring withdrawals [P19], and planned retirement is age 67 [P14]. All three wrappers appear capable of meeting these access timings without restriction. However, the report's assertion of liquidity needs includes 'Age 55', which is ambiguous: the client is already 55, so this either refers to a current need or is a reference point already in the past. The report does not explicitly state whether any withdrawal is needed at age 55 or whether all three wrappers are being recommended to address different time horizons. The extraction notes flagged that `/solution/recommendedPlans[]/selectedRetirementAge` and `/solution/recommendedPlans[]/plannedWithdrawals[]` are absent from the report, meaning the report does not articulate a planned withdrawal schedule. Without explicit planned withdrawal dates mapped to each wrapper, the match between access needs and wrapper flexibility cannot be fully verified, though the evidence does not contradict it — all wrappers offer sufficient flexibility for the identified time horizons (immediate, near-term, age 67+). The cashflow analysis [P19] shows that liquid assets and accessible pensions are projected to remain sufficient through the modelled period. The GIA and ISA offer immediate liquidity, and the SIPP, already accessible at age 55, offers flexible drawdown to manage the identified shortfall period. Vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics identified in the evidence that would materially affect this requirement's assessment.

  [G5.7] State Pension entitlement and other anticipated income sources
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts four income sources: (1) Private Pension (Defined Benefit from Aon): £22,000 gross per annum, stated as at 2025-12-16, with provenance from page 4 'Your current situation' section. (2) Employment (Client's own work): £890 net per month (stated as discontinued — 'you decided that this was not suited to you and have since stopped working altogether'), as at 2025-12-16. (3) Employment (Spouse Yvette): £28,000 gross per annum, stated as at 2025-12-16. (4) State Pension: £11,973 gross per annum, projected, starting at age 67 (i.e. from 2035-12-16 when client reaches age 67). The report states both clients 'expect to receive the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67'.
    File:     The evidence corroborates the first three income sources and the State Pension amount. [P23] (Suitability Report itself, category I) quotes: 'You also receive a defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum' and 'earning approximately £890 net per month however, you decided that this was not suited to you and have since stopped working altogether' and 'Yvette works full-time as a Medical Case Handler, earning £28,000 gross per annum'. [P1] (Cashflow - Tony Predeceases in 2040, category F) specifies 'State Pension of £11,973 (inflated by 2.5%) from age 67'. [P9] (Cashflow - Yvette Predeceases in 2040, category F) in its Income Statement shows 'DB Pension' as £22,000 for Anthony and 'Employment' as £16,800 for Yvette (not £28,000). [P6] (Cashflow - Tony Predeceases in 2040, category F) shows first year total regular income of £38,800 (£22,000 DB + £16,800 employment). The cashflow documents reference Yvette's salary as 'Yvette Salary £16,800 p/a salary income until 2030 retire in 2030 (inflated by 2% p/a)' [P1]. The Fact Find [P5] records 'Total Net Monthly Income' as £2,590.00 and 'Total Monthly Expenditure' as £2,206.67, with 'Total Monthly Disposable Income' of £383.33. The State Pension start date in the canonical model is given as 2035-12-16 (age 67), which aligns with the evidence showing client born 1970-01-06, making age 67 in 2037 (not 2035).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Yvette's employment income as £28,000 gross per annum (page 4, 'Your current situation'), but all cashflow projections [P1], [P6], [P9] consistently show £16,800 p/a until Yvette's planned retirement in 2030. This is a £11,200 annual difference (40% discrepancy).
      - Report states State Pension start date (startsOn field) as 2035-12-16, but client's date of birth (derived from age 55 as at 2025-12-16 in [P2]) is 1970-01-06, meaning age 67 would be reached in 2037, not 2035. The canonical model data contains a date error of approximately 2 years.
      - Fact Find [P5] records net monthly household income as £2,590.00, but if Yvette earns £28,000 gross (as the report states) this figure cannot be reconciled without knowing Yvette's tax; if she earns £16,800 gross as the cashflows show, the monthly equivalent before tax is approximately £1,400, making combined net income approximately £2,160 (client's £0 net after stopping work + £1,400 net Yvette + £22,000/12 = £1,833 DB pension net ≈ £3,233), which is higher than the £2,590 stated. The discrepancy suggests the Fact Find may have been completed at a different point or with different assumptions than the report.
    The requirement asks whether affordability is fully supported by and consistent with the evidence. Three material discrepancies are present: (1) Yvette's employment income is stated at two different values — £28,000 in the report text and £16,800 in all cashflow models. The cashflow models are the operative financial planning documents and consistently apply £16,800 across multiple scenarios (Current Situation, Tony Predeceases, Yvette Predeceases, Higher Inflation, Market Crash), suggesting the £16,800 is the correct operative figure used for affordability. The report's statement of £28,000 on page 4 is an unsupported assertion contradicted by the supporting cashflow calculations. (2) The State Pension start date in the canonical model (2035-12-16) is mathematically inconsistent with the client's age (55 as at 2025-12-16, making him age 67 in 2037, not 2035). This is a data extraction error; the report itself correctly states 'from age 67' and the cashflow evidence shows the pension flowing from 2037 onwards (see [P1] 'Retire age 67 in 2037'). (3) The Fact Find [P5] monthly income figure of £2,590 cannot be reconciled to either the report's stated income figures or the cashflow models, and pre-dates the report. The extraction ambiguity flag notes uncertainty about whether the £890/month employment ceased permanently or temporarily; the canonical model treats it as ceased (£0 ongoing), which the report text supports ('have since stopped working altogether'). The State Pension amount of £11,973 is corroborated as the full State Pension forecast figure [P1]. The affordability assessment in the report (page 5, 'target income of approximately £2,200 net per month') is stated in the report but the supporting cashflow calculations show the recommendation is affordable with the £16,800 Yvette income figure, not £28,000. The emergency fund of £10,000 is confirmed in [P5] and [P1]. Yvette's planned retirement at age 60 (which the report states as 'age 60' aligning with 2030 when she is 64) matches the cashflow timeline. The discrepancy between the report's stated Yvette income (£28,000) and the operative cashflow figure (£16,800) is material to affordability assessment: if affordability was demonstrated using £16,800, then stating £28,000 in the report overstates household income and creates an inconsistency. The report does not disclose which figure was used for the affordability cashflow shown. This is a Data inconsistency issue.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies five foreseeable changes: (1) Client retired after 35 years, ceased part-time work earning £890 net/month (dated 2025-08-01); (2) Inheritance of £100,000 expected this month (2025-12-16); (3) Yvette plans to reduce to 3 days per week from 2026, then fully retire when client turns 60 (2030-12-16); (4) State Pension eligibility for both clients at £11,973 per annum each from age 67 (2035-12-16); (5) IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 affecting unused DC pensions. Liquidity needs identified: emergency access (unquantified, confirmed as sufficient), property renovations £40,000 (near-term from inheritance), pension access via Flexi-Access Drawdown at age 55, and ad hoc access within 10-15 working days. Current monthly expenditure stated in report as £2,493 regular plus discretionary; cashflow inputs show £2,206/month. Clients confirm sufficient liquid capital retained for unexpected emergencies.
    File:     The Fact Find (P2) records: Total Net Monthly Income £2,590.00, Total Monthly Expenditure £2,206.67, Total Monthly Disposable Income £383.33. Emergency Fund amount set aside £10,000.00, Required £10,000.00, Shortfall £0.00. Assets include £100,000 'Incoming funds from sale of Dad's property' dated 06/08/2025 (not December 2025), £40,000 Chartered Savings Account, £20,000 MoneyBox Cash ISA, £20,000 Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA, £10,000 Cash Emergency Fund. Income section (P14) shows Anthony basic employed income £890 net/month, Private Pension Aon Final Salary £1,700 net/month, totalling £2,590 net. No changes expected in income or expenditure per Fact Find (P2). Cashflow scenarios (P1, P3-P13) project expenditure throughout retirement using £2,206/month baseline with 2% general inflation. Property renovations not explicitly modelled as a discrete item in cashflow but £100k inheritance is assumed in opening liquid assets in 2025. Yvette's work reduction from full-time to 3 days/week generates unquantified income reduction; full retirement at client age 60 (2030) shown in timeline but financial impact not modelled separately.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states current regular expenditure £2,493/month; Fact Find and cashflow model use £2,206.67/month (£287 difference unexplained and material to affordability assessment).
      - Report states inheritance expected 'this month' (December 2025); Fact Find records inheritance dated 06/08/2025, suggesting it may have already been received or the dates are inconsistent.
      - Report identifies Yvette's income reduction from full-time to 3 days/week from 2026 as a foreseeable change but does not quantify the financial impact (reduction from £28,000 gross per annum stated in report, or net ~£16,800 per Fact Find).
      - Report states Yvette will 'fully retire' at client age 60 (2030); cashflow does not explicitly separate or quantify income loss on that date—Yvette's employment income ceases but no transition modelling shown.
      - Fact Find (P2) states 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future?' answered 'No', yet report identifies Yvette's retirement as a planned change with financial impact and cashflow scenarios model variable expenditure through 2074.
    The requirement tests whether planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs are documented consistently and supported by evidence. The report does identify five foreseeable changes and articulates several liquidity needs. However, material inconsistencies undermine the reliability of the affordability assessment. First, the £287/month discrepancy between stated expenditure (£2,493) and modelled expenditure (£2,206.67) is unexplained. The extraction metadata notes this as an internal inconsistency (internalInconsistencies[2]), and the ambiguity guidance (ambiguities[2]) acknowledges that one figure includes discretionary spending while the other may not. This guard explains the difference in recording but not why the report asserts both without clarifying which is used in affordability conclusions. Second, the inheritance timing is recorded inconsistently: the Fact Find dates it 06/08/2025 but the report text states 'due this month' (December 2025 is the document date). The extraction metadata flags this as ambiguous (ambiguities[1]). This matters because if the inheritance was received in August and is already included in liquid assets, the 'due this month' language in December is misleading about its status as a foreseeable receipt. Third, Yvette's work reduction and retirement are identified as foreseeable changes but their financial impact is not quantified. The report states she earns £28,000 gross per annum and will reduce to 3 days/week from 2026, then retire at client age 60 (2030). No net income reduction is stated for the 3-day week scenario, and full retirement income loss is not isolated in the cashflow (the Fact Find states 'No' to expected income changes, contradicting the report's identification of Yvette's reduction as a change). The Fact Find also states 'No' to expected expenditure changes, yet the report and cashflow clearly model expenditure across 50+ years of retirement with variable scenarios. This appears to reflect a recording artifact (the Fact Find baseline was completed before these changes were formally incorporated into the plan), but it creates a discrepancy between the Fact Find's stated position and the report's assertions. Fourth, emergency fund provision is addressed: the Fact Find sets aside £10,000 and records no shortfall; the report states clients confirm sufficient liquid capital for unexpected emergencies. However, the quantum of 'sufficient' is not independently justified—it rests on client confirmation alone. The £10,000 emergency fund is not explicitly reconciled to the £383.33 monthly surplus or the £40,000 renovation spend. The planned £40,000 renovation is funded from inheritance; post-renovation, the client has £60,000 remaining (£100,000 less £40,000), which, combined with the £10,000 emergency fund, £20,000+£20,000 ISA holdings, and the £40,000 Chartered Savings account = £150,000 liquid assets excluding pension and property. The cashflow models open to 2025 with £142,326 liquid assets (P3), consistent with this quantum. The cashflow models show no red-bar events (insufficient assets) across all scenarios reviewed (P3-P13), indicating sustained affordability even under market crash and longevity stress scenarios. This supports the adequacy of the emergency fund and overall liquidity position. However, the report does not explicitly demonstrate that the planned £40,000 renovation spend is affordable within the £383.33 monthly surplus context, only that inheritance covers it. The discrepancies in income/expenditure reporting and the lack of quantified impact for Yvette's transitional changes weaken confidence that the cashflow model inputs fully align with the assertions in the narrative. The guard on expenditure ('expenditure already inside the monthly budget must not be counted a second time as a future one-off') applies: the £2,206/month figure includes all regular committed spending, so the £40,000 renovation is appropriately treated as a one-off, not double-counted. This does not resolve the £287 discrepancy or the inconsistency between the Fact Find's 'No changes' response and the report's identification of Yvette's changes as foreseeable.
