CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). Report asserts 'Aviva Platform is an excellent platform with comprehensive investment choices and facilities suitable for your needs' marked as non-client-specific, yet no evidence document justifies this assertion or compares platforms; no alternative platforms are documented as considered. Report asserts current funds 'have a risk level of 6' but evidence P8 and P14 show the existing holdings are 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds' with a 'combined risk level of 6' — this is an aggregate of two different risk levels (approximately 5 and 7), not a single fund at level 6; the distinction matters for whether the rationale is accurate. Report links rationales to objectives OBJ1-OBJ5 but no document in the file lists or defines these objectives; the mapping cannot be verified. 10 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report provides 12 rationale statements across three recommendations (Pension, GIA, ISA). The report marks 11 of these as client-specific (isClientSpecific true) and 1 as boilerplate (isClientSpecific false). Rationales include: platform suitability ('Aviva Platform is an excellent platform'); risk alignment ('current funds have risk level 6, does not match agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4'); cost reduction ('reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%'); fund design ('recommended new fund specifically designed for risk level 4'); professional oversight; pension contribution efficiency; GIA flexibility and tax planning; Bed & ISA tax efficiency; ISA suitability; and performance comparison (ISA funds 4.72% vs Moneybox 4.5%). The rationales are linked to objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3, OBJ4, OBJ5.
    File:     Research reports P2, P3, P5, P7 confirm risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) as the agreed profile and document the research process for product and platform selection across Pension, ISA, and GIA wrappers. P4 confirms existing Aviva pension details including 0.32% platform charge and £79,092.32 transfer value. P14 (suitability report itself) shows performance comparison data and volatility analysis. P6 documents ISA product selection with Bed & ISA feature confirmed. P9 confirms TFAS's independent advice approach and commitment to act in client's best interests. P12-P13 document fee structure and service agreements. However, the file contains no explicit statement of client objectives/goals (OBJ1-OBJ5), no documented consideration of alternatives with reasons for rejection, no explicit explanation linking each wrapper choice (Pension, GIA, ISA) to specific client circumstances or objectives, and no meeting notes explaining the rationale for the specific fund selections or why these particular wrappers suit the client's needs.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'Aviva Platform is an excellent platform with comprehensive investment choices and facilities suitable for your needs' marked as non-client-specific, yet no evidence document justifies this assertion or compares platforms; no alternative platforms are documented as considered.
      - Report asserts current funds 'have a risk level of 6' but evidence P8 and P14 show the existing holdings are 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds' with a 'combined risk level of 6' — this is an aggregate of two different risk levels (approximately 5 and 7), not a single fund at level 6; the distinction matters for whether the rationale is accurate.
      - Report links rationales to objectives OBJ1-OBJ5 but no document in the file lists or defines these objectives; the mapping cannot be verified.
      - Report asserts 'recommended ISA funds have provided an average performance of 4.72% over a 5-year period' with comparison to 'Moneybox interest rate of circa 4.5%'; P14 shows performance data but does not state 4.72% as the recommended ISA fund performance, and no Moneybox comparison appears in the evidence.
      - Report recommends Pension, GIA, and ISA wrappers but provides no explicit explanation of why three separate wrappers are needed, how they interact, or why each is suitable for the client's circumstances; alternatives (consolidation, single wrapper strategy) are not documented as considered.
      - Report states the recommended fund 'is specifically designed for risk level 4 investors' but P14 identifies the fund as 'Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II' and confirms it matches Risk Level 4 profile; the rationale is general to the fund type, not client-specific to this client's circumstances.
      - Report asserts 'By using a GIA, you can take advantage of your annual Capital Gains Tax (CGT) allowance through careful planning' but the file contains no evidence of CGT planning, no illustration of expected gains, no explanation of how the client's circumstances make CGT planning material or suitable, and no documentation that this was discussed.
      - Report asserts 'A pension contribution of £3,600 gross will utilise your annual allowance tax-efficiently' but no evidence document confirms the client agreed to this contribution amount, stated this as an objective, or was shown the impact on tax relief or retirement provision.
    The report contains rationale statements, and 11 are marked client-specific. However, the lack of corroborating evidence raises material concerns: (1) Objectives are referenced but not defined in the file. The mapping to OBJ1-OBJ5 cannot be verified. (2) The rationale 'Aviva Platform is an excellent platform' is marked non-specific and is unsupported by any comparison or research document. (3) The risk profile rationale is generally accurate — P3, P7, P10, P14 confirm risk level 4 is agreed — but the assertion that current funds 'have a risk level of 6' is imprecise: P14 states they are 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6,' not a single asset at level 6. (4) Cost reduction is stated (0.32% to 0.21%) and corroborated by P4; however, charge arithmetic in the case file shows the total charge impact is +0.08% (£427 to £512.69), not a reduction. (5) The fund, platform, and product selections are documented in research reports P2, P3, P5, P6, P7, confirming the Aviva funds and TFAS platform meet the DNA filters; however, no alternative funds, platforms, or products are documented as considered and rejected. (6) Professional oversight, ISA suitability, and tax efficiency are asserted but not linked to this client's specific circumstances, known support needs, or documented discussion. (7) The 4.72% ISA performance figure and Moneybox comparison do not appear in P14 or any other passage; this specific rationale is unsupported. (8) The three-wrapper strategy (Pension, GIA, ISA) is recommended but no evidence explains why three wrappers suit this client's objectives better than alternatives. (9) Vulnerability overlay: The extraction notes record ambiguities in the client's employment status and inheritance timing, and the risk capacity assessment notes a future shortfall period. None of these is reflected in the rationale statements, which do not address how these circumstances influence the suitability of the recommendations. The rationale statements are largely generic across investment recommendations and lack material client-specific grounding in actual circumstances, objectives, or documented consideration of alternatives.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that Aviva Platform is recommended as the provider based on nine justifications: (1) 'The Aviva Platform is an excellent platform with comprehensive investment choices and facilities suitable for your needs' (stated as client-specific); (2) 'Financial strength - Aviva is rated A (Stable) by S&P, with £407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024)'; (3) 'Choice - Over 6,900 funds and leading managers, across ISAs, pensions, and GIAs'; (4) 'Flexibility - ISA allows withdrawals and replacements in the same tax year'; (5) 'Expertise - Aviva Investors manages award-winning strategies across asset classes, plus access to private markets'; (6) 'Value - Transparent, competitive charges with potential tiered pricing'; (7) 'Convenience - Online tools for viewing performance, switching funds, and accessing documents'; (8) 'Governance - Strong regulatory oversight and risk management'; (9) 'Innovation - Ongoing digital investment and enhanced cash management tools'. For investment strategy, the report states: 'The funds reflect your risk profile and are managed to remain within this risk tolerance' and 'We have recommended a multi-asset fund because it provides a professionally managed, diversified investment solution that aligns with your financial goals and risk profile' linked to OBJ1. The report also states 'Lower Costs - Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' and 'Risk Alignment - The current funds have a risk level of 6, which is too high for your agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4. Switching to the recommended fund will ensure your investments match your risk tolerance.' The wrapper is SIPP (Self-Invested Personal Pension). The recommended fund is Aviva Pension Portfolio Multi-asset Plus II. The report states fund selection is justified by: 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers', 'competitive charges', and that the funds are 'part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition)' which is 'regularly reviewed by a quarterly investment committee' using 'a leading external research team'.
    File:     [P3] identifies Aviva Platform as 'Recommended Platform' with profile stating 'Aviva is the largest insurance group in the UK is also a provider of life and pensions products to Europe. The Aviva brand name was launched in July 2002. The group was formed by the merger of CGU and Norwich Union in 2000.' [P3] notes 'No additional justification text has been entered' under Platform selection. [P4] and [P9] confirm agreed risk level 4 'Cautious Balanced' and state 'Following an interactive discussion, and considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal, we agreed a risk level.' [P4] states the risk-rated fund selected was for 'level 4 Cautious Balanced' mapping. [P2] shows performance comparison of current strategy (A) against recommended fund (C for Pension) demonstrating 'lower volatility than your current mix of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6.' [P2] confirms 'The recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II provides more consistent performance aligned with your Risk Level 4 profile.' [P7] and [P8] contain extensive brochure comparisons of Aviva Platform features including platform access, fund range, withdrawal facilities, and adviser tools. [P1] details Aviva's withdrawal and facilities capabilities. [P14] confirms 'Risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level' and notes fund performance relative to peers. [P11] shows CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc objectives and policy. [P16] shows Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice product brochure comparison. No passages contain comparison of Aviva Platform against alternative providers, no evidence of alternatives considered, and no documented whole-of-market search or defined panel process explicitly justifying panel use. [P18] references 'products from a fully researched panel' but does not name it or explain the panel composition or criteria. No evidence provided of why Aviva was selected over other platforms in the market.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 'The Aviva Platform is an excellent platform with comprehensive investment choices and facilities suitable for your needs' as client-specific justification, but no passage supports this assertion with client-specific reasoning — file shows platform choice was made but provides no explicit client-specific rationale for Aviva over alternatives
      - Report cites 'Financial strength - Aviva is rated A (Stable) by S&P, with £407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024)' but this rating and figure are not corroborated in any retrieved passage
      - Report justifies choice with seven generic platform features (Flexibility, Expertise, Value, Convenience, Governance, Innovation, and their elaborations) that describe Aviva's absolute capabilities but are not presented as comparative advantages over named alternatives
      - Report states provider was selected but [P3] explicitly records 'No additional justification text has been entered' under Platform selection, creating internal inconsistency between the report's narrative and the research document's record
      - Report asserts fund selection is 'part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition)' regularly reviewed by investment committee, but no evidence of a documented, named CIP panel is provided in the case file, only generic reference in [P18] to 'a fully researched panel'
      - Report links investment strategy recommendation to objective OBJ1 and states funds 'reflect your risk profile', but does not identify or consider any alternative funds at risk level 4 Cautious Balanced or explain why Aviva Multi-asset Plus II was selected over other Level 4 funds available on platform or market
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Evidence of alternative providers evaluated and reasons for rejection of each, Documented panel composition, selection criteria, and process by which Aviva Platform was included or selected, Documented evidence of CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition) review process and which alternative funds at Risk Level 4 were considered and rejected, Comparative analysis of Aviva Platform charges against competing platforms available to the client, Comparative analysis of Aviva Multi-asset Plus II fund against other Level 4 multi-asset funds available on the market or platform, Evidence that S&P rating A (Stable) and £407bn AUM figure are current, verified, and sourced
    The requirement asks for client-specific explanation of why the recommended product, provider and investment solution meets the client's demands and needs, including consideration of relevant alternatives. The report provides justification for Aviva Platform consisting principally of nine marketing-style claims about Aviva's capabilities: financial strength, fund choice, flexibility, expertise, value, convenience, governance, and innovation. These are presented as absolute strengths of the provider, not as comparative advantages over alternatives. The guard notes that 'a provider's marketing strengths stated in isolation are not a justification for selecting it over alternatives — distinguish absolute claims from comparative ones.' Under this guard, the report's justifications are mostly absolute claims without comparative framing. The only claim framed as comparative is 'Lower Costs - Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%', which compares the new platform charge to the existing one, not to other platform alternatives. No named alternative platforms are mentioned, no research shows competitors were evaluated, and no reasons for rejection of alternatives are given. The research documents ([P3], [P7], [P8], [P16]) demonstrate that a platform research and selection process occurred (brochure comparisons and feature tables exist), but [P3] records that 'No additional justification text has been entered' under Platform selection, indicating the reasoning was not documented in the research phase. For investment strategy, the report links the recommendation to 'your risk profile' (OBJ1) and states funds reflect this profile and are at Level 4 Cautious Balanced, which is corroborated by [P2], [P4], [P9], and [P14]. However, the report does not identify or compare alternative Level 4 funds. The reference to CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition) as a legitimising factor is acknowledged by the guard: 'Firm panel or CIP membership is a legitimate justification if the report says so; do not require a fresh whole-of-market search where a documented panel process exists.' The report does invoke CIP, and [P18] references 'a fully researched panel', which may satisfy the guard. However, [P3] records 'No Product DNA analysis was carried out', suggesting the fund research may have been limited. The report claims funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers', and [P2] provides performance comparison showing the recommended fund has lower volatility than current holdings; [P14] shows performance relative to benchmark. This partially supports the performance claim. The S&P rating and AUM figure are not verified in the file. No passage confirms these figures are current as of 31 Dec 2024 or that they are accurate. The client circumstances show that risk alignment is a genuine driver (current funds at level 6, client agreed to level 4), and cost reduction (0.32% to 0.21% platform charge) is material. These client-specific factors are articulated in the report, but the selection of Aviva Platform and Aviva Multi-asset Plus II as the means to achieve them is not explained through comparison with alternatives. The comparison cannot be performed because alternative platforms and alternative Level 4 funds are not presented in the research documents or discussed in the report. Without evidence of alternatives considered, the claim that the recommendation is suitable because it meets the client's profile cannot be distinguished from a claim that it is the only option or the default option.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model extracted from the suitability report indicates that 12 recommendations are assessed for client-specificity. The first recommendation is marked as NOT client-specific (isClientSpecific: false), while recommendations 2 through 12 are all marked as client-specific (isClientSpecific: true). This indicates the report asserts that the majority of its recommendations (11 of 12) are personalised and linked to individual circumstances.
    File:     The case file contains abundant client-specific information: Client objectives documented in multiple places include (1) reviewing existing Aviva Platform pension for suitability [P4, P13]; (2) receiving £100k incoming from late father's property sale and seeking tax-efficient investment [P4, P6, P13]; (3) wanting to be fully retired by age 60 with minimum income of £2,200 net per month [P4, P6, P13]; (4) client completing ATRQ to determine risk tolerance, noted as not yet complete at fact-find but sent by email [P2, P4]. Assets are detailed: £400k main residence (mortgage-free), £40k cash savings, £20k cash ISA, spouse's £20k ISA, £10k emergency fund [P7]. Existing pension arrangement value is £88,590.88 as of 23/07/2026 [P8]. The file confirms client preferences regarding ISA transfers ("would have to be really worth his while - not dead set on doing this") [P4]. However, the suitability report itself — the document being assessed — is not present in the evidence pack. The case file contains fact-find, client agreement, research reports, and product key features documents, but does not include the actual suitability report text, rationales, product justifications, alternative analysis, or the specific personalised language used in each recommendation.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records isClientSpecific = false for recommendation 1, but the suitability report document itself is absent from the evidence pack, making it impossible to verify whether recommendation 1 actually lacks personalisation or whether this is an extraction error.
      - The canonical model records isClientSpecific = true for recommendations 2-12, but the suitability report containing the actual rationales, justifications and personalised language is absent, preventing verification that these recommendations are genuinely client-specific rather than boilerplate.
      - The requirement asks whether the report's explanation is 'fully supported by and consistent with' evidence, but the report's explanations themselves are not provided in the evidence pack — only the client's circumstances and research materials are present.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The suitability report itself is absent from the evidence pack. The comparison requires access to: the full text of each recommendation's rationale; the justifications given for product, provider and wrapper choices; the analysis of alternatives considered and reasons for their rejection; the personalised language used in each recommendation linking it to the client's stated objectives and circumstances.
    This requirement cannot be assessed because the primary document — the suitability report — is not included in the evidence pack. The canonical model extracted from the report shows which recommendations were assessed as client-specific, but extraction metadata alone cannot substitute for the actual content that would demonstrate personalisation. The evidence pack contains extensive client-specific factual information (objectives, circumstances, assets, preferences) which would be used to support personalised advice, but it does not contain the report's actual recommendations, rationales or language. The extraction note at the case outset indicates 'ambiguities' and 'internalInconsistencies' within the report regarding employment income status, inheritance timing, capacity for loss reconciliation, charge arithmetic, and expenditure figures — these are documented within the report itself but the report text is not provided for review. Without access to the actual suitability report, it is impossible to determine whether recommendations are personalised, whether rationales are client-specific or boilerplate, whether alternatives are presented with reasoned rejection, or whether claims made in the recommendations are supported by the evidence available. The requirement explicitly requires that the report's explanation be 'fully supported by and consistent with the evidence provided' — this consistency check cannot be performed when one side of the comparison (the report's actual language and reasoning) is not in the evidence pack.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that five alternatives were considered and rejected: (1) 'Retain current funds within pension' rejected because funds have risk levels 5 and 7, not aligned with agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4; (2) 'Stakeholder pension' rejected as less cost-effective than the recommended solution and lacking flexible access and fund ranges; (3) 'Leave inheritance uninvested in cash' rejected due to inflation erosion and inability to meet objectives for medium-to-long-term wealth growth; (4) 'Venture Capital Trusts (VCTs) and Enterprise Investment Schemes (EIS)' rejected as high-risk, illiquid, and unsuitable for the client's objectives; and (5) 'Investment bonds (onshore and offshore)' rejected on unspecified grounds. Each rejection reason is attributed to specific topics (RiskAlignment, CostComparison, LimitedFlexibility, InflationErosion, InvestorExperience). The report quotes: 'Although the funds have provided greater performance, they are not aligned with your risk profile. One fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7' and 'the solution is more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' and 'long-term investment in cash is not recommended due to its inability to keep pace with inflation, leading to erosion of real value over time'.
    File:     The research documents show that five alternatives were researched and documented: (1) 'Leave the existing pension scheme untouched' rejected due to current funds risk level 6 not matching agreed Cautious Balanced profile of 4; (2) 'Leave the existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds' rejected because moving to TFAS agency with Multi-Asset Plus Fund II provides reduced platform charges (0.32% to 0.21%), professional oversight, and adviser charging facilitation; (3) 'Switch the existing pension scheme into another existing pension arrangement' rejected as Aviva Platform is already excellent; (4) 'Switch existing schemes into employer's pension arrangement' rejected as client is retired; and (5) 'Set up a new pension plan' rejected as Aviva Platform is optimal. Research also documents a sixth option for ISA: retaining current Moneybox Cash ISA at 4.5% rejected because recommended ISA funds averaged 4.72% over 5 years. [P1], [P11], and [P16] show the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II was selected with Defaqto risk rating of 4, weighted AMC of 0.41%, and 5-year cumulative return of 36.21%, compared in data comparisons against CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund (31.28% return), HSBC Global Strategy Conservative Portfolio (25.90% return), and Vanguard LifeStrategy 40% (21.49% return). The pension switch report [P2] and [P4] show no material effect on switching: 'Effect of switching' is £0 or 0.0% decrease at low and mid growth rates. [P3] shows existing fund allocations: Vanguard 60% at 35.02% and Vanguard 80% at 64.95%, with combined performance of 12.86% (2021), -9.65% (2022), 11.23% (2023), 11.95% (2024), 13.47% YTD.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts five alternatives considered (retain current funds, stakeholder pension, leave inheritance in cash, VCTs/EIS, investment bonds). Research documents show additional alternatives researched (in-situ fund switch within existing arrangement, switch to another pension, set up new plan, ISA retention option).
      - Report rejection reason for 'Retain current funds' cites risk levels 5 and 7 for individual funds. Research shows existing funds described as combined risk level 6, and individual funds Vanguard 60% and 80% are presented without explicit mapping to risk levels 5 and 7 in the research documents.
      - Report states 'the solution is more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' as rejection reason. Research comparison [P1], [P11] does not include a stakeholder pension as a data comparison point; no empirical cost comparison between recommended solution and stakeholder pensions is presented in evidence.
      - Report rejects 'Leave inheritance uninvested in cash' citing client objectives for medium-to-long-term wealth growth. However, the case file does not confirm inheritance amount, timing, or explicit objectives addressing inheritance deployment.
      - Report mentions 'Investment bonds (onshore and offshore)' as fifth alternative but provides truncated rejection text in extraction. Complete rejection reasoning is not evidenced in the provided passages.
      - Research shows 'in-situ fund switch' alternative considered and rejected because 'moving under TFAS agency...provides added benefits,' but this alternative is not mentioned in the report's stated alternatives list.
    The report identifies five named alternatives with stated rejection reasons. The research documents support consideration of multiple alternatives but reveal a discrepancy in how alternatives are categorized and presented. The research documents show six distinct alternatives researched (five pension/product alternatives plus ISA retention), whereas the report names five alternatives. A key material difference: the research documents show an 'in-situ fund switch' option (switching funds within existing Aviva arrangement) was researched and rejected, but this is not named as an alternative in the report's recommendations section. The report's rejection reasons are client-specific and linked to objectives in most cases. However, critical discrepancies exist: (1) The risk level attribution to individual funds differs—the report claims the existing funds align to risk levels 5 and 7, but the research documents show Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% without explicit risk-level mapping in those terms; the research references only 'current funds have risk level of 6' [P16]. (2) The stakeholder pension cost comparison asserts the recommended solution is 'more cost effective' but no empirical comparison with an actual stakeholder pension product is presented in the data comparisons [P11]; the comparison only includes other actively managed and passive multi-asset funds. (3) The inheritance-in-cash rejection reason assumes the client has stated objectives about inheritance deployment, but the case file shows ambiguity about inheritance receipt timing and whether this is a confirmed receipt or expected receipt [per extraction report]. (4) The report cites investment bond alternative with stated reasons, but the rejection text is truncated in the extraction and the full justification is not accessible. The comparison under SetCoverage guard: options are listed with stated individual rejection reasons in both report and research, meeting the guard requirement. However, the alternative of in-situ fund switching, which appears in research but not in report recommendations, indicates a reporting gap—the report did not disclose to the client that a fund-switch-only alternative was considered. This is a material omission because it represents a less disruptive path (no platform change, no adviser charging model change) that was researched but not presented for client comparison.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report identifies one existing arrangement: Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd - Pension Portfolio (SIPP) with an advice action of 'RetainAndSwitchFunds'. The report provides 12 separate rationale statements linked to this action, spanning platform strengths, risk alignment, cost reduction, professional oversight, tax efficiency, adviser charging facilitation, and administrative convenience. These rationales are marked as client-specific and linked to stated objectives (OBJ1, OBJ4, OBJ5). The report states: 'The current funds have a risk level of 6, which does not match your agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4' and 'By moving the pension under TFAS agency, you will benefit from a reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%'. It recommends switching to a new fund 'specifically designed for risk level 4 investors' and making a £3,600 gross pension contribution.
    File:     The case file contains substantial evidence on the existing Aviva arrangement. Policy research [P1-P5] confirms the arrangement details: Aviva AV2075926 with fund value £79,092.32, 3000 funds available, flexi access drawdown available, no exit penalties, and adviser charging available. Transaction history [P11-P15] shows Aviva charges deducted regularly. The arrangement currently holds Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%) and 80% Equity (64.95%) with combined fund charges of 0.22% [P6]. Current platform charge is 0.32% [P21]. The pension switch report [P6, P21] confirms existing platform charge of 0.32% and transfer value of £79,092.32. The recommended new fund is identified as 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II' with 0.41% fund charge [P20], yielding a total charge of 0.62% (platform 0.21% + fund 0.41%) [P6]. The existing current risk level is documented as 6 in the Personal Pension Plan review [P17]. Risk profile documentation [P20] confirms the target risk level 4 is 'Cautious Balanced'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% as a benefit, but this is only the platform component; the overall annual recurring charge increases from 0.54% (platform 0.32% + funds 0.22%) to 0.62% (platform 0.21% + fund 0.41%), a net increase of 0.08%
      - Report presents rationales separately across 12 distinct text passages that repeat themes (e.g., 'Risk Alignment' appears in multiple passages with identical phrasing structure), suggesting potential boilerplate language
      - Report states current funds have 'risk level of 6' but evidence shows funds are weighted 60% and 80% equity (Vanguard LifeStrategy), which are different risk levels (approximately 5 and 7 respectively), not a simple unified level 6
      - Rationale states 'recommended fund is specifically designed for risk level 4 investors' but does not explain why the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II (at 0.41% charge) is superior to the current Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% (at 0.22% charge) beyond risk alignment, despite significantly higher cost
    The report does provide a stated recommended outcome ('RetainAndSwitchFunds') for the existing arrangement and multiple rationale statements. However, material concerns arise: (1) The primary cost-efficiency rationale is incomplete and potentially misleading. The report highlights the 0.11% platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%) as a benefit [canonical model rationale 3], but this masks a net 0.08% total charge increase when fund charges are considered (0.54% to 0.62%). Evidence [P6, P21] confirms the existing platform charge is 0.32% with fund charges of 0.22% (total 0.54%), and the new solution has platform charge 0.21% with fund charge 0.41% (total 0.62%). The report's rationale focuses on platform charge reduction without acknowledging the fund charge increase or net cost impact. (2) The risk alignment rationale states current funds have 'risk level of 6' [canonical model and P17], but evidence shows this comprises two different Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (60% and 80% equity) with different implicit risk levels [P6]. A 60% equity fund is typically risk level 5, and an 80% equity fund is typically risk level 7, not a uniform level 6. This conflation may misrepresent the risk profile. (3) The rationales, while marked as client-specific, show repetitive structure and near-identical phrasing across multiple passages (e.g., two separate 'Risk Alignment' passages with very similar wording, two separate 'Professional Oversight' passages, two separate 'Tax-Efficient Contribution' passages). This suggests potential reliance on template language rather than bespoke reasoning for this specific arrangement. (4) The recommendation does link to identified objectives (OBJ1, OBJ4, OBJ5), providing connection to client needs. (5) The action is clear: retain the platform, switch funds, and make a contribution. However, the cost comparison rationale does not fully support the suitability of this specific recommendation when total charges are considered.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report makes three recommendations: (1) Switch/Retain Aviva Pension Portfolio, moving to TFAS agency with reduced platform charge and switching funds to align with risk profile, with intended outcome to reduce charges from 0.32% to 0.21%, align investments with Cautious Balanced risk profile (level 4), and utilize annual pension allowance tax-efficiently. The report states this is 'Recommended' and the adviser 'will implement' it. (2) Invest £57,120 in Aviva Platform Investment Portfolio (GIA) using CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc, with intended outcome to invest remaining inheritance tax-efficiently while providing flexibility and growth to support desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month. The report states this is 'Recommended' and the adviser 'will implement' it. (3) Transfer £20,000 Cash ISA to Aviva Platform Stocks & Shares ISA and invest in CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc, with intended outcome to move funds to achieve greater growth while maintaining tax-free status and providing flexibility within consolidated platform. The report states this is 'Recommended' and the adviser 'will implement' it. Implementation timescale is stated as '10-15 working days'. A specific next step is documented: the adviser has posted out an Expression of Wish form for beneficiary nomination, with instructions to return it using a pre-paid envelope.
    File:     The evidence provided includes product key features documents for Aviva Pension Portfolio and ISA products [P4, P7, P1, P2, P3], an Aviva GIA Illustration [P5, P6] showing investment charges of 0.29% OCF/TER for CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc and adviser charges of 1.00% ongoing (estimated £47.60/month), a Risk Profile Report [P9, P13] containing no notes on risk level agreement or investment target, a Cashflow Current Situation document [P10, P11] with graphical information but limited detail, a Fact Find [P12], a Client Agreement To Proceed [P14] outlining ongoing services and fee structures generally, and an Investment ISA research report [P15] describing the Cautious Balanced risk profile (level 4). The evidence does not provide specific corroboration of the adviser's stated intent to implement the three recommendations, nor does it detail who specifically will carry out each action or confirm the timescales stated in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states that for Recommendation 1, the adviser 'will implement' the pension portfolio switch and fund change. The evidence does not document the adviser's commitment to execute this action or the specific steps the adviser will take to implement it.
      - The report states that for Recommendation 2, the adviser 'will implement' the £57,120 GIA investment. The evidence (GIA Illustration [P5, P6]) shows the product structure and charges but does not confirm the adviser's intention to implement this recommendation or document the adviser's role in doing so.
      - The report states that for Recommendation 3, the adviser 'will implement' the £20,000 Cash ISA transfer. The evidence (ISA Key Features [P1, P2, P3]) describes the ISA product but does not document the adviser's commitment to implement the transfer or their specific actions.
      - The report states an implementation timescale of '10-15 working days' but the evidence does not corroborate this timescale or explain what administrative steps are included within it.
      - The report documents a specific next step (Expression of Wish form for beneficiary nomination) but the evidence does not contain the actual form, instructions to the client, or confirmation that this step is client-specific beyond what is stated in the report itself.
    The requirement asks whether the report states (1) whether each change is recommended, (2) who will implement it, and (3) the intended outcome, and whether this explanation is fully supported by and consistent with the evidence. The report does state clearly for each of the three recommendations that they are 'Recommended' and that the 'Adviser' will implement them (willAdviserImplement: true, responsibleParty: 'Adviser'). The report also articulates a specific intended outcome for each recommendation. However, the evidence does not corroborate the adviser's stated commitment to implement these actions. The retrieved passages contain product documentation, illustrations, and general service agreements but do not include specific communications from the adviser to the client confirming implementation intent, implementation timescales, or the specific actions the adviser will undertake. The evidence is silent on the adviser's actual commitment to carry out these three changes. The fact that the adviser states they will implement the recommendations is an assertion the report makes for itself, not something the supporting documents confirm. Per the instruction that 'a recommendation names no objective, provider or wrapper choice is asserted without comparison, or alternatives are absent or listed without reasons' would constitute a Potential Concern, here the recommendations do name objectives, providers, and wrappers, and they are justified. The intended outcomes are articulated and linked to the client's objectives. The specific next step regarding the Expression of Wish form is documented in the report with isClientSpecific: true, but the evidence provided does not include independent verification that this step has been communicated to the client or that the form has been sent. The GIA Illustration [P5, P6] confirms that the investment vehicle and fund exist with stated charges, but does not confirm adviser implementation. The product Key Features documents [P1, P2, P3, P4, P7] provide factual product information but not evidence of adviser commitment to implement. The evidence is sufficient to assess that the products recommended exist and are available; it is insufficient to verify the adviser's stated intent and capability to implement each recommendation. This represents a gap between what the report asserts about its own actions and what the supporting file demonstrates.

  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report presents five options for the pension recommendation: Option 1 (Leave existing pension untouched), Option 2 (Leave in place and switch funds), Option 3 (Switch into another existing pension), Option 4 (Switch into employer's pension), and Option 5 (Set up new pension plan). The extracted model shows all five options marked isRecommended=false. The report then states a single recommended action: 'Retain Aviva Pension Portfolio on Aviva Platform, move to TFAS agency for reduced platform charge, switch underlying funds to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II to match risk profile, and contribute £3,600 gross (£2,880 net).' The adviceStatus field shows 'Recommended' for all three recommendations (pension action, GIA investment, ISA transfer). The extracted field presentsUnresolvedOptions is false, indicating the extraction determined no unresolved options remain.
    File:     The case file contains client notes, a risk profile report, and research documents but does not explicitly resolve which of the five presented options the client should select or why the recommended action differs from or supersedes these options. P1 contains advisor notes about considerations (e.g. 'Does he have a P60/P45? Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working') and P9 lists client objectives including 'Review his existing Aviva Platform pension for suitability', but neither document explicitly records the client's decision on which option to pursue or the adviser's reasoning for recommending the specific course of action (TFAS agency move with fund switch) over the five alternatives presented.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report presents five explicitly named options for the pension arrangement, all marked as not recommended (isRecommended=false for all), yet does not explicitly state in the extracted text which of these five options aligns with the final recommendation to 'move to TFAS agency for reduced platform charge, switch underlying funds' - this appears to be a hybrid or sixth option not among the five presented alternatives.
      - The five options presented (1: do nothing, 2: switch funds only, 3: switch to another pension, 4: switch to employer scheme, 5: set up new plan) do not clearly map to the recommended action (retain with provider change and fund switch), creating ambiguity about whether the recommendation constitutes a distinct sixth course of action or whether it should have been presented as one of the original five.
      - No passage in the file resolves which of the five options the client selected or agreed to, nor does any passage explain why the final recommendation differs from the five options explicitly set out.
    The requirement addresses whether recommendations present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why. The guard specifies that 'setting out options and then naming one as recommended is good practice, not a finding' and that 'the finding is an option left unresolved'. The extraction shows presentsUnresolvedOptions=false for recommendation[0] and adviceStatus='Recommended'. However, the five options presented to the client (leave untouched, switch funds only, switch schemes, use employer scheme, set up new plan) are all marked isRecommended=false. The final recommendation to 'move to TFAS agency for reduced platform charge, switch underlying funds to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' does not map cleanly onto any of these five options. Option 2 ('Leave the existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds') is closest, but the recommended action also involves 'move to TFAS agency', which is a provider/platform change, not merely a fund switch. The critical issue is that the five options were presented to the client with all marked as not recommended, yet the report advances a specific recommendation that does not clearly correspond to any of the five. The file contains no passage (P1-P10) that records which of the five options the client chose or that explains why the final recommendation represents the preferred course among them. While the adviser may have made a reasoned choice, the case file does not evidence that choice or its reasoning. The requirement for 'client-specific rationale' linked to the options presented is not demonstrably met by the evidence: the rationale statements in the extraction (risk alignment, platform quality, cost reduction, professional oversight) support the final recommendation but do not explain why it was preferred over the five alternatives set out. The absence of reasonGiven for each optionsPresented item (noted as absent from the extracted model) means the rejection reasons for the five options are not recorded in a way the file can corroborate.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states implementation timescale of '10-15 working days' for access to funds in the future. It specifies three actions: 'Switch', 'NewPlan', and 'Transfer', each to be undertaken by 'Adviser'. For the Aviva plan specifically, the report states next steps include posting out an Expression of Wish form for the client to sign and return using a pre-paid envelope. The report also discloses out-of-market risk and cancellation rights (30 days).
    File:     The supporting documents (Aviva Pension Illustration, Aviva Pension Key Features, Aviva GIA Key Features, and research reports) describe operational features of the platforms and products but do not corroborate specific implementation timescales, responsible parties for the recommended actions, or sequencing of the switch/transfer/new plan activities. The documents describe general platform processes and client access capabilities but do not address the specific timeline or party responsibility for executing these three discrete recommendations.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report specifies '10-15 working days' as timescale for fund access; this is stated in the context of 'Should you require access to these funds in the future' and appears to refer to a service standard for disinvestment/banking rather than the timescale for completing the recommendations themselves. No evidence corroborates a timescale for completing the Switch, NewPlan, and Transfer actions or their sequencing.
      - Report names 'Adviser' as responsible party for all three actions (Switch, NewPlan, Transfer) but does not specify which adviser, adviser firm, or whether implementation occurs simultaneously or sequentially.
      - Report states Expression of Wish form has been 'posted out' as a next step but does not specify when this form must be completed or returned relative to the implementation of the three main recommendations.
    The requirement calls for material timing and responsibility information on what action will be taken, when, and by whom. The report does identify actions (Switch, NewPlan, Transfer), assigns responsibility generically to 'Adviser', and names a timescale ('10-15 working days'). However, the timescale cited appears to address fund access in future drawdown scenarios, not the implementation timeline for the recommendations themselves. The Expression of Wish next step is client-specific and personal but lacks a completion deadline or link to recommendation implementation sequencing. The evidence retrieved from product literature confirms operational processes (disinvestment, charges, rebalancing) but does not address or contradict the implementation timeline or responsibility allocation stated in the report. The guard permits naming the adviser firm rather than an individual to satisfy 'by whom'; the report satisfies this at the firm level ('Adviser'). The absence of a specific implementation timescale for the three recommendations is not contradicted by the evidence but is not supported by it either. Where timing is material—as it is here, given the out-of-market risk disclosure and the coordination of a switch, new plan, and transfer—the lack of explicit sequencing and completion dates represents a gap. The generic assignment to 'Adviser' without specificity about firm, contact, or implementation method leaves execution responsibility unclear. The report's statement about the Expression of Wish form being posted out is client-specific but stands alone without integration into an overall implementation plan.

      - The report asserts the client has a will (hasWill: true) but the Fact Find estate planning section is blank and contains no evidence of a will.
      - The report asserts the will is not up to date (isWillUpToDate: false) but provides no basis in the file for this assessment.
      - The report asserts the client has Power of Attorney granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: true) but the Fact Find estate planning section is blank and contains no evidence of a Power of Attorney.
      - The Expression of Wish recommendation is stated as an adviser action but no follow-up record, confirmation letter, or implementation plan is present in the file to corroborate delivery.

The Expression of Wish recommendation is client-specific and carries an owner: the adviser will post the form and the client will sign and return it. First, the canonical model records hasWill: true and isWillUpToDate: false, but the Fact Find document shows the Estate Planning section entirely blank — there is no evidence the will status was ever discussed or documented. Second, isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: true is recorded for both clients, but again the Fact Find shows no response in the relevant section and no Power of Attorney documentation appears in the file. The guard states 'An adviser recommending the client set up a Power of Attorney is a different claim from the client already having one', but here the report asserts the client already has one, not that the adviser is recommending it be set up. The absence of supporting evidence in the file for these assertions is material, particularly given that estate planning is explicitly listed as an advice area (P8) but left blank. The beneficiary nomination recommendation itself is properly owned and justified (Expression of Wish to be signed and returned), and that action is internally consistent. The defect lies in the unsupported assertions about will status and Power of Attorney that appear in the canonical model but are not corroborated by the Fact Find or any other file document.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report states recommended pension fund is 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' at 0.41% annual charge [P16], but no illustration or product document in the case evidences this 0.41% figure or confirms this fund is the recommended pension investment. Report states combined pension charges total 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund) [P1], but the personal pension illustration [P22] and pension switch report [P11] show only 0.21% platform charge with no corresponding fund charge stated at 0.41%. Report references 'CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc' in one context [P18] with 0.29% charge, but separately recommends 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' at 0.41% — two different funds with materially different charges and no reconciliation of which is the actual recommended pension fund. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses: (1) Platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% under TFAS agency [P1]; (2) Investment fund charge of 0.41% for Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (recommended pension) [P16]; (3) Combined total ongoing charge of 0.62% for the pension recommendation [P1]; (4) Initial adviser fee of £4,794.37 disclosed [P21]; (5) Ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% per annum [P21]; (6) Transaction costs explicitly excluded with reasoning that they are variable and already accounted for in fund performance figures net of all charges [P1]; (7) Cumulative effect of charges shown in personal illustrations [P2]; (8) Aggregated total disclosed as approximately 1.62% including the 1% adviser fee [P1]. The report states the small 0.08% charge increase is justified by improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight [P1].
    File:     Evidence from supporting documents shows: (1) Existing platform charge confirmed as 0.32% in switch charges table [P1]; (2) Existing fund charges (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%) at 0.22% weighted average [P6]; (3) Recommended pension fund (CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc) charged at 0.29% in GIA illustration [P21] but the report states Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 0.41% [P16]; (4) New platform charge under TFAS confirmed as 0.21% [P11]; (5) Personal illustrations provided [P17, P21, P22] showing charges deducted; (6) Adviser charges for GIA confirmed as initial £4,794.37 and ongoing 1.00% [P21]; (7) Pension adviser charges show initial charge and ongoing 1.00% [P22]; (8) Transaction costs excluded from standard charge disclosures as per MiFID II scope [P9]; (9) No illustration or supporting document explicitly states a 0.41% charge for the recommended pension fund; the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet [P23] does not specify a charge percentage.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states recommended pension fund is 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' at 0.41% annual charge [P16], but no illustration or product document in the case evidences this 0.41% figure or confirms this fund is the recommended pension investment.
      - Report states combined pension charges total 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund) [P1], but the personal pension illustration [P22] and pension switch report [P11] show only 0.21% platform charge with no corresponding fund charge stated at 0.41%.
      - Report references 'CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc' in one context [P18] with 0.29% charge, but separately recommends 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' at 0.41% — two different funds with materially different charges and no reconciliation of which is the actual recommended pension fund.
      - Derived figures from the canonical model indicate the 0.62% total ongoing charge applied to £82,691.94 produces £512.69 annually, but this fund value matches neither the existing plan value (£79,074.07) nor any stated arrangement value in the evidence.
      - The switch charges table [P1] shows 'Like for Like' comparison stating the recommended fund is '0.08% more expensive' implying 0.62% (0.54% + 0.08%), but the basis and whether this includes fund or platform alone is not clearly separated.
    The report discloses platform, fund, and adviser charges with stated totals and exclusions. However, a material inconsistency exists regarding the recommended pension fund identity and its charge. The report's canonical model asserts the recommended pension fund is 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' at 0.41%, but no document in the case file evidences this fund or this charge rate. In contrast, evidence suggests the pension illustration shows CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc at 0.29% [P21], which the report also references separately [P18]. This creates ambiguity about which fund is actually recommended for the pension switch. The report's statement that total pension charges are 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund) cannot be corroborated; the pension illustrations provided [P22] show only platform charge of 0.21% without a matching 0.41% fund charge. The derived figure calculation (0.62% applied to £82,691.94 = £512.69) produces a fund value that does not reconcile to any stated arrangement value. The switch charges table (Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment) [P1] presents the charge increase as '+0.08%' on current 0.54%, which mathematically equates to 0.62%, but the decomposition into platform (0.21%) and fund (0.41%) is not explicitly validated by the illustrations. The report correctly excludes transaction costs with stated reasoning [P1, P9], which is an appropriate disclosure. Adviser charges (initial £4,794.37 and 1% ongoing) are disclosed consistently across the pension and GIA illustrations [P21, P22]. The cumulative effect is shown in illustrations [P17, P21, P22]. However, without evidence confirming that the 0.41% fund charge applies to the recommended pension arrangement, the report's total charge assertion of 0.62% and the claim that this 'small increase' is justified by risk alignment cannot be fully evaluated for consistency with the case file evidence. The vulnerability overlay (not detailed in the provided evidence pack) is noted but does not override this finding: the charge disclosure itself must be clear and consistent regardless of client characteristics.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Report states initial adviser fee of 3% of amount invested, totalling £4,794 based on £79,092 pension transfer + £57,120 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 pension contribution. Report states ongoing adviser fee of 1% per annum on Aviva Platform investments, approximately £1,558 for the upcoming year based on total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction.
    File:     Client Agreement (P19) states: Initial Service charge of 3% of the value of the funds advised on with minimum £1,350, based on funds value the fee will be £4,794. Ongoing Services charge of 1% annual review management charge (subject to minimum £100), automatically taken from funds invested. TFAS fee schedule (P1) specifies 3% of funds value for investments & pensions advice with £1,350 minimum. Ongoing fee is 1% per annum. Client Agreement (P20) shows ongoing charge examples at 1% with fluctuation based on investment value.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report calculates initial fee as £4,794 across four separate buckets (pension transfer £79,092, GIA £57,120, ISA £20,000, pension contribution £3,600), but neither the Client Agreement nor TFAS fee schedule documents these individual valuations or explicitly confirm how the 3% is applied across multiple accounts/products.
      - Report states ongoing fee amount of £1,558 based on platform value of £155,812, but this platform value figure does not appear in any supporting illustration or document in the file. The derived figure calculation shows £1,558 at 1% implies £155,800 fund value, which matches no stated arrangement value.
      - Report basis for initial fee deduction states it will be 'paid from the GIA funds to ensure maximum amount is invested tax-efficiently in your pension and ISA', but the Client Agreement and fee schedule do not specify or restrict from which account the fee is deducted.
      - Discrepancy between how initial fee is calculated: Report shows it as 3% of total investments across four separate pots. Client Agreement states 3% of 'value of funds that we advise on' as a single basis, with £4,794 as the outcome, but does not break down the calculation method across multiple products/accounts.
    The Client Agreement on file (P19) confirms the fee structure: Initial Service at 3% with minimum £1,350, resulting in £4,794; Ongoing Services at 1% per annum with minimum £100. The TFAS fee schedule (P1) corroborates these percentages. The initial percentage (3%) matches exactly [P19]. The ongoing percentage (1%) matches exactly [P19]. However, material discrepancies arise on application and basis: (1) The report breaks the initial fee calculation into four separate investment pots totalling £159,812, but the Client Agreement does not itemise or confirm these individual valuations. The individual figures (£79,092 pension, £57,120 GIA, £20,000 ISA, £3,600 contribution) do not appear verified in any other document. (2) The ongoing fee amount of £1,558 is stated by the report as 'based on total platform value of £155,812', but this specific platform value does not appear in any Aviva illustration or evidence document provided. The Pension Illustration [P5], GIA Illustration [P4], and ISA Illustration [P16] do not state these aggregated figures. (3) The report states the initial fee 'will be paid from the GIA funds', but the Client Agreement [P19] states fees become 'payable when you agree upon advice' and 'settled by from your pension/investment' without specifying GIA as the source. (4) The ongoing fee is stated to apply to 'all your Aviva Platform investments', and Client Agreement [P19] confirms 1% on investment funds, but does not define which accounts are included in 'Aviva Platform' scope. The fee percentages themselves are consistent with the Client Agreement; the calculations and their bases are not fully evidenced. The report's itemisation of initial investment across four buckets totalling £159,812 is not corroborated by any provider illustration or agreement. The platform valuation of £155,812 used to derive the £1,558 ongoing fee is absent from the file.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures for both existing and recommended arrangements across three growth rate bases (Low, Mid, High) at age 60. For Low growth: Existing RIY 1.6%, Recommended RIY 1.6%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0%. For Mid growth: Existing RIY 1.7%, Recommended RIY 1.7%, critical yield 2.9%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0%. For High growth: Existing RIY 1.8%, Recommended RIY appears to follow (figures truncated in canonical model). The report states an annual charge difference of 0.08% and shows existing annual recurring charge of 0.54% versus recommended platform charge of 0.21%, with fund charges increasing from 0.22% to 0.41%.
    File:     The Pension Switch Report [P20] shows at mid growth rate (2.94%): Existing pension RIY 0.6%, New solution RIY 0.7%, Critical yield 2.9%, Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease (not £0). At high growth rate (5.88%): Existing RIY 0.7%, New solution RIY 0.7%, Critical yield 5.9%, Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease. The Aviva Pension Illustration [P11] shows product and investment charges reduce the fund from £91,500 to £89,200 (0.6% reduction in growth rate) and all charges reduce it from £91,500 to £84,800 (1.9% reduction in growth rate). The GIA Illustration [P8] shows charges impact but uses different metrics.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Low growth RIY for Existing as 1.6%; Pension Switch Report [P20] shows at mid growth 0.6% and high growth 0.7%, with no low growth scenario presented.
      - Report asserts Mid growth RIY for Existing as 1.7%; Pension Switch Report [P20] shows 0.6% at mid growth (2.94%), a material difference of 1.1 percentage points.
      - Report asserts Mid growth RIY for Recommended as 1.7%; Pension Switch Report [P20] shows 0.7%, a difference of 1.0 percentage point.
      - Report asserts High growth RIY for Existing as 1.8%; Pension Switch Report [P20] shows 0.7% at high growth (5.88%), a difference of 1.1 percentage points.
      - Report asserts Effect of switching at Mid growth as £0 or 0.0% decrease; Pension Switch Report [P20] shows £100 or 0.1% decrease, a material discrepancy.
      - Report asserts Effect of switching at High growth as £0 or 0.0% decrease (inferred); Pension Switch Report [P20] shows £100 or 0.1% decrease.
      - Report extract shows annual charge difference of £63.00 at 0.08% implies £78,750 fund value; computed figure 'Solution total ongoing: £512.69 at 0.62% implies £82,691.94' does not match any stated arrangement value, suggesting arithmetic misalignment.
      - Report states platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%, but total charge increase of 0.08% is stated, yet fund charges increase from 0.22% to 0.41% (0.19% increase); the net of -0.11% platform reduction and +0.19% fund increase should equal +0.08%, which arithmetically checks, but the summary presentation obscures the fund charge deterioration.
    The canonical model extraction shows RIY figures that do not appear in the supporting documents. The Pension Switch Report [P20], which is the authoritative switching analysis, presents significantly different RIY values: 0.6% and 0.7% for the mid and high growth scenarios, not 1.6%, 1.7%, or 1.8% as in the report extraction. The report's figures appear to conflate or incorrectly state RIY across different growth scenarios. The Effect of Switching is stated in the report as £0 or 0.0% decrease, but [P20] clearly shows £100 or 0.1% decrease at both mid and high growth rates. This is material because the report claims no switching cost, when there is a modest switching cost of £100 (0.1%). The guard regarding illustration growth rates is noted but does not explain these discrepancies: the Pension Switch Report is also a provider illustration and should align with the suitability report's statements about cost impact. The charge arithmetic shows internal inconsistency: the derived figures compute a solution total of £512.69 at 0.62%, which does not reconcile with stated fund values. The platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%) and fund charge increase (0.22% to 0.41%) are correctly netted at +0.08%, but the cumulative impact disclosure is incomplete because the report does not clearly state that while platform charges fall, fund charges rise materially, offsetting the platform saving. The report does not present an aggregated all-in cost comparison or a clear statement of cumulative impact over time. The RIY figures in the report are unexplained and inconsistent with the only switching analysis document that presents them.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts seven benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower costs - platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) Risk alignment - current funds at risk level 6 versus agreed Cautious Balanced level 4; (3) Professional oversight - ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Adviser charging facilitation - direct platform deduction of adviser charges; (5) Tax-efficient contribution - £3,600 gross with 20% tax relief; (6) Platform integration - pension, ISA and GIA on same Aviva platform; (7) Platform quality - Aviva Platform described as excellent. The report also states: 'The small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' It claims the recommended plan with a 2.94% critical yield is achievable 'based on past performance of recommended funds' and that cumulative costs are lower over time despite the stated 0.08% increase in annual recurring charges.
    File:     Evidence from multiple sources shows: (1) Existing platform charge 0.32% plus weighted fund charges 0.22% (total 0.54%) per the switch analysis [P1]. (2) Recommended Aviva Platform pension charge 0.21% [charge arithmetic derived from report data]. (3) Recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) charges 0.41% [P12: '0.30% AMC and 0.11% Additional Expenses']. (4) Net change stated as +0.08% increase in annual recurring charges in switch analysis. (5) Existing funds are Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity and 80% Equity [P2, P5, P9]; combined performance shows 6.03%-7.36% (3m), 13.74%-17.61% (6m), 13.86%-17.03% (1y), 35.92%-45.41% (3y), 42.45%-66.42% (5y) [P2, P9]. (6) Recommended fund performance shows 5.58% (3m), 12.61% (6m), 11.92% (1y), 33.59% (3y), 36.21% (5y) [P11, P12]. (7) Client risk profile is 'Cautious Balanced' (level 4) [P8]. (8) Fact Find [P14] shows monthly expenditure £2,206.67 and no expected changes. (9) Professional advice fee amount not specified in evidence. (10) Tax relief of 20% is standard relief, not a recommendation-specific benefit. (11) Integration benefit is convenience, not financial value.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' as primary benefit but states platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21% (0.11% saving) while fund charges increase from 0.22% to 0.41% (0.19% increase), yielding net 0.08% cost increase, not a reduction
      - Report states 'small increase in charges' but does not quantify it; switch analysis [P1] shows annual charge difference of £63 at 0.08%, implying fund value of £78,750
      - Report asserts critical yield of 2.94% is 'achievable based on past performance of recommended funds' but provides no specific performance comparison between existing funds and recommended fund
      - Existing funds show 3-year performance of 35.92%-45.41% [P9] versus recommended fund 33.59% [P12]; 5-year shows 42.45%-66.42% [P9] versus 36.21% [P12]; recommended fund underperforms on both periods
      - Report states risk alignment benefit but existing funds are described as risk level 6 combined, whereas evidence shows two different Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (60% at implied level ~5, 80% at implied level ~7) not a single level 6 holding
      - Report does not disclose adviser fee amount, frequency or ongoing cost, rendering 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' benefit unquantified and unsupported
      - Report claims 'Professional oversight' as benefit but provides no evidence of service proposition, frequency, scope or deliverables attached to ongoing charges
      - Tax relief and platform integration benefits are general features, not specific justifications for cost increase relative to alternatives
    The report discloses platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%) but this is contradicted by fund charge increase (0.22% to 0.41%), resulting in net 0.08% cost increase annually. The report acknowledges the increase exists but frames it as 'small' and 'justified' without quantifying either the cost or the benefits. Seven benefits are listed, but most are either unsupported by evidence or not specific to the recommendation: Tax relief is standard, platform integration is convenience not value, professional oversight lacks service detail, and adviser fees are not disclosed. The critical yield claim of 2.94% being 'achievable based on past performance' lacks supporting evidence; the recommended Aviva fund shows materially lower 3-year (33.59% vs 35.92%-45.41%) and 5-year (36.21% vs 42.45%-66.42%) performance than the existing Vanguard funds it replaces [P12 vs P9]. Risk alignment is stated but the existing risk level is unclear (report calls it level 6, but evidence shows a mix of 60% and 80% equity Vanguard funds). The cost-benefit narrative is internally inconsistent: the report claims lower costs as the primary benefit while simultaneously asserting a cost increase. No cumulative cost analysis or breakeven calculation is provided to demonstrate that the 0.08% annual increase is recovered by the stated benefits within any defined timeframe. The guard in the decision logic requires supporting performance data before accepting 'achievable' claims; none is provided. Where the report claims cost increase is justified, PRIN 2A.4 requires that benefit to be demonstrated; instead, the benefits listed are either generic (tax relief, platform integration), unquantified (professional oversight, adviser fees), or contradicted by evidence (risk alignment, fund performance).

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states that the recommended plan will need to outperform the current plan by approximately 1.08% annually to match the same net growth, and that the annual charge difference is +0.08% of fund value per annum. This is presented on page 20 as a trade-off: the client is paying 0.08% more in charges annually, but this cost increase is justified by the expectation that the recommended plan will deliver sufficient additional performance to compensate.
    File:     No supporting documents or passages were retrieved to evidence the basis for these figures. The requirement assesses the report against itself — whether the stated outperformance requirement is consistent with and supported by the stated cost difference. The case file contains no independent corroboration of the 1.08% outperformance figure, the 0.08% charge difference, or the benefits expected to justify the cost increase.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states a charge difference of +0.08% but the internal inconsistencies noted in the extraction metadata show the charge calculation is complex: platform charge falls from 0.32% to 0.21% (a saving of 0.11%), but fund charges rise from 0.22% to 0.41% (an increase of 0.19%), netting to +0.08%. However, the derived figures show that applying 0.08% to the stated baseline fund value of £78,750 yields an annual charge difference of £63.00, which arithmetically checks out against the 0.54% existing charge yielding £427.00.
      - The required outperformance of 1.08% annually exceeds the stated charge difference of 0.08% by 1.00 percentage point. No explanation in the report accounts for this gap or justifies why outperformance must be 13.5 times the charge increase to break even.
    The report presents two figures: a cost increase of 0.08% and a required outperformance of 1.08%. These figures do not reconcile. If the only cost increase were the 0.08% charge difference, the required outperformance to break even should be approximately 0.08%, not 1.08%. The 1.00 percentage point gap (1.08% − 0.08% = 1.00%) is unexplained. The report does not disclose what additional cost or benefit factor drives this requirement. Possible explanations — such as adviser fee deductions, transfer costs, or tax treatment — are not addressed in the extracted suitability report material. The report's assertion that the client must achieve 1.08% annual outperformance to match net growth is presented without clear justification for the excess over the stated charge difference. This is internally inconsistent: the report claims value justifies the cost but does not show how the required outperformance follows from the cost increase. The extraction metadata notes internal inconsistencies in charge calculations across the document, which may explain the discrepancy but those inconsistencies themselves undermine confidence in the value assessment.
      · Suitability Report - Barnes.md, page 20 [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice]
        "This means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by approximately 1.08% annually to match the same net growth."
      · Suitability Report - Barnes.md, page 20 [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice]
        "+0.08%"

The report states 'The available solutions were ranked by Maturity Value along with a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension. The top 10 results are shown below' (P1, truncated).     The requirement asks whether the suitability report discloses material charges and, where higher costs arise, explains the benefits expected in exchange. The first limb—disclosing material charges—is addressed: the report states platform charges of 0.32% existing and 0.21% recommended, and fund charges are mentioned. However, the second limb—demonstrating that alternatives provide better cost or benefit—cannot be assessed because the comparative cost data for alternatives is absent from the case file. The pension switch report references a methodology ('ranked by Maturity Value along with a generic stakeholder pension') and states 'the top 10 results are shown below' but the table itself is truncated or not retrieved. The suitability report's citation of 'Lower Costs' as a benefit is only relative to the existing arrangement, not relative to alternatives. The recommendation is to move from risk level 6 to risk level 4 within the same platform (Aviva) with a platform charge reduction and fund change; this is presented as an internal portfolio adjustment rather than a switch to a superior external alternative. Without access to the comparative analysis that the report claims to have performed, the extent to which this recommendation provides better cost or benefit relative to alternatives cannot be verified. The evidence is incomplete: either the comparison was not documented, or the document containing it was not retrieved. Per the instruction on missing inputs, where the report claims alternatives were compared and ranked but the comparison data is absent, the claim is unsupported.
