  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts RIY figures at three growth rate bases (Low, Mid, High) for both Existing and Recommended arrangements at age 60. Low basis: Existing 1.6%, Recommended 1.6%, Critical Yield 0.0%. Mid basis: Existing 1.7%, Recommended 1.7%, Critical Yield 2.9%. High basis: Existing 1.8%, Recommended 1.7%, Critical Yield 5.9%. Effect of switching is stated as £0 or 0.0% decrease across all scenarios.
    File:     The Pension switch report [P3] and [P4] present the same RIY and Critical Yield figures and confirms them: Low basis at £74,000 projected value with RIY 1.6% existing and recommended, Critical Yield 0.0%. Mid basis at £83,300 with RIY 1.7% both, Critical Yield 2.9%. High basis at £93,400 with RIY 1.8% existing, 1.7% recommended, Critical Yield 5.9%. The switch report [P3] also shows a comparison table with RIY 0.6% existing and 0.7% recommended at an unstated (implied low) growth rate, and Effect of switching as £100 or 0.1% decrease—figures that differ materially from the suitability report's Low basis disclosure.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Pension switch report [P3] Low growth rate comparison shows RIY 0.6% for Existing (report states 1.6%) and RIY 0.7% for Recommended (report states 1.6%), and Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease (report states £0 or 0.0% decrease)
      - The Pension switch report [P3] High growth rate comparison shows RIY 1.7% for Existing (report states 1.8%) and RIY 1.7% for Recommended (consistent with report), with Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease (report states £0 or 0.0% decrease)
      - The suitability report presents RIY figures without explicit growth rate basis labels in the canonical extraction narrative, but [P4] confirms three distinct growth scenarios; the Low scenario in [P3] comparison table contradicts the report's stated Low RIY of 1.6% by showing 0.6%
    The suitability report and the Pension switch report [P3] and [P4] present conflicting RIY figures within the Low and High growth rate scenarios. The switch report [P3] contains two distinct comparison tables: one showing Low growth with RIY 0.6%/0.7% and another showing High growth with RIY 1.7%/1.7%. The report's canonical model extraction shows RIY 1.6% for Low and 1.8% for Existing High, but the switch report's Low comparison shows 0.6% existing. The switch report's High comparison table shows 1.8% existing RIY but also states RIY 1.7% in a different section of the same table. The Effect of switching is consistently stated in [P3] as £100 or 0.1% decrease (Low and High scenarios), contradicting the report's assertion of £0 or 0.0% decrease. The Mid growth scenario (RIY 1.7% both, Critical Yield 2.9%, Effect £0 or 0.0% decrease) is consistently supported by [P4]. The discrepancy in the Low and High scenarios between the switch report's headline comparison table and the detailed projection sections, combined with the Effect of switching contradiction, indicates internal inconsistency within the supporting evidence itself. The guard permits comparison of RIY at the same growth basis; these are the same bases (Low, Mid, High), so the guard does not explain the differences. The report's figures at Mid basis are corroborated by [P4]; the Low and High basis figures in the switch report [P3] do not match the report's assertions. This constitutes a material data inconsistency: the RIY figures the report relies upon to support its recommendation are contradicted by the switch analysis document itself.

        "Reduction in yield [Low growth rate] 0.6% [Existing] 0.7% [New solution]; Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease; Reduction in yield [High growth rate] 0.7% [Existing] 0.7% [Recommended]; Critical yield 5.9%; Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease"

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an ongoing adviser charge of 1.0% of Aviva Platform investments, reviewed annually. The services included for this ongoing charge are: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure investment portfolio remains suitable; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs; (6) Professional oversight and investment monitoring process to ensure the fund remains suitable; (7) Investment committee oversight of the CIP. The report states these services are provided and quotes them from page 19 and pages 11 and 30 of the suitability report.
    File:     The Client Agreement To Proceed (P4, P12) specifies: 'Ongoing Services - For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' The agreement states (P4) that ongoing advice and review services are 'as described in the Important Information about our Services document' until the service is no longer appropriate. The IMPORT_2.md (P2) document lists ongoing services as including: (1) Review meeting at least once a year; (2) Recommendations which may include: market updates, changes in financial legislation, monitoring and reviewing of plans, ad-hoc advice, and potentially other services 'as part of' the annual review. The agreement (P4) confirms fees are 'set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation' and that clients must 'provide confirmation of your acceptance by approving them expressly or by written consent.' The IMPORT_2.md (P1, P2) describes the service framework but does not itemise a definitive list matching the suitability report's seven specific service components.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report lists seven distinct service components for the 1% ongoing charge; the Client Agreement (P12) states '1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100)' but does not enumerate the specific services attached to this charge, instead referring to 'Important Information about our Services document' which (P2) provides a less granular description
      - The suitability report describes 'Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required' as part of ongoing services; the Client Agreement does not explicitly commit to 'at any time' access, stating instead 'review meeting at least once a year' and mentioning ad-hoc advice in general terms without the same level of commitment (P2, P4)
      - The suitability report claims 'Our CIP is actively monitored and reviewed by an experienced investment committee' as a service benefit of the ongoing charge (page 30 per the report extraction); neither the Client Agreement nor IMPORT_2.md explicitly attaches investment committee oversight as a discrete ongoing service component for which the client pays the 1% charge
      - The suitability report states services are reviewed 'Annually' in a discrete field; the Client Agreement (P2, P4) specifies 'at least annually' which is technically consistent but the extraction treats this as a separate data element suggesting potential misalignment in how frequency is recorded across documents
    The comparison can be performed because both the report and the client agreement address the ongoing 1% charge and its service components. However, there are material differences in specificity and completeness. The suitability report provides seven granular service commitments extracted from pages 11, 19, and 30 of the report itself, whereas the Client Agreement refers back to the suitability report for fee detail (P4: 'fees...are set out in the suitability report') and to IMPORT_2.md for service scope, creating a fragmented disclosure. The IMPORT_2.md document (P2) provides a generic framework ('Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include...') rather than the specific commitments listed in the report. Most critically, the report asserts access to advisers 'at any time' and investment committee oversight as part of the ongoing service proposition, but the Client Agreement does not commit to 'at any time' availability (P2 states 'review meeting at least once a year' and ad-hoc advice availability is not stated with the same absoluteness) and does not mention investment committee oversight at all. While the fee percentage (1%) and minimum (£100) match between report (extracted canonical model shows 1.0% of Aviva Platform investments) and Client Agreement (P12: '1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100)'), the description of what that fee purchases is inconsistent in scope and detail. The Client Agreement (P4) places the onus on the client to confirm acceptance of 'fees...set out in the suitability report' which suggests the report is the primary specification, but the report's scope of service commitments exceeds what is independently confirmed in the Client Agreement or supporting service literature. This creates a disclosure gap: a client reviewing the Client Agreement alone would not have the same service expectations as a client reviewing the suitability report alone, and the Client Agreement does not independently corroborate each of the seven service elements the report describes. The reference structure (suitability report → Client Agreement → IMPORT_2.md) obscures rather than clarifies the service proposition. The guard 'An ongoing charge with no ongoing service is a finding even where the client agreement is silent — silence in both places is worse, not better' applies: here there is service description but it is fragmented across documents and some commitments (at-any-time access, investment committee oversight) are described in the report but not independently reflected in the Client Agreement or referenced service literature.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states six key benefits to justify the recommendation despite higher costs: (1) 'Lower costs - moving to TFAS agency terms will reduce platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%'; (2) 'Risk alignment - current funds have risk level 6, too high for agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4; switching to recommended fund will ensure investments match risk tolerance'; (3) 'Professional oversight - access to ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review services'; (4) 'Adviser charging facilitation - platform allows adviser charges to be deducted directly'; (5) 'Tax-efficient contribution - £3,600 gross contribution will utilise annual allowance and benefit from 20% tax relief'; (6) 'Platform integration - keeping pension, ISA and GIA on same Aviva Platform will provide administrative convenience'. The report also states: 'this small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' and 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 2.94% per year up to age 60 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I believe this is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight.'
    File:     Evidence from the case file supports some benefits but reveals internal inconsistencies about the actual cost change: (P21) The report's own cumulative performance table shows the current strategy (A) returning 45.28% over 5 years vs the recommended fund (C) returning 30.12%, contradicting the assertion that recommended funds demonstrate competitive performance. (P22) The existing arrangement details show current holdings are Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%) and 80% Equity (64.95%) with risk level 6, confirming the risk alignment claim. (P21) Volatility analysis confirms the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II provides lower volatility aligned with Risk Level 4 profile. (P23) The report confirms ongoing services include 'Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable for you' and 'Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio', supporting professional oversight claim. (P9-P11) Key Features documents confirm the Aviva Platform offers comprehensive investment choices and facilities. However, the charge comparison contains arithmetic issues: the report claims the platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21% (an 11 basis point saving), yet the total ongoing charge is stated to increase by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), meaning fund charges increase by approximately 19 basis points, offsetting the platform saving and resulting in net higher costs. (P12) Aviva scheme details confirm no market value adjustment or early transfer charges, and over 3,000 funds available. The report does not evidence that the client will use the adviser charging facilitation benefit or that this justifies costs. The tax relief benefit (5%) is generic tax law, not a benefit of the recommendation itself. Administrative convenience from consolidation is noted but is not a financial benefit.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' with platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%, but total charges increase by 0.08% annually (0.54% to 0.62%), indicating fund charges rise to offset platform savings
      - Report states recommended funds 'demonstrate lower volatility than current mix' and are 'competitive' in performance, but the report's own 5-year cumulative performance table shows current strategy returned 45.28% vs recommended fund 30.12%
      - Report justifies recommendation by improved risk alignment and professional oversight, but the incremental cost (0.08% = £63 annually on £78,750 valuation) is not quantified against the magnitude of these benefits
      - Report claims advisory charging facilitation as a benefit but provides no evidence the client uses or requires this functionality
      - Report lists tax relief on £3,600 contribution as a benefit justifying the recommendation, but tax relief is a statutory entitlement independent of the product chosen
    The comparison can be performed. The report asserts multiple benefits to justify costs, and the evidence pack contains sufficient information to evaluate them. Analysis by benefit: (1) LOWER COSTS - The report's own charge tables show platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%, but the net annual cost increases by 0.08% (£63) due to fund charge increases. This is internally inconsistent: the benefit of lower platform costs is negated by higher fund costs, yet the benefit is listed first and most prominently. The stated benefit is partially false—while platform charges do reduce, the overall recommendation is not cheaper. (2) RISK ALIGNMENT - Supported. The evidence confirms current funds carry risk level 6 (Vanguard 60%/80% mix) vs target level 4, and the recommended fund provides appropriate alignment with documented lower volatility. This is a genuine benefit. (3) PROFESSIONAL OVERSIGHT - Supported in principle. The report describes ongoing services (P23), and the evidence confirms these are available. However, these ongoing services are funded by the 1% ongoing fee, not by the product switch itself; this benefit was available before the switch. (4) ADVISER CHARGING FACILITATION - Not evidenced. The file contains no indication the client requires or uses platform-based adviser charging, nor that this functionality is unavailable on the existing platform. (5) TAX RELIEF - Not a benefit of this recommendation. Tax relief on pension contributions is statutory and applies regardless of provider. (6) PLATFORM INTEGRATION - Supported but not a financial benefit. The file confirms multiple Aviva products (P22-P23), and consolidation provides administrative convenience, but this is explicitly a non-financial benefit (per the guard). The critical issue is that the report claims a cost benefit ('Lower Costs') that is contradicted by the arithmetic. The 0.08% net increase in annual charges means the recommendation is more expensive, not cheaper. The claimed justifications (risk alignment and professional oversight) may be valid, but they are not supported by a cost saving, and the professional oversight benefit was already available. The guard states that 'A benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify the extra cost.' The existing Aviva platform already provided adviser access and professional oversight capability (P22 shows 'Allows Servicing Adviser: Yes'). The recommendation's incremental cost must therefore be justified by benefits not previously available (risk alignment is the substantive new benefit), but the report presents it as justified by professional oversight services that were already accessible.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended solution is more expensive than the existing arrangement by +0.08% pa, and states that 'your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by approximately 1.08% annually to match the same net growth.' The report projects values at age 60 under three growth scenarios (Low 0%, Mid 2.94%, High 5.88%) and provides Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures ranging from 1.6% to 1.8% for the new solution, with Critical Yield thresholds of 0.0%, 2.9% and 5.9%.
    File:     The evidence (P2, P7) contains a detailed switching comparison table showing both existing and new solution projections. At Mid growth (2.94%), the existing pension projects to £86,800 versus the new solution at £86,700—a £100 or 0.1% decrease. At High growth (5.88%), existing projects to £97,400 versus new solution at £97,300—also a £100 or 0.1% decrease. The Low growth scenario (0%) shows both projections at £74,000 with no effect. The RIY figures in evidence match the report (1.6%, 1.7%, 1.8% for new solution) and Critical Yield matches (0.0%, 2.9%, 5.9%). The evidence table explicitly states 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease at Low, £100 or 0.1% decrease at Mid, and £100 or 0.1% decrease at High.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the charge difference is +0.08%, but the arithmetic check shows this figure does not reconcile: the 0.08% difference stated on page 20 implies a fund value of £78,750, yet the fund value is £79,092.32 (current) and £82,691.94 (recommended), neither matching that implied value.
      - The report claims required outperformance of 1.08% annually, but the evidence shows projected value decreases of only £100 (0.1%) at both Mid and High growth rates, not requiring 1.08% outperformance to match outcomes.
      - The RIY figures for the new solution (1.6%, 1.7%, 1.8%) match the evidence, but these are marginally lower than or equal to the existing pension's RIY at High growth (0.7% vs 0.7%), contradicting the assertion of materially higher charges.
    The comparison can be performed because both projections and charge data are present. The key issue is an internal contradiction in the report's own cost assertion. The report states +0.08% higher charges requiring 1.08% annual outperformance, yet its own switching comparison table (evidence P2, P7) shows projected values diverge by only £100 (0.1%) across the two material growth scenarios. This is not consistent with a 1.08% outperformance requirement. The guard permits findings where a lower projection is driven by a lower prescribed growth rate to be normalized, but both sides use identical growth assumptions (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%). The RIY analysis further reveals that at the High growth scenario, both arrangements show 0.7% RIY, suggesting the charge increase does not translate to materially higher yield reduction. The report's assertion of an 0.08% charge increase cannot be corroborated by the arithmetic: the derived fund values do not support this percentage when applied to actual fund values in the case. The required outperformance of 1.08% is mathematically inconsistent with projected outcomes that show minimal divergence (£100 on funds of ~£97,000 at high growth = 0.1% effect, not 1.08%). This suggests either the charge comparison is misstated or the projections do not reflect the stated cost structure. The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this finding: while the client has ambiguous employment income and inheritance timing, neither of these impacts the cost-to-benefit analysis of the switch itself.

      [G7.4] No Issue
        - Report states existing combined charge of 0.54% (0.32% platform + 0.22% fund charges), but P17 shows new platform charge of 0.21% is described as a reduction 'from 0.32%' and P2/P15 show recommended fund charge of 0.41% for Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus, which combined would be 0.62% (0.21% + 0.41%), yet report claims only +0.08% increase over 0.54% existing, which would be 0.62% — this is internally consistent mathematically. However, the composition has changed: existing is 0.32% platform + 0.22% fund = 0.54%; recommended is 0.21% platform + 0.41% fund = 0.62%. The 0.08% increase is correctly derived.
        - The stated £427 annual charge on existing £79,074 fund value (0.54%) is arithmetically sound.
        - The stated £63 incremental cost represents 0.08% of £78,750 — this is arithmetically sound but uses different fund value basis than existing charge calculation (£79,074 vs £78,750). Derived figures note this discrepancy: £63 at 0.08% implies fund value of £78,750, which 'matches no arrangement value and neither total.'
        - Adviser fee stated separately: 1% on £155,812 total platform value stated as approximately £1,558 for upcoming year in P18, which is arithmetically sound (1% × £155,812 ≈ £1,558).
        - Report's 'Like for Like' comparison excludes adviser fees; 'Including Adviser Fee' section adds them separately, creating two bases for comparison which is permissible under the guard.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report states only generic deferred disclosure ('All other risks...in the product literature') without specifying material product-specific risks identified in Aviva literature. Report does not disclose income drawdown depletion risk that arises where 'income you take will deplete your pension fund. Especially if you choose to take a high level of income' [P4]. Report does not disclose that pension fund value 'can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in' [P1, P3] — a fundamental investment risk material to pension transfer. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report identifies three material disadvantages and limitations: (1) cancellation risk where 'the cancellation notice includes a provision that any previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back' [page 20]; (2) access limitation stating 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' [page 12]; (3) market timing risk during transaction, noting 'Whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market' and 'If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' [page 9]; and (4) deferred disclosure stating 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you. You should read this document carefully' [page 12].
    File:     Product literature identifies multiple material disadvantages and risks: Aviva Pension Key Features [P3, P4, P14] disclose that 'the value of your pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in,' property investment delays, potential inability to access funds during suspension periods, tax treatment changes, income drawdown depletion risks, and that 'If you withdraw high levels of income, there'll be less money to provide for dependants or to buy a lifetime annuity in the future.' Aviva GIA Key Features [P7, P10] state potential losses on structured products, that investments 'may get back significantly less than you initially invested,' and various risks from overseas investments and smaller companies. The factsheet [P5, P6] identifies foreign exchange risk, emerging markets volatility, property fund illiquidity, high-yield bond risks, and alternative investment fund liquidity constraints. The research file [P18, P22] shows client expenditure discrepancy (£2,493 stated vs £2,206.67 modelled) and inheritance timing ambiguity. Aviva ISA Key Features [P2, P11] note that as a stocks and shares ISA 'the value can go down as well as up, and may be worth less than has been invested.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states only generic deferred disclosure ('All other risks...in the product literature') without specifying material product-specific risks identified in Aviva literature
      - Report does not disclose income drawdown depletion risk that arises where 'income you take will deplete your pension fund. Especially if you choose to take a high level of income' [P4]
      - Report does not disclose that pension fund value 'can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in' [P1, P3] — a fundamental investment risk material to pension transfer
      - Report does not disclose loss of guarantees or valuable benefits that transferring out of existing arrangement may sacrifice, beyond generic cancellation provision
      - Report does not address tax consequences of the GIA investment or ISA transfer, despite receiving £60k inheritance and mentioning tax efficiency concerns in fact-find [P18]
      - Report does not disclose foreign exchange risk, emerging markets risk, property fund liquidity risks, or alternative investment fund constraints identified in fund factsheet [P5, P6]
      - Report does not disclose potential impact on means-tested benefits from income drawdown [P4]
      - Product literature [P4] states 'If you withdraw high levels of income, there'll be less money to provide for dependants or to buy a lifetime annuity in the future' but report makes no corresponding disclosure about reduced legacy or future income options
      - Expenditure figure inconsistency in file itself: report states £2,493/month current expenditure but fact-find model uses £2,206.67 [P22], a material £287 discrepancy affecting suitability assessment
    The report discloses three specific disadvantages (cancellation risk, access timing, market timing) and defers full risk disclosure to product literature. However, material disadvantages identified in the supporting evidence are not disclosed in the report itself. The regulatory requirement under COBS 9.4.7R demands that material disadvantages be stated fairly, clearly and prominently in the report where the client will see them, not delegated to appendices. A pension transfer carries inherent risks: loss of potential guarantees, depletion of capital through drawdown, means-tested benefit impacts, and tax consequences—all identified in product literature [P1, P3, P4] but absent from the report narrative. The fund factsheet [P5, P6] identifies product-specific risks (FX risk, emerging markets, property illiquidity, alternative investment constraints) not mentioned in the report. The report's statement 'All other risks...detailed in the product literature' fails to explain material risks in language the client will engage with; this delegates risk disclosure rather than presenting it prominently as required. The fact-find [P18, P22] reveals an internal inconsistency on expenditure (£2,493 vs £2,206.67) that affects cash-flow adequacy and thus suitability, yet the report's risk assessment and disadvantage discussion do not reference this discrepancy or its implications. Regarding vulnerability overlay: the fact-find does not flag vulnerability drivers, support needs or communication requirements, so no material modification to this assessment is required on that basis. However, the general underdisclosure of risks heightens concern where a client's capacity for loss is stated as 'Medium-High' but is contingent on a temporary shortfall period [extraction note on /riskAssessment/0/capacityForLoss], and risks are not clearly articulated to enable informed consent.
      · Aviva - Pension - Key Features.md [H] (P1)
        "As a result of the transfer you may not get higher benefits in the new scheme as the growth is defined by future investment returns and these and the retirement income from them can go down as well as up."
      · Aviva - Pension - Key Features.md [H] (P3)
        "Remember that the value of your pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in."
      · Aviva - Pension - Key Features.md [H] (P4)
        "Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund. Especially if you choose to take a high level of income."

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts eight product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (pages 10, 16); (2) 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (pages 9, 12); (3) Market timing risk: 'whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market...If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged insofar as the cost of buying your new fund could be greater. Conversely, if there is a fall in the market between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially advantaged insofar as the cost of buying your new funds could be lower' (page 12); (4) 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' (page 9); (5) Liquidity risk: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 9); (6) Capital risk stated twice: 'Unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it' and 'Unit prices and the value of your investment can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital' (page 10); (7) 'Past performance is not a guarantee of future returns' (page 13).
    File:     The case file contains extensive product literature including KIIDs, factsheets and key features documents for the recommended and existing funds. [P1] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID identifies multiple specific risks including Derivative Risk ('derivative values rise and fall at a greater rate than equities and debt instruments. Losses can be greater than the initial investment'), Fixed Interest Securities Risk ('changes in interest rates can affect the values of fixed interest holdings'), Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, and Liquidity Risk. [P3] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc Factsheet states 'The value of investments and any income derived from them can go down as well as up as a result of market or currency movements' and includes the recommendation 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' [P7] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID contains detailed risk disclosures including that derivative losses 'can be greater than the initial investment' and liquidity risk affecting the ability to 'buy and sell assets at a favourable price'. [P2] and [P4] Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 factsheet lists specific risk warnings (A through F, including General, Foreign Exchange Risk, Emerging Markets, Smaller Companies, Fixed Interest, and Derivatives) with detailed definitions. [P9] Aviva Pension Key Features states 'the value of your pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in. This may not be the case in your previous pension scheme' and notes 'valuable benefits that could be lost' in transfer context. [P6] Aviva ISA Key Features addresses property investment risks, fund suspension/dealing risks, and tax treatment risks. [P14] Pension projection notes 'The growth rates are not guaranteed. They are not minimum or maximum amounts. What you get back will depend on what you make on your actual investments' and 'The amount of annuity you may eventually buy may be adversely affected if the investment returns are low when you purchase your annuity. Annuity rates may be more or less favourable if you choose to buy an annuity in the future.' [P15] Personal Pension Plan review report documents that 'The 10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' No product literature specific to the recommended Aviva funds appears on file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' but the case file does not contain product literature for the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 or other Aviva funds being recommended—only literature for CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc and Aviva platform key features are present.
      - The report does not explicitly disclose the fund-specific risk warnings (A-F) that appear in the Aviva factsheet [P4] (General, Foreign Exchange Risk, Emerging Markets, Smaller Companies, Fixed Interest, Derivatives).
      - The report does not address the specific warning from [P3] that 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years,' particularly relevant given the client's possible future liquidity needs.
      - The report does not disclose derivative-specific risks documented in [P7] that 'derivative values rise and fall at a greater rate than equities and debt instruments. Losses can be greater than the initial investment.'
      - The report does not address the annuity purchase risk identified in [P14] that 'The amount of annuity you may eventually buy may be adversely affected if the investment returns are low when you purchase your annuity. Annuity rates may be more or less favourable if you choose to buy an annuity in the future.'
      - The report does not disclose the mismatch between agreed risk level 4 and existing volatility mapping to risk level 6, as documented in [P15].
    The report makes generic and standard product-specific risk disclosures covering no-guarantee-of-outperformance, market timing risk during transition, liquidity access timelines, capital-at-risk, and past performance disclaimers. These are internally consistent and fairly standard. However, the report delegates detailed product risk disclosure by stating 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' without verifying that such literature is actually present or accessible to the client. The case file contains product literature for CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc (the recommended fund under one scenario) but no specific literature for Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 appears on file. The report fails to disclose specific fund-level risks documented in the available literature: (1) derivative risk with losses potentially exceeding initial investment [P7]; (2) the five-year investment horizon recommendation [P3]; (3) emerging markets, foreign exchange, smaller companies and fixed interest-specific risks [P4]; (4) the future annuity rate volatility risk [P14]; (5) the noted mismatch between the client's agreed risk level of 4 and the existing portfolio's volatility-mapped risk level of 6 [P15]. The report's approach of incorporating generic capital-at-risk and no-guarantee language satisfies COBS 2.1.1R's general requirement but does not satisfy COBS 9.4.7R's requirement for clear, fair and prominent disclosure of 'material disadvantages of the recommendation, including any charges, fees and costs to the consumer.' The delegation to unverified literature, combined with the absence of fund-specific risk articulation in the report itself where material risks are documented in the file, represents a disclosure shortfall. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability drivers are recorded in the case file that would materially alter this assessment, though the client's stated anxiety about investment decisions (noted in [P15]) may suggest that generic rather than product-specific risk language is insufficient for adequate comprehension.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states multiple tax consequences: (1) Personal pension contributions benefit from 20% tax relief, with a net contribution of £80 becoming £100 gross; (2) Client-specific: £2,880 net contribution receives £720 tax relief from government; (3) If contributions exceed earnings in the tax year, a tax charge applies at the client's marginal rate; (4) Death benefits before age 75 are tax-free to nominees; death after age 75 are taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate; (5) ISA investment gains are tax-free with no Income Tax or Capital Gains Tax on stocks and shares ISA profits; (6) GIA allows use of annual CGT allowance; (7) Capital gains above £3,000 exemption taxed at 24% for basic rate taxpayers; (8) Dividends: first £500 tax-free, above that taxed at 8.75% for basic rate taxpayers; (9) Current IHT liability is £0; (10) From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions will be included in taxable estate for IHT; (11) All tax statements subject to change in law.
    File:     The evidence supports tax relief on pensions (pension illustration and research reports confirm 20% relief mechanism); ISA tax-free status is confirmed in Key Features document; death benefit taxation before/after age 75 is confirmed in both the report and Personal Pension Plan research (P2); GIA CGT allowance planning is referenced; pension projection illustrations (P14, P23) show accumulation projections but do not state tax consequences explicitly in the illustrations themselves; the pension research report (P2) confirms death benefit types (lump sum, drawdown) are available but does not detail tax treatment within the product literature extracted; Key Features documents (P5, P9) do not provide detailed tax consequence explanations beyond cost disclosures; the Aviva illustration (P11) includes a caveat that 'Your tax liability depends on your personal circumstances and may change in the future' but does not itemise tax consequences for this client's specific situation.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' in the tax implications section, but the earlier admission that 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year, as you retired and then only worked for a short period of time' creates internal inconsistency about whether the adequacy of earnings has been established or remains uncertain
      - The report asserts IHT liability is £0 and states the combined estate is 'well below the £1 million combined allowances available to married couples', but no evidence in the file establishes the spouse's asset value or combined estate position needed to verify this calculation
      - The report states dividends taxed at 8.75% for basic rate taxpayers and CGT at 24% for basic rate taxpayers, but the client's tax band has not been confirmed in the evidence (the client has retired and briefly worked part-time; current tax status is ambiguous per the extraction report)
      - The report recommends £3,600 gross pension contribution (£2,880 net plus £720 relief) but states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without showing the earnings figure or tax year employment income that justifies this—the extraction notes ambiguity about whether the client is currently earning £0 or recently stopped earning £890/month
    The report provides generic tax explanations (tax relief on pensions, ISA tax-free status, death benefit taxation, CGT/dividend rates) that are consistent with standard tax law and supported by pension and investment product literature in the evidence. However, the requirement distinguishes between generic legislative explanation and client-specific tax consequences. The report makes three client-specific tax assertions: (1) £2,880 net contribution receives £720 relief; (2) 'your current earnings are sufficient' for the contribution limit; (3) IHT liability is £0 based on combined estate position. For assertion (1), the arithmetic is correct under 20% relief but no evidence confirms the client's actual contribution amount or that it was made. For assertion (2), the report earlier admits uncertainty about earnings ('We are unsure of your earnings this tax year') then later asserts adequacy without resolving the ambiguity. The extraction report notes the client's employment income status is ambiguous—either earning £0 (unemployed) or recently ceased earning £890/month but may resume. No current earnings figure appears in the supporting evidence. This means the core justification for why the recommended contribution does not exceed the earnings limit cannot be verified. For assertion (3), the IHT calculation depends on combined spousal estate; the client's own assets are shown (£669,092 in P4) but no spouse asset total is provided, so the combined figure cannot be corroborated. The report correctly notes the April 2027 change to pension inclusion in IHT but provides this as generic information, not client-specific consequence. The guards note that tax consequences stated without the client's tax band established are unverifiable, not necessarily wrong. The CGT and dividend rates given are generic (not linked to this client's specific position). The report does disclose risks and limitations appropriately (contribution limit, tax charge risk, death benefit tax treatment), but fails to establish the factual premises (current earnings, spouse assets) needed to verify the client-specific assertions made. This is a Disclosure Shortfall: material information needed to support the tax consequence claims is absent from the evidence.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts one loss scenario: 'Stress test: immediate market crash with 30% loss in investments combined with increased inflation levels' with a lossPercentage of 30. The report states (page 21): 'I completed a stress test combining an immediate market crash with increased inflation levels. This showed that even if your investments sustained a loss of 30% with no recovery in 2028, there would still be no shortfall throughout your projected lifetime.' This is presented as a stress test demonstrating resilience to a severe shock.
    File:     The evidence pack contains a cashflow stress test document [P19] titled 'Current Situation- Stress Test' prepared by Kayleigh Sambhi on 16/12/2025, which models a scenario for Anthony Barnes. This document describes itself as showing 'the financial plan for your scenario Current Situation- Stress Test' based on 'forward-looking analysis of your assets, income and expenditure.' The document contains wealth tables [P18] showing projected liquid assets, pensions and total wealth across multiple years under stress conditions. The specific loss percentage applied in this stress test model is not explicitly stated in the retrieved passages, but the document confirms a stress test scenario was modelled and used to generate cashflow projections. The Aviva product literature [P1], [P4], [P6], [P7], [P8], [P10] contains generic risk warnings that 'the value of your pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in' and discusses various product-specific risks (foreign exchange, emerging markets, property, fixed interest, etc.), but does not present a specific illustrated loss scenario of 30%.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a specific 30% loss scenario with stated outcome of 'no shortfall throughout projected lifetime.' The supporting evidence file [P19] confirms a stress test was performed and modelled, but the passages retrieved do not disclose the specific loss percentage assumption (30%) applied in that stress test, nor do they disclose the specific outcome conclusion stated in the report. The stress test model exists but its parameters and conclusions are not quoted in the evidence pack.
      - The report states the stress test outcome as 'even if your investments sustained a loss of 30% with no recovery in 2028, there would still be no shortfall throughout your projected lifetime.' This specific quantified conclusion does not appear in the retrieved passages from the stress test document [P18], [P19].
    A comparison can be performed because the evidence pack contains both the report's assertion of the loss scenario and the supporting cashflow stress test document [P19] that was used to generate it. The guard permits recognition that 'a stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one in the risk section,' confirming the stress test qualifies as a loss scenario. The report asserts a specific 30% loss scenario with a deterministic outcome statement ('no shortfall throughout your projected lifetime'). The stress test document [P19] exists in the file and is titled 'Current Situation- Stress Test,' confirming that a stress test was indeed conducted and modelled. However, the passages retrieved from [P19] (wealth table [P18] showing projected assets 2058-2066) do not contain the specific loss percentage assumption (30%) or the outcome conclusion the report attributes to the stress test. The report's quotation at page 21 states the advisor completed the stress test, but the detailed parameters and conclusions of that test are not reproduced in the retrieved passages. The report does illustrate at least one loss scenario (the 30% stress test) and relates it to a client outcome (no projected lifetime shortfall). The presentation does not appear to be in an appendix only — the quote appears on page 21 of the suitability report, suggesting prominence in the main advice document. The risk disclosure in product literature [P1], [P4], [P6], [P7], [P8], [P10] is generic and does not contradict the illustrated scenario but neither corroborates the specific 30% assumption or outcome. The stress test is modelled and disclosed in principle, but the full substantiation of the parameters and outcome conclusion is not fully evident in the retrieved passages. This represents a situation where the report's claim about the stress test outcome is unsupported by quoted evidence from the stress test document itself, even though the stress test exists.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Report asserts a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' exists on page 12, addressing disadvantages and risks. The section is not confined to an appendix and appears prominently in the main body. The report also contains embedded risk warnings and disclaimers throughout, including statements about unit price volatility, no guarantee of capital return, pension switch guarantees, performance assumptions, alternative solutions analysis, and product-specific risks (bonds, cash, ISAs).
    File:     The suitability report [P1-P8] contains multiple risk and disadvantage disclosures: (1) A dedicated section 'Other Considerations of Switching' appears on page 12 discussing alternative solutions including stakeholder pensions, bonds, cash ISAs, and National Savings [P4]; (2) Risk warnings state 'unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it' [P3]; (3) Performance warnings: 'Past performance is no guarantee of future returns' appears multiple times [P3, P8]; (4) Pension switch caveat: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' [P3]; (5) Assumption sensitivity: 'the above charts are projections based on certain assumptions, and even a small variance between the assumptions used and actual experience can result in a marked difference in the outcome when compounded over a long period of time' [P4]; (6) Long-term care risk: 'the chart does not consider any potential long-term care costs which could occur in later life' [P4]; (7) Cancellation provision: 'the cancellation notice includes a provision that any previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back' [P5]; (8) Tax treatment extensively detailed including ISA tax-free status, GIA taxation, dividend and interest rules [P1-P2]; (9) Capacity for loss discussed with Medium-High rating and explanation of guaranteed income resilience [P6]; (10) Knowledge and experience section noting client's 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' [P6]; (11) Pension switch report [P9-P11] discloses 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P11]; (12) Charges comparison showing reduction in yield, critical yield, and effect of switching [P9-P11].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims to present material disadvantages and risks prominently, and this assertion is supported by evidence of multiple dedicated sections and embedded warnings throughout the main body rather than relegation to appendices. However, the internal extraction noted inconsistencies about expenditure figures (£2,493 vs £2,206 monthly) and charge presentations across sections. The pension switch report shows 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P11], which is peculiar—it suggests an absence of stated benefits for the fund being left, but does not explicitly state what is being given up. The extraction flagged that '/replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost' was not present in the document, meaning the report does not contain a dedicated articulation of features or guarantees lost through the switch.
      - comparisonPerformed
    The requirement examines whether material disadvantages, risks, tax consequences, loss scenarios and consumer harms are fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently explained and consistent with evidence, and whether material information identified elsewhere is adequately disclosed. Comparison can proceed because both the report's prominence assertion and the evidence pack are complete. The report does present a dedicated section 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 12 [P4], which is placed in the main body and not confined to an appendix, satisfying the prominence criterion on its face. The section discusses alternative solutions including why stakeholder pensions, bonds, and cash ISAs were rejected or not recommended, with specific reasoning provided for each. Risk warnings appear multiple times in the main text: volatility and capital loss warnings [P3], performance disclaimers [P3, P8], pension switch non-guarantees [P3], assumption sensitivity [P4], long-term care cost exclusion [P4], and cancellation notice provisions [P5]. Tax consequences are comprehensively addressed in dedicated sections covering ISA tax-free status, GIA taxation, dividend and interest allowances, and pension lifetime allowances [P1-P2]. Loss scenarios are illustrated through the cashflow analysis projections [P8] and market crash scenario [P14]. However, a material gap exists: the extraction noted that '/replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost' is absent from the document. The pension switch report [P11] states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products,' which is inadequate disclosure. The client is giving up a Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% portfolio for an Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II, yet the report does not explicitly state what features, guarantees, or protections (if any) were attached to the Vanguard funds that are being surrendered. The Vanguard LifeStrategy funds are named-brand products with known characteristics; the silence on what is being lost is a disclosure shortfall. Additionally, the report's discussion of 'advantages' of the new solution (simplicity, cost-efficiency, flexibility, managed by experts) appears in the main text [P3] without corresponding quantified disadvantages presented in equally visible form. The charge comparison shows a net increase of 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62% total ongoing charges) [P9-P12], but this cost increase and its long-term impact are not highlighted as prominently as the benefits narrative. The internal expenditure inconsistency (£2,493 vs £2,206 monthly) raises a question about the reliability of the cashflow projections on which loss scenarios depend, though this is an extraction ambiguity rather than evidence of misstatement in the report itself. The capacity for loss assessment of 'Medium-High' is supported by explanation of guaranteed income and resilience [P6], but the noted 'small period of time' shortfall when the spouse retires is not quantified in the main disadvantages section.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies that current funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity) have achieved higher historical performance (45.28% over 5 years) compared to recommended fund (30.12% over 5 years). The report states current funds have 'outperformed the recommended funds' but 'suffered much steeper losses'. The report notes the existing arrangement has 'Good platform with comprehensive facilities' and offers 'Full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available'. The report explicitly states there are 'None' for Safeguarded Features (TFC/GARs/MIG).
    File:     The Pension switch report [P10] shows existing pension is with Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited (Current Aviva) on 'None' platform, while new solution moves to 'Aviva Platform'. Plan literature [P13] confirms the existing arrangement offers '25% Tax free cash, 75% Taxable flexible drawdown available' and that 'We do offer full and partial encashment from age 55. We offer flexi-access drawdown.' The policy research [P2] confirms 'Guaranteed annuity rates (GAR)? No', 'Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)? No', 'Guaranteed growth rates? No'. Transaction history [P14-P20] shows the client held Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity funds continuously from 2018 onwards. The new solution details [P11] show the client will invest in different funds (not specified in retrieved passages as to exact names and performance comparisons).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts performance comparison (45.28% vs 30.12% over 5 years) but the specific performance figures for the recommended fund are not corroborated in the retrieved passages
      - The report identifies that current holdings do not match agreed risk profile, yet the specific features being given up regarding investment choice or fund manager quality are not detailed
      - The new solution fund details are not provided in the retrieved passages with sufficient specificity to verify what specific fund benefits are being lost
    The report acknowledges the key trade-off: higher historical performance of current funds versus steeper losses during downturns. This addresses a material disadvantage of switching. The report explicitly confirms no safeguarded benefits are being lost (no GAR, GMP, or guaranteed growth), which aligns with provider literature [P13] and policy research [P2]. The report identifies the platform will change from 'None' to 'Aviva Platform' which is disclosed but not elaborated as a feature trade-off. The retirement access options (flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS) appear to be retained in the new solution per [P13] which states Aviva Platform offers 'full and partial encashment from age 55. We offer flexi-access drawdown.' The critical missing element is whether the new solution provides equivalent fund choice, manager quality, or other product features compared to the existing 'good platform with comprehensive facilities' described at [P22]. The report does not adequately explain what specific product features or investment characteristics are being sacrificed beyond the performance differential, nor does it address whether the move from a 'good platform with comprehensive facilities' to Aviva Platform represents a feature loss.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states: (1) 'This small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' [P21]. (2) 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 2.94% per year up to age 60 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I believe this is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight.' [P21]. The report identifies a charge increase of +0.08% (£63 annually) on a like-for-like basis [P21]. When adviser fees are included, total costs rise from 0.54% to approximately 1.62% annually [P21]. Projected values at age 60 are identical at all three growth rates: Low (£74,000), Mid (£83,300), High (£93,400) [P21 references extracted data].
    File:     The pension switch report [P4] and [P10] show projections comparing existing and new solution at all three growth rates (Low, Mid, High). At Low growth (0%): both £74,000. At Mid growth (2.94%): existing £86,800 vs new £86,700 (difference £100, 0.1% decrease). At High growth (5.88%): existing £97,400 vs new £97,300 (difference £100, 0.1% decrease). The pension switch report notes 'Critical Yield' requirements: at mid growth 2.9% is required; at high growth 5.9% is required [P4, P10]. Research reports [P15, P16] show the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) has a 5-year cumulative return of 36.21% and 10-year return of 70.66%, compared to alternatives like CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C (31.28% and no 10-year data) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 40% (21.49% and 66.95%). The existing funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity) are documented in [P6] with weighted average charge of 0.22%. Charge data in [P3] shows recommended fund charge of 0.29% (CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc). The report in [P21] states the recommended fund strategy is '0.08% more expensive' on a like-for-like basis. No loss scenario or illustration of how the increased cost might impact retirement outcomes is provided in the main report body.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report projects identical values (£74k, £83.3k, £93.4k) across all three growth scenarios, but pension switch report [P4, P10] shows existing plan at mid-growth £86,800 vs new £86,700 (£100 decrease, 0.1%), and at high growth existing £97,400 vs new £97,300 (£100 decrease, 0.1%); the suitability report does not disclose these small projected shortfalls in the recommended plan.
      - Report justifies the 0.08% like-for-like charge increase solely by 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' but does not explain the material total cost increase from 0.54% to 1.62% when adviser fees are included, nor does it compare projected outcomes under the two cost structures.
      - Report states the 2.94% mid-growth rate is 'achievable' based on past performance and improved risk alignment, but does not connect this to whether that growth is sufficient to offset the higher costs; the pension switch report reveals a 2.9% critical yield is required, leaving minimal safety margin.
      - No loss scenario is presented showing how outcomes would differ under lower growth conditions or market stress, despite the charge increase creating a higher break-even requirement.
    The report asserts a cost increase is justified by 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' This is a client-specific benefit claim, not a generic statement. However, the justification is incomplete in three ways. First, the like-for-like charge increase (0.08%) is addressed, but the material total cost increase when adviser fees are included (from 0.54% to 1.62%, a 3x multiplier) is stated but never justified or explained to the client as to why that level of cost is acceptable given the projected outcomes. Second, the pension switch report [P4, P10] reveals projected outcomes at mid and high growth rates are marginally worse under the recommended plan (£100 decrease), which means the cost increase, while small in percentage terms, creates a harder hurdle to clear; the suitability report does not disclose this trade-off. Third, the report claims the 2.94% mid-growth rate is 'achievable' but the pension switch report's 'Critical Yield' table [P4, P10] shows 2.9% is required to match the existing plan's maturity value—a negligible margin that leaves little room for underperformance, yet the report does not highlight this risk or explain why it is acceptable. The guard permits a justification that repeats the benefit without connecting it to cost (e.g., 'alignment is good' without explaining cost/return trade-off), and that is present here; however, where projected outcomes are marginally worse and costs are materially higher in absolute terms, the absence of a loss scenario or explicit client acknowledgment of the break-even hurdle constitutes a disclosure shortfall. The report does address why the cost is justified—'improved risk alignment'—but does not clearly present the full trade-off: higher total costs, identical or marginally lower projections, and a tight critical yield. For a client in retirement or pre-retirement (age 56, retiring at 60), this is material information. The custody of 'disadvantages' in the canonical model shows only market timing risk during transition, not the ongoing cost/return trade-off.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses: (1) cancellation risk with the provision that any previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back [P1, page 20]; (2) access limitation that funds will usually take 10-15 working days to access [P1, page 12]; (3) that all other investment risks are detailed in product literature and should be read carefully [P1, page 12]; (4) no guarantee that switching will result in greater pension fund or that new funds will outperform existing ones [P1, page 16]; (5) no guarantee that returns on new investment will be greater than existing arrangement [P1, page 12].
    File:     The evidence contains: (1) Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet [P2, P6] listing specific risk warnings including General Risk (A), Foreign Exchange Risk (B), Emerging Markets (C), Fixed Interest (E), and Derivatives (F), with explicit statement that 'Investment is not guaranteed: The value of an investment is not guaranteed and can go down as well as up. You could get back less than you've paid in' [P6]; (2) CT Universal MAP Cautious C KIID [P4] stating fund is 'rated 4 because historically it has shown a medium level of volatility' and specifically disclosing Derivative Risk, Fixed Interest Securities Risk, Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, and Liquidity Risk with detailed explanations [P4]; (3) Pension switch analysis [P3, P5, P18] showing 'Effect of switching' produces £100 or 0.1% decrease in projected value at both mid and high growth rates [P3, P5, P18]; (4) Reduction in Yield comparison showing new solution at 0.7% versus existing at 0.6% on mid-growth [P3, P18]; (5) Critical Yield requirement of 2.9% (mid) and 5.9% (high) for new solution to match existing [P3, P18]; (6) Fund performance data [P16, P17] showing Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity funds with discrete annual returns; (7) Aviva Key Features [P10] disclosing tax consequences on death after age 75 where beneficiaries pay income tax at marginal rate or 45% if paid to trust; (8) Research reports [P7, P12] mapping agreed risk level 4 as 'Cautious Balanced' with moderate volatility expectation.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report does not disclose the specific named risk warnings (A-F) from the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet
      - Report does not disclose the specific risks detailed in CT Universal MAP Cautious C KIID including Derivative Risk, Fixed Interest Securities Risk, Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, and Liquidity Risk
      - Report does not explicitly quantify or disclose the £100 (0.1%) reduction in projected maturity value from switching shown in switch comparison tables
      - Report does not disclose the Critical Yield requirement (2.9% mid-growth, 5.9% high-growth) needed for new solution to match existing pension projections
      - Report does not disclose the Reduction in Yield increase (0.7% new versus 0.6% existing on mid-growth) indicating higher charges on new solution
      - Report does not disclose specific tax consequences on death after age 75 where beneficiary pays income tax at marginal rate or 45% tax charge if paid to trust, as detailed in Aviva Key Features
      - Report does not reference or discuss the risk that Emerging Markets exposure in either fund creates concentration or geopolitical risk despite both funds containing emerging market components
    The guard 'Generic market-risk wording in product literature does not need repeating' applies to general investment risks already stated generically in the report. However, the file contains material product-specific and quantified risks that exceed boilerplate disclosure. Specifically: (1) The Aviva and CT Universal factsheets contain named, enumerated risk warnings (A-F for Aviva; multiple specific risks for CT Universal) that are more detailed than the report's generic statement to 'read product literature carefully'. (2) The switch analysis tables [P3, P5, P18] quantify that the new solution will deliver £100 (0.1%) less at maturity across growth scenarios—a material disadvantage of the recommendation that is disclosed in supporting analysis but not highlighted in the report's risk section. (3) The Critical Yield figures (2.9%, 5.9%) establish a hurdle rate the new funds must clear to break even, information material to understanding downside risk but absent from the report. (4) The Reduction in Yield (0.7% new vs 0.6% existing) represents a measurable increase in the drag from charges on the new solution, a cost disadvantage not flagged in risk disclosure. (5) The Aviva Key Features [P10] disclose tax consequences on death after 75, including marginal rate taxation and potential 45% charges—consequences not mentioned in the report despite the recommendation involving a pension transfer. The guard 'A research finding that undercuts the recommendation is material information the report withheld' applies to the switch comparison tables showing modest adverse effect. While the report states 'no guarantee' of outperformance, it does not disclose that the switch analysis actually projects a £100 shortfall. The report's dismissal of risks to 'product literature provided' satisfies COBS 9.4.7R disclosure but does not fully comply with COBS 4.2.1R and PRIN 2A.5 requirement for fair, clear and not misleading information when material disadvantages and specific quantified risks are embedded in the file but not integrated into the report's own risk narrative or prominently repeated.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 4 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The suitability report asserts a registered Power of Attorney covering financial affairs and health and welfare, but no corroborating document (Power of Attorney deed, registration certificate, or attorney confirmation) appears in the case file. The report records the LPA as present (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true) but the extraction shows thirdPartySupport isPresent=false, an internal inconsistency in how the report's own data models the same fact. The client's knowledge deficits (no understanding of stocks and shares, very little understanding of pensions) recorded in the Risk Profile are not reflected in any discussion of comprehension support or adjustments needed in the suitability report. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts at page 27 under 'Power of attorney': 'You confirm you have a registered Power of Attorney covering your financial affairs and health and welfare.' The extracted model records this as thirdPartySupport present=false with authorityType=LPA and states isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true for both clients in the parties/clients section. The report does not extract support needs, communication adjustments, or vulnerability characteristics that would require support arrangements. No third-party relationship is documented in the extraction.
    File:     The Fact Find (P20, P24) records client details including spouse Yvette McGealy (age 58, relationship Spouse, long-term, financially dependent status blank). Communication preference is recorded as 'No Preference' with no accessible format requirement (P7). The Risk Profile report (P1, P8) shows the client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement. Capacity for Loss questionnaire notes record 'No additional notes were recorded' (P8). No explicit vulnerability indicators, support needs, or third-party support arrangements are documented anywhere in the file. The Client Agreement (P23) states 'Should you require us to make a reasonable adjustment in providing the advisory services to you, please speak to your financial adviser' and references communication methods (telephone, video, letters, email, portals, newsletters) without indicating any adjustments were needed or made. No evidence of a registered Power of Attorney is present in any document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report asserts a registered Power of Attorney covering financial affairs and health and welfare, but no corroborating document (Power of Attorney deed, registration certificate, or attorney confirmation) appears in the case file.
      - The report records the LPA as present (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true) but the extraction shows thirdPartySupport isPresent=false, an internal inconsistency in how the report's own data models the same fact.
      - The client's knowledge deficits (no understanding of stocks and shares, very little understanding of pensions) recorded in the Risk Profile are not reflected in any discussion of comprehension support or adjustments needed in the suitability report.
    The requirement tests whether information the report includes about client vulnerabilities, support needs, communication preferences, capacity and comprehension is fully supported by evidence. The report asserts a Power of Attorney but provides no supporting document—no deed, no registration certificate, no contemporaneous confirmation from an attorney or the client. The assertion appears only as a statement attributed to the client ('You confirm you have...') in the report itself, which is the assertion side, not evidence. A [I] passage cannot corroborate itself. The file contains no [A], [B] or [D] evidence that such an LPA exists. Where the report claims a formal legal arrangement (a registered Power of Attorney), the absence of any document evidencing it, either from the Land Registry (for property LPA), the Court of Protection, or the attorney themselves, is material. The case file does not record vulnerability indicators beyond the risk profile questionnaire responses showing low investment knowledge and comprehension. The Fact Find captures spouse information and communication preferences but records no special communication needs, accessible formats requested, or support arrangements. The report does not discuss these knowledge deficits or propose any compensating adjustments to communication or decision-making support. The Internal Inconsistency noted in the extraction metadata—thirdPartySupport.isPresent=false alongside isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true—suggests the report itself does not treat the LPA as an active support arrangement. The Client Agreement's generic reference to reasonable adjustments is not evidence of any adjustment being considered, discussed or implemented for this client. No evidence of a vulnerability assessment, identification of support needs, or tailored support arrangements appears anywhere in the file. The absence of a comprehensive vulnerability and support needs assessment in a case where the client shows low investment knowledge and the report relies on complex financial modelling (cashflow analysis, multiple asset classes, tax wrappers, pensions legislation) creates a process risk: the client's comprehension and informed decision-making may not have been adequately verified.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report contains no explicit statement about adjustments made to support client comprehension.     The comparison can be made because the file contains sufficient evidence to establish what adjustments (if any) were recorded. The trigger `hasVulnerabilityIndicators` is false, but the instruction states the check runs anyway and applies to every case—the question is whether vulnerability was ever considered. The evidence shows: (1) The adviser identified a material characteristic: the client described having 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and limited ability to understand financial matters. This is a capability driver under FCA Guidance 21/1. (2) Accessibility and format preferences were formally assessed in the Fact Find [P2], indicating the adviser went through a standard assessment protocol. The client selected 'No Requirement' for accessible format and 'No Preference' for delivery method. (3) However, no document records what specific adjustments were made in response to the identified knowledge gap. (5) The ATRQ (Attitude to Risk Questionnaire) incompleteness is relevant: the client did not complete it during the meeting and was sent an email version to complete independently. There is no evidence in the suitability report that the client actually completed it or that the adviser followed up. (6) The guard (standard cancellation rights are regulatory, not an adjustment) does not apply here: no cancellation rights are being cited. (7) Internal consistency: The report acknowledges very limited knowledge but does not articulate any compensating adjustments to support informed decision-making. The absence of adjustments is not the same as adjustments being made transparently—the requirement asks whether adjustments are recorded where characteristics exist, not whether the firm chose to make none. Where a capability gap is identified and no adjustments are documented, the claim that the recommendation is suitable is not fully supported by the evidence of comprehension support.
